## WHAT THIS EDITION IS, AND WHAT IT REFUSES TO DO

This is a Mains-focused edition, not a second comprehensive package. It teaches the ocean as a
**physical system whose quantities are all conventions in disguise** — an ocean's area depends on
where you put its southern edge, an SST anomaly depends on which thirty years you call normal, a
salinity value depends on whether the sea is open or enclosed, and a climate index depends on a
published threshold. That is the discipline the three owned syllabus clauses actually reward.

Four things this edition does that a conventional treatment does not.

1. **It teaches conflicts rather than smoothing them.** Where two books disagree on a current's
   sign, a trench's depth or the number of oceans, **both sides appear with their page locations**
   and neither is adopted. Where a book contradicts itself, both pages are shown and neither is
   asserted. Eight inter-source conflicts and six intra-source self-contradictions are carried
   through the edition on purpose. Each is a discriminating fact an examiner can test.
2. **It dates every volatile number.** Book-era quantities carry an author and a folio. Live
   quantities carry a document date. Nothing quantitative appears bare.
3. **It routes aggressively.** Fourteen other owners hold half of what looks like this topic's
   material. Every route-out is stated by exact file path in the lesson where the temptation
   arises. A Geography answer that argues fisheries policy, cyclogenesis or sea-level-as-climate
   has left its own paper.
4. **It never answers an objective question.** Five of the seven owned demands are Prelims demands.
   They appear as a **demand statement and a capability statement**. There is no option letter, no
   key and no elimination chain anywhere in this file. The two owned Mains demands are subjective
   and therefore do receive full models.

**The structural fact that shapes everything below.** Topic 12 is the first topic in this lane whose
analytical core has **no local book support at all**. Across the four local books, the words
*upwelling*, *Ekman*, *Walker*, *Indian Ocean Dipole*, *Madden-Julian*, *thermohaline*, *AMOC*,
*marine heatwave*, *acidification*, *oxygen minimum* and *dead zone* return **zero** occurrences in
the oceanography chapters. Leong explains the Peruvian fishery through guano and sea-birds without
ever naming upwelling. The only ENSO section in any of the four books belongs to Topic 16. The
mechanisms the answer-worthiness audit grades at 15 to 20 marks of answer value therefore rest on
**C1's own ⚠️ standard-knowledge tier plus dated official sources**, and this edition says so in
every lesson where it is true rather than manufacturing a citation.

## READING CONVENTIONS — FROZEN

| Mark | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ✅ | Taken from a named local book, with its printed folio or scan page |
| 📄 | Taken from a named official document, with its document date |
| ⚠️ | Standard physical geography carried by the owner's own ⚠️ tier; no book or live citation exists |
| ✗ | A source defect, quoted so that it can be recognised, never asserted |
| ⇄ | A live conflict between two sources, both of which are shown |
| ↪ | A route-out to the owning file, by exact path |

**Citation conventions, frozen.**

- Leong, *Certificate Physical and Human Geography*: `Leong printed 87 (scan PDF p.90)`. Where the
  folio is cropped out of the scan, **PDF page only**: `Leong scan PDF p.93`. The measured offset
  `printed = PDF − 3` is reported as evidence of chapter extent and is **never used to mint an
  unseen folio** (U-10).
- Majid Husain, *Indian and World Geography*: `Majid IWG printed 4.7 (scan PDF p.135)`. Pagination
  is **chapter-relative**: "4.13" is a chapter-4 folio, not page 413. Where no folio appears in the
  text layer, **PDF page only** (U-11).
- Khullar, *India: A Comprehensive Geography*: `Khullar printed folio 21 (scan PDF p.27)`. The
  folio offset drifts across this book; **no Khullar folio is computed anywhere** (U-12).
- Units are **SI-first with the book unit in parentheses**, because Leong's chapter is entirely
  imperial and Majid's entirely metric. Salinity is written **‰ or ppt and never "%"**, even where
  the source prints a per-cent sign (N-24).

## THE TWENTY LESSONS — FROZEN SEQUENCE AND MANDATORY INDIA HOOK

There is **one** sequence. The world half is not a block that ends before India begins; India enters
as the worked case of each process from Lesson 1 onward, and **every lesson without exception
carries an India hook**.

| # | Lesson | Stage | Core question | India hook |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The ocean as a planetary system | Foundation | Why is an ocean's area an answer to a question, not a fact? | The only ocean named after a country, whose southern edge is four different lines |
| 2 | Ocean relief: shelf, slope and deep | Foundation | Why do the richest fishing and the richest drilling share 5 per cent of the planet? | Kachchh Shelf, Gulf of Khambhat and Mumbai High on one platform |
| 3 | What falls to the bottom | Foundation | Why does the sediment under a ship tell you where you are? | Why the Bay of Bengal floor is terrigenous and the central basin is not |
| 4 | The temperature structure of the ocean | Foundation | Why is the ocean's annual range smaller than any land surface's? | Why the Arabian Sea and the Bay, at one latitude, do not behave alike |
| 5 | What salinity is, and what it is made of | Foundation | Why is sea water 3.5 per cent salt, and why does that barely change? | Why Indian bulletins write ‰ and not per cent |
| 6 | Why the saltiest sea is not the hottest | Foundation | Why is the equator a minimum and 25°N a maximum? | Arabian Sea against the Bay, and the Red Sea beyond both |
| 7 | Density, water masses and the layered ocean | Foundation | What is the difference between water that moves and water that *is*? | The Bay of Bengal's freshwater cap and what it does to mixing |
| 8 | The forces that move the ocean | Core | Why do three authorities give three different lists? | Why India's own ocean is the best evidence that wind comes first |
| 9 | The Atlantic circuit | Core | Why can two coasts at one latitude have opposite climates? | Why India's west coast is wet where Namibia's and Peru's are desert |
| 10 | The Pacific and Southern circuits | Core | Why is one named current warm in one book and cold in another? | Why India has no cold eastern-boundary current and no coastal desert |
| 11 | Upwelling | Core | Why does wind *along* a coast matter more than wind *onto* it? | Somali and south-west-coast cells, and why Indian productivity is seasonal |
| 12 | Where the fish are | Core | Why does naming upwelling answer only a third of the question? | Shelf fisheries against a seasonal upwelling fishery in one country |
| 13 | The ocean's slow loop | Core | How do you describe a return limb you cannot see? | Why the Indian monsoon response to an AMOC change is routed, not asserted |
| 14 | Tides: the geometry and the arithmetic | Core | Why is a spring tide nothing to do with spring? | Why Khambhat and Kachchh dominate every Indian tidal list |
| 15 | Tides as a resource and a constraint | Core | Why does a port's timetable belong to the Moon? | The Hooghly, Kolkata, and the 3.75 MW plant sanctioned in **Feb 2008** and never built |
| 16 | The Indian Ocean as a basin | Advanced | Why does India hold two contracts on a seabed no country owns? | The whole lesson |
| 17 | The ocean that changes direction twice a year | Advanced | Why does the North Indian Ocean have no fixed gyre? | The whole lesson |
| 18 | ENSO and the Walker circulation | Advanced | Why must an answer describe the neutral state first? | Why "El Niño causes drought in India" is this topic's most expensive sentence |
| 19 | The IOD and the family of modes | Advanced | Why can an ocean that is warm everywhere be IOD-neutral? | The whole lesson |
| 20 | Measuring the ocean, and writing the answer | Advanced | Why does an anomaly need a base period before it means anything? | The whole lesson |

**Stage allocation.** Lessons 1-7 Foundation · Lessons 8-15 Core · Lessons 16-20 Advanced.

**Twenty lessons, twenty distinct primary visual grammars.** Of the twenty primaries, **two** are
matrices or tables; the remaining eighteen are maps, sections, curves, loops, wheels, ladders,
trees, spirals, vector rosettes, panels, strips, stacks and spines. A grammar may recur as a
*secondary* visual and never as a second primary.

### The intuition, before any term

Ask how big the Indian Ocean is and you will be given a number. The number is not wrong. It is an
**answer to a question you were not told was being asked** — namely, where does the Indian Ocean
stop in the south? Move that line and the number moves with it. There is no edge painted on the
water. Every ocean area in every textbook is a convention plus arithmetic, and the convention is
the part that is argued over.

This is not a quibble. It is the discriminating idea of the whole topic, and it recurs in Lesson 5
(what counts as a salt), Lesson 19 (what counts as a positive index) and Lesson 20 (what counts as
normal). Learn it here and the rest of the edition is a variation on it.

**Caption.** One ocean name, four live boundary conventions, four different areas. The panel is the
whole lesson: the quantity is downstream of the convention, and the convention is contested by
named states on the record.

### What the IHO proposal actually says — 📄 first-party, read in full

The International Hydrographic Organization's 3rd Assembly carried **Proposal PRO-3.3, "Recognition
of the Southern Ocean"**, submitted by the Secretary-General as Chair of the Hydrographic Commission
on Antarctica. Four of its statements are load-bearing.

1. 📄 The Southern Ocean "**became an official ocean in 1937, but lost its official status in
   1953**". An ocean can therefore be **de-recognised**. This single sentence disposes of the idea
   that the inventory of oceans is a natural fact.
2. 📄 The proposed Resolution would have the IHO "recognize the Southern Ocean as the global sea
   area surrounding the continent of Antarctica", with "**the northern geographic limit of the
   Southern Ocean … defined by the parallel of Latitude 60°S**".
3. 📄 The consequence is stated explicitly: "the southern geographic limits of the Atlantic, Indian,
   and Pacific Oceans are identical with the northern geographic limit of the Southern Ocean. **This
   joint geographic limit supersedes those mentioned in Publication S-23, Ed. 3, 1953** for the
   areas concerned." Recognising one ocean redraws three others.
4. 📄 Appendix 1 records the national positions. **Argentina** "considers that there are no technical
   grounds to support a separate reference for the area referred as 'Southern Ocean'". The **United
   Kingdom** records that its S-23 comment — that the UK uses **55°S** — "does not apply anymore".
   **Chile** and **Ecuador** accept 60°S. The **United States** supports, "without prejudice".
   **CCAMLR** uses the **Antarctic Convergence** instead, "despite the seasonal and annual changes
   of its latitude".

⚠️ **U-01, flagged and not resolved.** This is a **proposal**. Whether Assembly-3 adopted it was
**not retrieved during this pass**. Every sentence in this edition that uses CA-8 carries the word
*proposal*. **It is not stated anywhere that the IHO has recognised the Southern Ocean.**

### ⇄ Conflict 1 of 8 — how many oceans are there?

| Source | Position | Exact location |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Majid IWG | "There is only one world ocean, but the continents divide the ocean into **five** distinct parts … (v) **Southern Ocean (Antarctic Ocean)**" | scan PDF p.129 (no folio in text layer, U-11) |
| ✅ Khullar | "**The International Hydrographic Organisation has recognised the coast of Antarctica as the southern extreme of the Indian Ocean**" — which leaves no room for a fifth ocean | printed folio 21 (scan PDF p.27) |
| 📄 IHO PRO-3.3 | Official in 1937, de-recognised in 1953, and **proposed** for re-recognition at a 60°S limit | Assembly-3 proposal, with Annex A and Appendix 1 |
| 📄 Ministry of Earth Sciences | Operational Indian practice already treats it as a basin: a Directional Wave Rider Buoy "deployed off Mauritius to improve the prediction of **Southern Ocean swells** affecting the north Indian coastal waters" | RS Unstarred Q.1324, answered 30/07/2026; LS Unstarred Q.1752, answered 29 July 2026 |

⚠️ **The resolution is that there is no resolution, and that is the teachable point.** The Indian
government uses the Southern Ocean as a working basin in a parliamentary answer of July 2026 while
the international instrument that would formalise it remains a proposal of unretrieved disposition.
**Teach the inventory as convention-dependent and dated** (N-13). An answer that writes "there are
five oceans" without a date and an authority has asserted a convention as a fact.

### ✗ Two source defects on this page, recorded and not reproduced

**✗ N-14 — Khullar, printed folio 21: "Tropic of Cancer forms its northernmost limit."** The Indian
Ocean's northern margin is the **Asian coast**, and the head of the Persian Gulf reaches beyond
23.5°N. The claim is recorded as a defect and is not reproduced. The correct statement is that the
basin is **closed in the north by land** — which is precisely the geometry Lesson 17 needs to
explain why the North Indian Ocean cannot hold a closed gyre.

**✗ N-15 — Majid IWG, scan PDF p.129, two claims inside one paragraph.** "The Earth's oceans and
seas cover more than **367 million sq km**", alongside "Approximately **70.8%** of the Earth is
covered with water and 29.2 per cent is land area." The two cannot both hold:

```text
   ARITHMETIC AUDIT — Majid IWG, scan PDF p.129
   Earth's surface area, standard value           ≈ 510 million km²
   70.8 % of 510 million                          ≈ 361 million km²   ← implied by the percentage
   367 million km² as a share of 510 million      ≈  71.9 %           ← implied by the area
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   DIVERGENCE INSIDE ONE PARAGRAPH                ≈   6 million km²
                                                  ≈ 1.1 percentage points

   CONTRAST — Leong printed 84 (scan PDF p.87)
   "more than 70 per cent or 140 million square miles"
   140 million sq miles ≈ 362.6 million km² ≈ 71.1 % of 510 million km²
   → internally consistent. One book closes, the other does not.
```

**Do not average the two.** Show the divergence and name the page. An examiner who has set a
statement question on ocean coverage is testing whether a candidate quotes a number or audits one.

### The water-store ordering — the owned 2021 Prelims Q62 demand's substance

✅ Majid IWG, scan PDF p.129: "**About 98% of the water is in the ocean; 2% is in streams, lakes,
ground-water, and glaciers.**"

**✗ N-36.** This is the **only** coverage of the owned demand anywhere in the evidence base, and it
fails the demand. A two-way split suppresses exactly what an ordering question tests: **within the
non-ocean fraction, ice comes first, groundwater second, and surface water — rivers and lakes —
last and by a very long way.** The demand asks a candidate to rank rivers, groundwater and polar
ice. Majid's 98/2 split cannot rank them because it puts all three inside one undifferentiated 2
per cent.

⚠️ **What the edition therefore teaches is the ordering and not a number.**

```text
   GLOBAL WATER STORES — ORDERING ONLY, NO SHARES ASSERTED
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  1. OCEAN AND SEA — overwhelmingly the largest store, saline │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       │  the rest is FRESH WATER, a small remainder:
       ├── 2. GLACIERS AND ICE SHEETS — the largest FRESH store,
       │      and overwhelmingly polar. Mostly NOT liquid, NOT
       │      accessible, and NOT part of the usable resource.
       ├── 3. GROUNDWATER — the largest LIQUID fresh store, and
       │      far larger than all rivers and lakes combined.
       └── 4. SURFACE FRESH WATER — lakes, then soil moisture,
              then the atmosphere, then RIVERS. Rivers are among
              the SMALLEST stores and among the FASTEST-turning.

   THE DISCRIMINATION THE DEMAND TESTS
   Largest store ≠ most used store ≠ fastest-replaced store.
   A river carries very little water at any instant and replaces
   it very quickly; an ice sheet holds a great deal and replaces
   it over millennia. Rank by STOCK and the order is ice →
   groundwater → rivers. Rank by FLOW and it inverts.

   📘 Attribution: the 98 / 2 split is Majid IWG's, scan PDF p.129.
      The internal ordering above is ⚠️ standard physical geography;
      no local book states it and no primary source was retrieved.
```

↪ **Route-out.** Polar ice as a **planetary object** — the cryosphere, ice-cap change, the Earth's
rotation and axis — belongs to `Geography/basic/01_The-Earth-and-the-Universe`. Topic 12 owns it
only as a **water store** in the ranking above. ↪ Freshwater resource stress and access belong to
`Geography/advanced/36` and `Geography/basic/36_Contemporary-Geographical-Issues-India`.

### The Indian Ocean's measured facts, as the books give them — ✅ attributed and dated

✅ Khullar, printed folio 21 (scan PDF p.27):

- "With an estimated area of about **73,442,700 sq km** the Indian ocean is the **third largest
  ocean** in the world after the Pacific Ocean and the Atlantic Ocean."
- "**This is the only ocean in the world to be named after a country**, i.e., Indian Ocean."
- "It is bounded by South Asia in the north, Indonesian islands and Australia in the east and by
  Africa in the west. **The meridian of Cape of Tasmania (147°E) forms the boundary line between the
  Indian and the Pacific Oceans while the meridian of Cape Town (18° 22'E) forms the dividing line
  between the Atlantic Ocean and the Indian Ocean.**"
- "This ocean is encircled by **46 countries** … **27 littoral countries (including Australia, 7
  island countries) and 12 landlocked countries** as recognised by the U.N."

**The littoral arithmetic closes exactly: 27 + 7 + 12 = 46.** That is worth saying, because so
little else in this topic's book layer closes. Note what the closure means: Khullar's 27 littoral
states **include** the 7 island states, and the 12 landlocked states are counted as part of the
Indian Ocean's political rim because they depend on it for access.

⚠️ **The seven-significant-figure area is the lesson's own warning.** 73,442,700 sq km is stated to
the nearest hundred square kilometres for a basin whose southern edge is four different lines. It is
reproduced here **only as Khullar's figure, attributed, dated to the book, and tied to convention
[A]** — and **U-02** records that no official area under convention [B] was retrieved, so **no
difference may be computed**.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why the naming point is a geography point and not a trivia point

Khullar's "only ocean named after a country" is usually taught as a fact to remember. It is better
taught as a consequence. The Indian Ocean is the only major basin that is **closed in the north by a
continent**, and the landmass that closes it is the Indian subcontinent projecting 1,600 km into it.
Three structural consequences follow, and every one of them is examined later in this edition:

| Consequence of northern closure | Where this edition develops it |
|---|---|
| No northern limb to a gyre, so the basin's circulation must be **driven and reversed by wind** rather than closed by rotation | Lesson 17 |
| The basin is divided by the peninsula into **two sub-basins with opposite freshwater budgets** — an evaporative Arabian Sea and a river-fed Bay of Bengal | Lessons 6, 7 and 17 |
| The basin has **limited outlets**, all of them narrow, which is why Khullar's geopolitical section exists at all | Lesson 16 |

The name records the geography; the geography is the closure; the closure is the mechanism.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "This is pedantry. Every atlas gives an Indian Ocean area; candidates should learn
one and move on."

**Reply.** The objection would hold if the conventions differed trivially. They do not. The band
between 60°S and the Antarctic coast is a very large sector of ocean, and whether it is counted
changes the Indian Ocean's area, its mean depth, its sea-ice cover and its share of the global
ocean. More decisively, the geophysical-phenomena clause that this topic owns asks about **changes
in critical geographical features including water-bodies**. You cannot report a change in a feature
whose boundary is unstated — the change and the redefinition are indistinguishable. This is the
same logic that governs Topic 10's coastline series and Topic 11's reef-area series.

**Unresolved residual.** Whether PRO-3.3 was adopted (**U-01**) and what the Indian Ocean's area is
under a 60°S limit (**U-02**) were both unretrieved this pass. The honest position is a **named gap**,
not a substituted figure.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing "the IHO has recognised the Southern Ocean."** It is a **proposal** whose disposition
  was not retrieved. Write *proposed*.
- ❌ **Quoting 73,442,700 sq km bare.** It is Khullar's, book-era, and an answer to one convention of
  four.
- ❌ **Reproducing "Tropic of Cancer forms its northernmost limit."** The northern margin is the
  Asian coast (N-14).
- ❌ **Averaging 367 million km² and 70.8 per cent.** They are inconsistent inside one paragraph;
  showing that is the answer (N-15).
- ❌ **Answering a water-store ranking question with "98 per cent ocean, 2 per cent the rest."** The
  ranking lives entirely inside the 2 per cent (N-36).
- ❌ **Treating the ocean count as settled.** An ocean was de-recognised in 1953.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson serves the **salient-features** clause (the planetary water system as a
feature of world physical geography) and, decisively, the **geophysical-phenomena** clause's limb on
"changes in critical geographical features (including water-bodies and ice-caps)" — because it
establishes that a change in a water body can only be measured against a declared boundary.

**Owned demand served.** **O6 — 2021 Prelims GS-I Q62, "Global water distribution rivers groundwater
polar ice."** The capability tested is the **ordering of the world's water stores**. C1 has no
section on this at all; Majid's 98/2 split is the only book coverage and it is defective. **The key
is not available locally and none is inferred, implied or reconstructed here.**

**Answer use.** In any question about an ocean's extent, change or share, open with the convention
and its authority before the number. Two sentences buy the whole discrimination: *"Ocean areas are
convention-dependent: the IHO's Assembly-3 Proposal PRO-3.3 would place the Southern Ocean's
northern limit at 60°S, superseding S-23 Ed.3, 1953, while Argentina objects and CCAMLR uses the
moving Antarctic Convergence. Khullar's 73,442,700 sq km answers the Antarctic-coast convention."*

### Revision points

1. An ocean's area is an answer to a boundary convention, not an independent fact.
2. Four live southern-limit conventions: **60°S (proposed), 55°S (UK, withdrawn), the Antarctic
   coast (Khullar's statement of the IHO position), the Antarctic Convergence (CCAMLR, moving)**.
3. IHO PRO-3.3: the Southern Ocean was **official in 1937 and lost that status in 1953**.
4. The proposed 60°S limit would **supersede S-23 Ed.3, 1953** for the southern limits of the
   Atlantic, Indian and Pacific Oceans simultaneously.
5. Argentina objects on technical grounds; the UK withdraws 55°S; Chile and Ecuador accept 60°S;
   the USA supports without prejudice.
6. **U-01:** the proposal's adoption was not retrieved. Never write that the IHO *has* recognised it.
7. Majid IWG lists **five** oceans including the Southern; Khullar's formulation implies **four**.
8. MoES, July 2026, uses "Southern Ocean swells" as settled operational language.
9. Khullar: Indian Ocean **73,442,700 sq km**, third largest, **the only ocean named after a
   country**, bounded by **147°E** (Cape of Tasmania) and **18°22′E** (Cape Town).
10. Littoral arithmetic closes: **27 littoral (incl. 7 island) + 12 landlocked = 46**.
11. **✗ N-14:** "Tropic of Cancer forms its northernmost limit" is a defect; the margin is the Asian
    coast.
12. **✗ N-15:** 367 million km² and 70.8 per cent diverge by about **6 million km²** in one
    paragraph; Leong's "140 million square miles" (~71.1 per cent) is internally consistent.
13. **✗ N-36:** the 98/2 water split cannot rank rivers, groundwater and ice; the ordering is
    **ice → groundwater → surface water**, with rivers among the smallest stores.
14. Rank by **stock** and ice leads; rank by **flow** and rivers lead. The demand usually tests stock.

### Concept check

**Question:** A statement reads: "The Indian Ocean has an area of 73,442,700 sq km and is bounded in
the south by Antarctica." Identify precisely what is assumed rather than stated, and say what a
Mains-standard sentence would add.

**Model answer:** The statement assumes a **boundary convention** and presents its output as a
measurement. Khullar's figure at printed folio 21 is computed under the convention that the Indian
Ocean runs to the **Antarctic coast** — the largest of four live alternatives. The IHO's Assembly-3
Proposal PRO-3.3 would instead fix the Southern Ocean's northern limit at **60°S**, expressly
superseding **S-23 Ed.3, 1953**, which would make the Indian Ocean smaller; the United Kingdom's
withdrawn comment used **55°S**; and CCAMLR uses the **Antarctic Convergence**, a front that moves
seasonally and annually, so under it the basin has no fixed area at all. A Mains sentence therefore
names the convention and its authority before the number, dates the figure to its source, and —
because no official area under a 60°S limit was retrieved — refuses to state a second figure or to
compute a difference. It would also note that the proposal's adoption status is unverified, so the
Southern Ocean may be described as *proposed* for recognition and not as recognised.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the number of oceans and their limits are physical facts
established once. The Southern Ocean was an official ocean in 1937 and lost that status in 1953; its
re-recognition is a live proposal with recorded national objections. Inventories of oceans are
instruments, and instruments have dates.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 1

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "An ocean's area is a decision before it is a
measurement." Examine this statement with reference to the southern limit of the Indian Ocean.

**Model:** Ocean areas are computed inside declared limits, so the limit decides the number.
The Indian Ocean's southern edge is governed by four live conventions. The International
Hydrographic Organization's Assembly-3 **Proposal** PRO-3.3 would recognise the Southern Ocean with
a northern limit at **60°S**, expressly superseding Publication **S-23, Ed. 3, 1953** for the
southern limits of the Atlantic, Indian and Pacific Oceans together. The United Kingdom's earlier
**55°S** comment is withdrawn; Chile and Ecuador accept 60°S; **Argentina records that there are no
technical grounds** for a separate Southern Ocean; and CCAMLR uses the **Antarctic Convergence**, a
front that shifts seasonally. Khullar's **73,442,700 sq km** (printed folio 21) answers the
Antarctic-coast convention alone. Since the proposal's adoption was not verified and no official
area under a 60°S limit was retrieved, the defensible position is to name the convention, date the
figure and decline a second one. The decision precedes the measurement. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Ocean inventory stated **4** · limit conventions named with authorities **3**
· Indian Ocean naming point **2** · date and attribution discipline **1**

Progress: 2 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **relief traverse with a hypsographic sidebar**

### The intuition, before any term

Walk into the sea from an Indian beach and for a long way the water gets deeper very slowly. Then,
quite suddenly, it does not: the floor tips away and the depth multiplies. That break is the single
most economically consequential line on the planet's surface. **Landward of it sits roughly 5 per
cent of the Earth, and on that 5 per cent sit the world's great fishing grounds, almost all offshore
oil and gas, almost all placer minerals and almost all the ports that need a tide to work.** Seaward
of it is two-thirds of the ocean floor and almost nothing that anyone extracts.

Lesson 2 is therefore not a list of features. It is the explanation of why one narrow fringe does
all the economic work, and why the deep ocean — vastly larger — does almost none.

**Caption.** One traverse, both books' numbers side by side, with the two conflicts marked where
they occur rather than hidden. The sidebar converts the same information into a vertical
distribution: land averages **+840 m**, ocean averages **−3790 m**, and the extremes are the two
numbers everyone quotes.

### Four conversions that close, and why that matters

✅ Leong's chapter is entirely imperial, and four of his conversions are exact:

| Leong's statement | Conversion | Closes? |
|---|---|---|
| Shelf edge at "the **100 fathom (600 feet)** isobath" | 100 × 6 ft = 600 ft | ✓ exactly |
| Deeps "to a depth of **5,000 fathoms or 30,000 feet**" | 5,000 × 6 = 30,000 | ✓ exactly |
| Water "uniformly cold" below "**2,000 fathoms (12,000 feet)**" | 2,000 × 6 = 12,000 | ✓ exactly |
| Mariana "more than **36,000 feet**"; Everest "only **29,028 feet**" | 36,000 > 29,028 | ✓ the comparison holds |

✅ Majid's shelf-and-slope arithmetic also closes: **11.4 − 6.4 = 5.0**, matching his separately
stated "approximately 5 per cent of the Earth's surface" for the shelf. ✅ And his vertical volume
split closes exactly at **2 + 18 + 80 = 100** (Lesson 4).

⚠️ **Why audit arithmetic at all?** Because it tells you which parts of a book are transcribed and
which are reasoned. Leong's conversions and Majid's percentage splits close; Majid's ocean-area
paragraph (N-15) and his salinity composition table (N-16) do not. The same book is reliable in one
place and not in another, and the only way to know which is to check.

### ⇄ Conflict 2 of 8 — the Japan Trench is 2,000 metres deep in the gap between two books

| Source | Statement | Metric equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Majid IWG printed 4.3 | "Ramapo Deep or the **Japan Trench (10,554 m)**" | 10,554 m |
| ✅ Leong scan PDF p.89 | "the **Japanese Trench (28,000 ft)**" | ≈ **8,534 m** |

**A conflict of roughly 2,000 m on the same named feature.** No primary bathymetric source was
retrieved this pass (**U-27**), so **neither is asserted and neither is corrected**. Both appear with
their page and their unit. Note also N-17: **Majid's Everest at 8848 m / Leong's 29,028 ft and the
Mariana pair are all book-era**; the 2020 joint Nepal-China Everest revision and modern multibeam
soundings of the Challenger Deep were not retrieved, so **no modern value is asserted** either.

### ⇄ Conflict 3 of 8 — how much of the floor is abyssal plain?

✅ Leong printed 85: the deep-sea plain covers "**two-thirds of the ocean floor**". ✅ Majid printed
4.4: abyssal plains cover "**from 80 to 85 % of the floor of the Pacific Ocean**" and are "the most
widespread landforms on the Earth". **The numerators differ** — all ocean floor against the Pacific
floor alone — and even allowing for that, the two cannot be reconciled without a source neither
supplies. **U-27:** neither was checked against a primary bathymetric source. **Present both; assert
neither; name the differing denominators.** That last move is the one that earns the mark: a
candidate who notices that one figure is global and the other Pacific-only has read both sentences.

### ⇄ Conflict 4 of 8 — when did *Challenger* sail?

✅ Leong printed 84: the **Challenger** expedition, "**1873-1876**", "the first successful world-wide
deep-sea expedition". ✅ Khullar printed 22: polymetallic nodules "first discovered on the scientific
voyage of **Challenger, 1872-76**" and seafloor phosphorite from "the Challenger Expedition of
**1872-76**". **A one-year divergence on the start date of the same voyage**, stated twice by
Khullar. Teach the conflict; it recurs in Lesson 16 where Khullar's nodule history is used.

### The two features C1 does define, and the one it leaves out

✅/⚠️ C1's ➕ block at lines 51-67 supplies three definitions and one trap, and they are kept:

- ⚠️ **Seamount** — an isolated submarine volcanic mountain rising from the ocean floor but **not
  reaching sea level**. ✅ Majid printed 4.5 adds the measurable form: "circular or elliptical
  projection from the seafloor, **more than one kilometre in height**, with a relatively steep slope
  of **20 to 25°**".
- ⚠️ **Guyot** — a **flat-topped** seamount, ✅ Majid printed 4.5: "a flat topped, submerged, inactive
  volcano … Generally they are confined to the **west-central Pacific**".
- ✅ **Black smokers** — Majid printed 4.5: "vents in the ocean floor which disgorge hot,
  sulfur-rich water from deep in the crust". These matter in Lesson 16: **polymetallic sulphide**
  deposits form at ridge-crest vents, which is why India's second ISA contract is on a **ridge** and
  its first is on an abyssal **plain**.

> 🔑 **The guyot trap, from C1 line 66, kept verbatim in substance:** a guyot's flat top indicates it
> was once **at or above sea level** and was planed by waves before subsiding. It is **not** flat
> because sediment filled a basin.

**What C1 leaves out and this lesson supplies:** the shelf, the slope, the rise, the abyssal plain
itself, and the hypsographic curve. Without them the ➕ block floats — a seamount rises from a floor
C1 never describes.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — one platform, three economies

✅ Khullar printed 22-23 (scan PDF p.28) ties India's marine resource geography to the shelf
explicitly: "Most of the oil and gas producing areas of the oceans are confined to the **continental
shelf** … India has **3.2 lakh sq km offshore area of sedimentary deposits on the continental shelf
upto a depth of 200 metres** … **Kachchh Shelf, Gulf of Khambhat and Mumbai High. Mumbai High is the
largest oil producer of India.** Huge gas reserves have been found in the **Krishna-Godavari basin**."

```text
   INDIA'S SHELF — ONE PHYSICAL PLATFORM, THREE SEPARATE ARGUMENTS
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  THE SHELF: ≤200 m, gentle, sunlit, sediment-trapping          │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
      │                     │                        │
      ▼                     ▼                        ▼
   FISHERIES           HYDROCARBONS              TIDAL RANGE
   sunlight reaches    sediment basins           shallow + gentle
   the floor →         buried and sealed →       → range amplified
   plankton →          Kachchh Shelf,            → Gulf of Khambhat,
   shelf fisheries     Gulf of Khambhat,         Gulf of Kachchh
   (Lesson 12)         Mumbai High, K-G basin    (Lessons 14, 15)
      │                     │                        │
      ▼                     ▼                        ▼
   OURS: where the     ↪ ROUTED: output,         OURS: the
   fish are and why    reserves, pricing,        mechanism of range
                       Sagarmala, port-led       as a shelf property
                       development
```

**The discipline this lesson enforces.** Topic 12 owns the **shelf as a physical locus** — why
resources and fish are there. It does **not** own what is extracted, by whom, at what price or under
what policy. ↪ Offshore hydrocarbons, Mumbai High output and mineral economics route to
`Geography/basic/31_Mineral-Energy-Resources-World-and-India` and
`Economy/basic/18_Infrastructure-PPPs-Logistics-and-Public-Investment`. Khullar's claim that "**at
present half of the world's total output of oil and gas comes from offshore wells**" is undated and
unverified (**U-30**) and is **not reproduced as a current share**.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison — the two classifications of ocean relief.**

| | ✅ Leong, printed 85 and PDF 89 | ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.2-4.5 |
|---|---|---|
| Named features | Shelf, slope, deep-sea plain, deeps, with plateaux, ridges, trenches, basins and oceanic islands as sub-features | **Seven**: continental shelf (margin), slope, **rise**, abyssal floor, trenches, ridges, seamounts |
| Shelf edge | **100 fathom (600 ft) ≈ 183 m** isobath | "**200 m** is often conveniently used as a depth limit" |
| Units | Imperial throughout | Metric throughout |
| Strength | Function — why the shelf is the richest fishing ground, why it gates ports | Quantity — percentage shares, named trench depths, the hypsographic curve |
| Gap | **No continental rise**; no percentage shares | No functional argument about fisheries or ports |

⚠️ **Why the shelf-edge divergence is not an error.** 183 m and 200 m are two **operational
definitions** of the same break. The shelf break is a change of gradient, not a depth contour;
whether you call its edge the 100-fathom line or the 200-metre line is a cartographic convention.
This is Lesson 1's idea reappearing in a different costume — and it is the reason an examiner can
ask "the continental shelf is usually taken to extend to about …" and have two defensible answers in
two standard books.

**Objection.** "The shelf is 5 per cent of the Earth; why does it dominate the lesson?"

**Reply.** Because economic and biological significance are not proportional to area. The abyssal
plain is a far larger share of the planet and contributes, at present, **no fishery and no
production**. ✅ Leong printed 85 states the functional reason the shelf is different: "Their limited
depth allows sunlight to penetrate through the water, which encourages the growth of minute plants
and other microscopic organisms. They are thus rich in plankton on which millions of surface and
bottom-feeding fishes thrive. **The continental shelves are therefore the richest fishing grounds in
the world, e.g. the Grand Banks off Newfoundland, the North Sea and the Sunda Shelf.**" Light depth,
not area, is the variable.

**Unresolved residual.** The abyssal extent (**U-27**), the Japan Trench depth (**N-18**) and the
modern values for Everest and the Challenger Deep (**N-17**) are all unresolved here, and are left so.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Choosing between 10,554 m and 28,000 ft for the Japan Trench.** Both are quoted; neither is
  asserted (N-18).
- ❌ **Writing "the abyssal plain covers two-thirds of the ocean floor" as a fact.** Leong says so of
  the world floor; Majid says 80-85 per cent of the **Pacific** floor. Different denominators,
  neither verified (N-19, U-27).
- ❌ **Treating 183 m and 200 m as a contradiction.** They are two conventions for one gradient break.
- ❌ **Quoting 8848 m or 11,022 m as current values.** Both are book-era; no modern value was
  retrieved (N-17).
- ❌ **Explaining a guyot's flat top by sedimentation.** It is wave planation followed by subsidence.
- ❌ **Writing an offshore-production or reserves argument inside a Geography answer.** The shelf is
  ours; the barrels are not.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography, directly. The relief sequence is the
canonical "salient feature" of the ocean.

**Owned demands served.** This lesson supplies the **physical setting** for **O2 (2022 GS-I Q14)** —
the shelf mechanism is one of the three fishing-ground mechanisms of Lesson 12 — and for **O4 (2021
Prelims Q56)**, since the ISA contract areas of Lesson 16 sit on an abyssal **plain** and a
**ridge** respectively, which only makes sense once both features exist.

**Adjacent demands this lesson must NOT absorb.** ↪ **2025 GS-I Q16**, "change in continents and
ocean basins due to crustal tectonics", belongs to `Geography/basic/02_The-Earths-Crust-Rocks`: the
ocean **basin** as a tectonic object is Topic 02's; the ocean **water** in it is ours. ↪ **2020 GS-I
Q4** on the Circum-Pacific Zone's geophysical characteristics belongs to
`Geography/basic/03_Vulcanism-and-Earthquakes`: trench and arc **seismicity** is not ours, though
the trench as a relief feature is.

**Answer use.** When a question asks for ocean relief, run the traverse in order and attach **one
function to each feature** rather than a depth to each name. Shelf → light and sediment; slope →
canyons and mass transport; rise → apron; plain → the great flat majority; ridge → where crust is
made and sulphides form; trench → where it is destroyed.

### Revision points

1. Relief sequence: **shelf → slope → rise → abyssal plain → ridge and seamounts → trench**.
2. Shelf edge: **100 fathom (600 ft) ≈ 183 m** (Leong) or **200 m** "conveniently used" (Majid) —
   two conventions for one gradient break.
3. Shelf gradient **≈1 in 500**; slope gradient **≈1 in 20** — "an abrupt change" (Leong printed 85).
4. Shelf width: a few miles to a **maximum ≈1,207 km (750 miles)** off Arctic Siberia; **32-161 km
   (20-100 miles)** usual (Leong); **mean ≈70 km** (Majid 4.2).
5. Shelf **≈5 per cent** of the Earth's surface; shelf + slope **≈11.4 per cent** of which slope is
   **≈6.4 per cent**; rise **≈3.8 per cent**. **11.4 − 6.4 = 5.0 ✓ closes** (Majid 4.2).
6. Abyssal floor **3,000-5,500 m** (Majid 4.4) or "two to three miles" (Leong printed 85).
7. **⇄ N-19:** two-thirds of the ocean floor (Leong) against 80-85 per cent of the **Pacific** floor
   (Majid). Different denominators; **U-27** neither verified.
8. **⇄ N-18:** Japan Trench **10,554 m** (Majid, "Ramapo Deep") against **28,000 ft ≈ 8,534 m**
   (Leong) — a ≈2,000 m conflict.
9. Ridges: **≈22.1 per cent** of the ocean surface, **≈66,000 km** long, **>1,400 km** wide, peaks
   **≈3,000 m** above the floor, **Mid-Atlantic Ridge ≈14,450 km** (Majid 4.4).
10. Hypsographic means: land **+840 m**, ocean **−3790 m** (Majid 4.4). Extremes book-era (N-17).
11. **Seamount** = submarine volcanic high, >1 km, 20-25° slope. **Guyot** = flat-topped by **wave
    planation then subsidence**, mostly west-central Pacific. **Black smokers** = sulphide-forming
    vents — the reason the PMS contract is on a ridge.
12. Leong's four conversions all close: 100 fathom = 600 ft; 5,000 fathom = 30,000 ft; 2,000 fathom
    = 12,000 ft; 36,000 > 29,028.
13. **⇄ N-31:** *Challenger* is **1873-1876** in Leong and **1872-76** in Khullar.
14. India's shelf: **3.2 lakh sq km of sedimentary offshore area to 200 m**, with the **Kachchh
    Shelf, Gulf of Khambhat, Mumbai High** and the **Krishna-Godavari basin** (Khullar printed 23).
15. **U-30:** Khullar's "half of the world's total output of oil and gas" is undated; not reproduced.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does roughly 5 per cent of the Earth's surface carry the world's richest fisheries,
almost all offshore hydrocarbons and the largest tidal ranges, when two-thirds of the ocean floor
carries none of them?

**Model answer:** Because three independent consequences follow from one geometric fact — the shelf
is **shallow and gently sloping**. First, light: ✅ Leong, printed 85, states that the shelf's
"limited depth allows sunlight to penetrate through the water", sustaining plankton and therefore
"the richest fishing grounds in the world, e.g. the **Grand Banks** off Newfoundland, the **North
Sea** and the **Sunda Shelf**". Below the shelf break, light fails and primary production with it.
Second, sediment: the shelf is the trap where terrigenous material accumulates, buries and seals
organic matter, which is why ✅ Khullar, printed 23, places India's **3.2 lakh sq km** of offshore
sedimentary area, the **Kachchh Shelf**, the **Gulf of Khambhat** and **Mumbai High** on the shelf,
and why placers concentrate there. Third, tide: ✅ Leong, printed 85, notes that shelf depth and
gradient "**increase the height of tides**", which amplifies range in funnel-shaped shelf gulfs and
gates port access. The abyssal plain is vastly larger but dark, sediment-starved and tidally
unremarkable. **Significance beats area.**

**Misconception to avoid:** That the deep ocean is where the ocean's resources are because it is
bigger. Area is not the operative variable; depth of light penetration, sediment supply and basin
geometry are. The shelf's importance is a consequence of its shallowness, not of its size.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 2

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Describe the sequence of ocean-floor relief features
from shore to trench, and examine why the two standard classifications of this sequence differ.

**Model:** The traverse runs **continental shelf → continental slope → continental rise → abyssal
plain → oceanic ridge and seamounts → trench**. ✅ Leong, printed 85, places the shelf edge at the
**100 fathom (600 ft) ≈ 183 m** isobath, with a gradient of about **1 in 500**, widths of **32-161
km (20-100 miles)** and a maximum of about **1,207 km (750 miles)** off Arctic Siberia, giving way
to a slope at "an abrupt change of gradient to about **1 in 20**". ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.2, instead
uses **200 m** as the shelf limit, a **mean width of about 70 km**, and quantifies the shares: shelf
**≈5 per cent** of the Earth's surface, shelf and slope together **≈11.4 per cent** of which slope is
**≈6.4 per cent**, and rise **≈3.8 per cent** — an internally closing set, since 11.4 − 6.4 = 5.0.

The classifications differ for three reasons. **Units**: Leong is imperial and Majid metric, so the
same break appears as 183 m or 200 m — a cartographic convention, not a disagreement about physics.
**Completeness**: Majid names a **continental rise**, which Leong omits entirely. **Purpose**: Leong
argues function — the shelf as the richest fishing ground and as the amplifier of tidal range —
while Majid supplies structure and percentage shares.

They also conflict. ⇄ Leong gives the deep-sea plain as "two-thirds of the ocean floor"; Majid gives
80-85 per cent of the **Pacific** floor, and neither was verified against a primary bathymetric
source. The sequence is agreed; its measurement is not. *(239 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Relief sequence shelf-to-trench **5** · two classifications compared **4** ·
hypsographic reading **4** · shelf-resource consequence **2**

## Lesson 3 — What falls to the bottom: oozes, clays and the two classifications

```text

📚 Owner context : Neither owner covers ocean deposits at all. C1's ➕ relief block
                   stops at seamount, guyot and trench. This lesson is carried
                   entirely by the two books, which classify the same material in
                   two incompatible ways.
📕 Book evidence : Leong printed 86 (scan PDF p.89) — muds, oozes, clays · Majid IWG
                   printed 4.8-4.9 (scan PDF p.136-137) — terrigenous, volcanic,
                   biotic, cosmogenous
🔍 Live check    : none owned by this lesson
📰 CA anchor     : none owned by this lesson
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/05_Landforms-by-Running-Water (sediment supply,
                   delta building) · Geography/basic/10_Coastal-Landforms (coastal
                   sediment budgets, long-shore drift, delta morphology)
⚠️ Live gaps     : no primary sedimentological source was retrieved; both schemes are
                   presented as the books' own and neither is adopted
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 3 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **facies-belt strip with twin legends**

### The intuition, before any term

Drop a grain of quartz and a microscopic shell into the open ocean at the same moment. The quartz,
being heavy and having come from a river mouth, will almost certainly have settled before it ever
reached deep water. The shell, which is tiny and made itself in the surface layer, will drift down
for days and land wherever it happens to be. **The floor of the ocean therefore records not what is
above it, but what manages to reach it.** Coarse, land-derived material falls out near land. Fine
biological material accumulates where nothing else does. And in the very deepest, most remote
basins, where even shells dissolve on the way down, what is left is a slow dust.

That is the whole logic of ocean deposits: **a sorting by distance from land and by depth**. It is
also, unexpectedly, a navigational and forensic tool — the sediment under a ship is a statement
about where the ship is.

### The primary visual — one strip, read twice

```text
 THE SAME SHORE-TO-ABYSS STRIP, READ WITH TWO INCOMPATIBLE LEGENDS

 ╔════════════ ✅ LEGEND ABOVE: LEONG, printed 86 (scan PDF p.89) ════════════╗
 ║  MUDS                  │  OOZES                      │  CLAYS              ║
 ║  "terrigenous"         │  "pelagic"                  │  "mainly as RED     ║
 ║  on the SHELVES        │  "the shelly and skeletal   │   CLAYS in the      ║
 ║  BLUE, GREEN or RED    │   remains of marine micro-  │   deeper parts of   ║
 ║  "by chemical content" │   organisms with CALCAREOUS │   the ocean basins" ║
 ║                        │   or SILICEOUS parts"       │  "particularly      ║
 ║                        │                             │   abundant in the   ║
 ║                        │                             │   PACIFIC OCEAN"    ║
 ║                        │                             │  "an accumulation   ║
 ║                        │                             │   of VOLCANIC DUST" ║
 ╚════════════════════════╧═════════════════════════════╧═════════════════════╝
   ▲                        ▲                             ▲
   │                        │                             │
 ┌─┴────────────┬───────────┴──────────────┬──────────────┴──────────────────┐
 │  SHELF       │  SLOPE + RISE            │  ABYSSAL PLAIN                  │
 │  0-200 m     │  200-3,000 m             │  3,000-5,500 m and deeper       │
 │              │                          │                                 │
 │ ████████████ │ ▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒ │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
 │ COARSE, FAST │ MIXED, GRADED            │ FINE, VERY SLOW                 │
 │ river-fed    │ turbidity currents rework│ nothing coarse ever arrives     │
 └──────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘
   │                        │                             │
   ▼                        ▼                             ▼

 ║  TERRIGENOUS           │  BIOTIC                     │  BIOTIC → then      ║
 ║  land-derived, carried │  "NERETIC deposits,         │  COSMOGENOUS        ║
 ║  by rain, running      │   PELAGIC deposits (OOZE),  │  "dust, TEKTITES    ║
 ║  water, ground water,  │   GLOBIGERINA OOZE,         │   and particles"    ║
 ║  wind, sea-waves and   │   RADIOLARIAN OOZE,         │  + VOLCANIC         ║
 ║  glaciers              │   RED CLAY"                 │  (ash, pumice,      ║
 ║                        │                             │   lava fragments)   ║
 ╚════════════════════════╧═════════════════════════════╧═════════════════════╝

 ⇄ THE DIVERGENCE, NAMED
   Leong classifies by WHAT THE DEPOSIT IS: mud / ooze / clay — a descriptive,
     three-fold scheme keyed to texture and composition.
   Majid classifies by WHERE IT CAME FROM: terrigenous / volcanic / biotic /
     cosmogenous — a four-fold scheme keyed to PROVENANCE.
   The schemes CUT ACROSS each other. "Red clay" is a deposit TYPE for Leong and
   sits inside the BIOTIC class for Majid, while Leong attributes it to VOLCANIC
   DUST — which is Majid's separate VOLCANIC class. One phenomenon, two
   incompatible taxonomies, and the overlap is a genuine source conflict.
```

**Caption.** Descriptive taxonomy above, provenance taxonomy below, on one strip. The point is not
that one is right; it is that **a classification is a question asked of the material**, and asking a
different question produces a different set of classes — the same move as Lesson 1's boundary
conventions.

### Reading the two schemes against each other

| | ✅ Leong, printed 86 | ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.8-4.9 |
|---|---|---|
| Organising principle | **What it is** — texture and composition | **Where it came from** — provenance |
| Classes | **Three**: muds, oozes, clays | **Four**: terrigenous, volcanic, biotic, cosmogenous |
| Shelf material | **Muds** — terrigenous, "blue, green or red by chemical content" | **Terrigenous**, listing six transport agents: rain, running water, ground water, wind, sea-waves and glaciers |
| Biological material | **Oozes** — "pelagic", calcareous or siliceous micro-organism remains | **Biotic**, subdivided into **neretic** and **pelagic**, with **Globigerina ooze**, **Radiolarian ooze** and **red clay** |
| Red clay | A **class of its own**, attributed to **volcanic dust**, "particularly abundant in the **Pacific Ocean**" | Placed **inside the biotic class**, alongside the oozes |
| Extraterrestrial material | **Absent** | A whole class: **cosmogenous** — "dust, **tektites** and particles" |
| Volcanic material | Appears only as the **cause of red clay** | A separate class |

⚠️ **The sharpest divergence is red clay.** It is simultaneously (a) a named deposit class, (b) a
member of Majid's biotic class, and (c) attributed by Leong to volcanic dust, which is Majid's
*volcanic* class. **All three statements come from the two books in front of us.** This is taught as
the divergence it is and not harmonised.

### Why depth sorts the material — the causal chain

⚠️ The sorting is produced by three independent filters acting together, and each can be stated as a
rule with an exception:

```text
   FILTER 1 — DISTANCE FROM SOURCE  (controls terrigenous supply)
   river mouth ──► coarse sand drops first ──► silt next ──► clay
   carried furthest ──► beyond the shelf break, terrigenous supply
   effectively ceases EXCEPT where turbidity currents run down
   submarine canyons and deliver graded land sediment to the rise.
   ⇒ RULE: terrigenous material dominates near land.
   ⇒ EXCEPTION: a canyon system can place land sediment in deep water.

   FILTER 2 — PRODUCTION IN THE SURFACE LAYER  (controls biogenic supply)
   plankton with CALCAREOUS (CaCO₃) or SILICEOUS (SiO₂) hard parts live in
   the sunlit surface, die, and sink. Where surface production is high and
   terrigenous dilution is low, their remains DOMINATE the floor as OOZE.
   ⇒ RULE: ooze accumulates under productive, land-remote water.
   ⇒ COROLLARY: an ooze records what lived ABOVE it, not what lies beside it.

   FILTER 3 — DISSOLUTION WITH DEPTH  (controls what survives the fall)
   cold, high-pressure deep water is more corrosive to CALCIUM CARBONATE.
   Below a certain depth, calcareous shells DISSOLVE before they land.
   ⇒ RULE: beyond that depth only SILICEOUS remains and insoluble dust
     survive — which is why the deepest, oldest basins carry RED CLAY.
   ⇒ WHICH IS WHY Leong records red clay as "particularly abundant in the
     PACIFIC" — the Pacific has the largest area of very deep floor.

   ⚠️ Standard oceanography. No local book states Filter 3 explicitly; Leong
   states its RESULT (red clay in the deepest parts) without the mechanism.
```

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why the Bay of Bengal floor is terrigenous and the central basin is not

This is the cleanest available Indian application of the three filters, and it is available because
Lesson 1 established the basin's geometry.

| | **Bay of Bengal** | **Central Indian Ocean Basin** |
|---|---|---|
| Terrigenous supply | Enormous. The **Ganga-Brahmaputra** system delivers Himalayan sediment directly into the head of the Bay; ✅ Majid printed 4.8 notes "comparatively low salinity … along the mouth of the **Amazon, Congo, and Ganga deltas**", which is the same discharge seen through the salinity window | Effectively nil. No major river reaches it; it lies far from any coast |
| Dominant deposit character | **Terrigenous** — land-derived mud, graded and reworked, extending far beyond the shelf because the sediment load is so large | **Pelagic** — fine biogenic and authigenic material, accumulating very slowly |
| Consequence for Lesson 16 | A sediment-blanketed floor | **Polymetallic nodules** grow at the sediment-water interface over very long periods; they are found where sedimentation is **slow enough not to bury them**. India's PMN contract area is in the **Central Indian Ocean Basin**, not in the Bay |
| Consequence for Lesson 7 | The same freshwater that dilutes the Bay's surface also delivers the sediment — one discharge, two signatures | — |

⚠️ **The India hook's payoff is a causal link, not a fact.** The reason India's nodule contract is in
the central basin rather than the Bay of Bengal is, at bottom, **sedimentation rate** — and
sedimentation rate is the subject of this lesson. ✅ Khullar printed 22 confirms the association
from the other side: seafloor phosphorite was "first discovered as nodules in dredge samples
obtained from the **Agulhas Bank** off South Africa during the Challenger Expedition of 1872-76 …
mostly on the **continental margins and upper parts of the continental slopes** at depths of less
than **500 metres** … normally confined to the zone between **40°N and 40°S**", with "total reserves
… estimated at **450 million tonnes**" — a different nodule, a different depth belt, a different
facies. ⚠️ Khullar's phosphorite figures are **book-era and attributed**, not current.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "Ocean deposits are a Prelims-only topic. Why a whole Mains lesson?"

**Reply.** Three reasons, all of which are cashed later. First, the **facies logic is the only way to
explain why nodule fields are where they are**, which Lesson 16 needs for the owned 2021 demand.
Second, it is the **cleanest available illustration of the classification problem** that runs through
this topic — two authorities, one phenomenon, incompatible classes. Third, it supplies the
**sediment half of the shelf's resource argument**: placers and hydrocarbons exist on the shelf
because the shelf is where land-derived material stops.

**Unresolved residual.** No primary sedimentological source was retrieved. Both schemes are
presented as the books' own, with the red-clay divergence named and left open.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Merging the two classifications into a single list of seven classes.** They answer different
  questions and overlap; a merged list is a fabrication.
- ❌ **Calling red clay a biogenic deposit without qualification.** Majid places it with the oozes;
  Leong attributes it to volcanic dust.
- ❌ **Assuming terrigenous material cannot reach deep water.** Turbidity currents in submarine
  canyons deliver it to the rise.
- ❌ **Writing a delta or coastal-erosion argument here.** ↪ Delta building, sediment budgets and
  long-shore drift belong to `Geography/basic/05_Landforms-by-Running-Water` and
  `Geography/basic/10_Coastal-Landforms`.
- ❌ **Treating ooze as evidence of what lives on the floor.** It is evidence of what lived in the
  **surface layer** above it.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography. Ocean deposits are a classic
salient-feature item and are among the few parts of this topic with no live-source dimension at all.

**Owned demands served.** Indirectly: this lesson supplies the sedimentation-rate reasoning that
makes **O4 (2021 Prelims Q56)**'s contract geography intelligible in Lesson 16.

**Answer use.** If a question asks for ocean deposits, **lead with the sorting principle and then
give the classes**, naming both schemes. A paragraph that says "deposits are sorted by distance from
land and by depth of dissolution; Leong classifies them descriptively as muds, oozes and clays while
Majid classifies them by provenance as terrigenous, volcanic, biotic and cosmogenous" has already
demonstrated more command than a list of eight names.

### Revision points

1. Ocean deposits are sorted by **distance from land**, **surface production** and **dissolution
   with depth** — three filters, not one.
2. ✅ Leong printed 86, descriptive scheme, **three** classes: **muds** (terrigenous, on the shelves,
   blue/green/red by chemical content), **oozes** (pelagic, calcareous or siliceous micro-organism
   remains), **clays** (mainly red clay in the deepest basins).
3. ✅ Leong attributes **red clay to volcanic dust** and calls it "particularly abundant in the
   **Pacific Ocean**".
4. ✅ Majid printed 4.8-4.9, provenance scheme, **four** classes: **terrigenous**, **volcanic**,
   **biotic**, **cosmogenous**.
5. ✅ Majid's biotic class contains **neretic**, **pelagic (ooze)**, **Globigerina ooze**,
   **Radiolarian ooze** and **red clay**.
6. ✅ Majid's cosmogenous class is "dust, **tektites** and particles" — **absent from Leong entirely**.
7. **⇄ The divergence:** red clay is a class for Leong, a biotic member for Majid, and volcanic in
   origin on Leong's own account. Do not harmonise.
8. Six terrigenous transport agents in Majid: rain, running water, ground water, wind, sea-waves,
   glaciers.
9. **Calcareous ooze fails at depth** because deep water dissolves carbonate; siliceous remains and
   insoluble dust survive. ⚠️ Standard oceanography; Leong gives the result, not the mechanism.
10. An ooze records the **surface** community, not the floor community.
11. 🇮🇳 The **Bay of Bengal floor is terrigenous** because the **Ganga-Brahmaputra** delivers Himalayan
    sediment; the **Central Indian Ocean Basin** is pelagic and slowly accumulating — which is why
    nodules are viable there.
12. ✅ Khullar printed 22: seafloor phosphorite at **<500 m**, on margins and upper slopes, between
    **40°N and 40°S**, first found on the **Agulhas Bank**, reserves **≈450 million tonnes**
    (book-era, attributed).

### Concept check

**Question:** Two sediment samples are raised, one from the outer Bay of Bengal and one from the
Central Indian Ocean Basin at comparable depth. Explain, using the controls on deposition, how they
would differ and why.

**Model answer:** The Bay sample would be dominated by **terrigenous** material — fine, land-derived
mud carried from the Himalaya by the **Ganga-Brahmaputra** system, which delivers so large a load
that terrigenous deposition extends well beyond the shelf break through canyon and turbidity
transport. The central-basin sample would be **pelagic**: fine biogenic remains and slowly
accumulating insoluble residue, because no river reaches it and the only significant supply is what
settles out of the surface layer. The difference is produced by **distance from a sediment source**,
not by depth alone. Depth then adds a second filter: calcareous remains dissolve during the fall in
cold, high-pressure deep water, so the deepest and most remote floors retain siliceous material and
insoluble dust — the setting in which ✅ Leong, printed 86, places **red clay**, "particularly
abundant in the Pacific Ocean". The practical consequence is that the central basin accumulates
**slowly enough for polymetallic nodules to grow at the sediment-water interface rather than being
buried**, which is why India's PMN contract area lies there and not in the Bay. Classification of the
two samples would also differ by scheme: Leong would call them mud and clay, Majid terrigenous and
biotic.

**Misconception to avoid:** That depth alone determines the deposit. Distance from a terrigenous
source is the first and usually dominant control; two floors at the same depth can carry entirely
different material if one sits off a great river and the other does not.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 3

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Account for the distribution of ocean deposits, and
comment on why standard texts classify them differently.

**Model:** Ocean deposits are sorted by three filters. **Distance from land** controls terrigenous
supply: coarse material drops near river mouths, and beyond the shelf break supply largely ceases
except through canyon-fed turbidity currents. **Surface productivity** controls biogenic supply:
calcareous and siliceous plankton remains settle as **ooze** where terrigenous dilution is low.
**Dissolution with depth** removes carbonate during the fall, leaving the deepest basins with
insoluble residue — ✅ Leong, printed 86, records **red clay** as "particularly abundant in the
Pacific Ocean". Classification differs because the two texts ask different questions. Leong
classifies **descriptively** — muds, oozes, clays. ✅ Majid, printed 4.8-4.9, classifies by
**provenance** — terrigenous, volcanic, biotic, cosmogenous, adding **tektites** that Leong omits.
The schemes cut across each other: red clay is a class for one and a biotic member for the other.
The distribution is agreed; the taxonomy is a choice. *(137 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Four deposit sources with their provenance **5** · depth-facies logic **3** ·
classification divergence named **2**

### Route-outs required of this lesson

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| Sediment supply from rivers, delta building and sediment budgets | `Geography/basic/05_Landforms-by-Running-Water` |
| Coastal sediment budgets, long-shore drift, delta morphology at the coast; 2023 Prelims Q5 | `Geography/basic/10_Coastal-Landforms` |

## Lesson 4 — The temperature structure of the ocean

```text
                   own thermal structure — no mixed layer, no thermocline, no deep
                   zone, no volume shares, no horizontal gradient.
📕 Book evidence : Majid IWG printed 4.4-4.7 (scan PDF p.132-135) — seven
                   determinants, horizontal distribution, three vertical zones with
                   volume shares · Leong printed 87 (scan PDF p.90) — annual range,
                   latitudinal values, vertical gradient, the 80-per-cent statement
🔍 Live check    : MoES Lok Sabha Starred Question No. *273, answered 19 March 2025
📰 CA anchor     : CA-5 — tropical Indian Ocean SST +about 1°C during 1951-2015;
                   India surface air temperature +about 0.7°C during 1901-2018; both
                   from the MoES report "Assessment of Climate Change over the
                   Indian Region". DIFFERENT WINDOWS.
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements (land-sea thermal contrast,
                   isotherms, air temperature; 2023 Prelims Q64, 2025 Prelims Q27)
⚠️ Live gaps     : N-35 any per-decade rate is DERIVED BY DIVISION and must be
                   labelled as such; no MoES document retrieved states one
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 4 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **three-layer thermal column with a depth-temperature curve**

### The intuition, before any term

Land heats and cools fast; the sea does neither. Stand on a Rajasthan dune at midnight in June and
the sand is cool; the Arabian Sea a few hundred kilometres away has barely moved. Three properties
explain it: water has a very high heat capacity, it is **transparent** so solar energy is deposited
through a thickness rather than on a surface, and it **moves**, so the heat it absorbs is mixed down
and carried away. **The ocean is therefore the planet's thermal flywheel** — slow to spin up, slow
to spin down, and the reason a maritime climate is not a continental one.

But the flywheel is not uniform. Almost all of the ocean is cold. The warm ocean everyone pictures
is a thin lid.

### The primary visual — the thermal column, with its curve and its arithmetic

```text
  THE OCEAN'S VERTICAL THERMAL STRUCTURE          TEMPERATURE →
  ✅ Majid IWG printed 4.6-4.7, with ✅ Leong      0°C    10°C   20°C   30°C
     printed 87 as an independent check            │      │      │      │
                                                   │      │      │      │
   0 m ┌─────────────────────────────────┐         │      │      │   ●──┤ warm, well
       │ SURFACE / MIXED ZONE            │         │      │      │   │  │ mixed, nearly
       │ "Photic or Euphotic Zone"       │         │      │      │   │  │ isothermal
       │ ≈2 % of total ocean VOLUME      │         │      │      │   │  │
       │ depth "only about 100 metre"    │         │      │      │   │  │
       │ temperature AND salinity        │         │      │      │   │  │
       │ "relatively constant"           │         │      │      │   │  │

       │ THERMOCLINE (or PYCNOCLINE)     │         │      │     ╱─────┘  │ ← STEEP
       │ 100 m → 1000 m                  │         │     ╱│      │       │   FALL
       │ ≈18 % of total VOLUME           │         │    ╱ │      │       │

  ║  2 %  (mixed)  +  18 %  (thermocline)  +  80 %  (deep)  =  100 %   ✓    ║
  ║  ✅ Majid IWG printed 4.6-4.7                                           ║
  ║                                                                         ║
  ║  INDEPENDENT CONVERGENCE from the other book, by a different route:     ║

  ✅ LEONG'S INDEPENDENT VERTICAL GRADIENT (printed 87, imperial in original)
     surface → 200 fathoms (≈366 m) : ≈1°F per 10 fathoms (≈0.56°C per ≈18 m)
     200 → 500 fathoms (≈366-914 m) : slower
     below 500 fathoms              : under 1°F per 100 fathoms
     below 2,000 fathoms (12,000 ft ≈ 3,658 m) : "uniformly cold"
```

**Caption.** The warm ocean is a hundred-metre lid on a cold ocean four kilometres thick. The
thermocline is not a layer so much as a **barrier**, and that barrier is the pivot of Lessons 7, 11,
18 and 19 — it is what upwelling must break through, what El Niño deepens, and what stratification
strengthens.

### Why the ocean's annual range is so small — the mechanism, then the numbers

⚠️ Four properties, each with its consequence:

| Property of water | Consequence | Where this edition uses it |
|---|---|---|
| Very high **specific heat capacity** | A given energy input raises sea temperature far less than it raises land temperature | The whole lesson |
| **Transparency** — radiation penetrates tens of metres | Energy is distributed through a thickness, not concentrated on a skin | Lessons 11, 12 (the photic zone) |
| **Mobility** — the surface layer is stirred by wind and moved by currents | Absorbed heat is mixed down and exported horizontally | Lessons 8-10, 13 |
| **Evaporation** consumes latent heat at the surface | A powerful cooling term the land lacks in equal measure | Lessons 5, 6 (E − P) |

✅ **Leong, printed 87**, gives the resulting numbers: the annual range is "under 10°F" (≈5.6°C) "for
most open seas", with surface values from "**70°F**" (≈21°C) in equatorial waters to "**55°F**"
(≈13°C) at 45°N and S. ✅ **Majid, printed 4.4-4.6**, gives a different and finer horizontal set:
lower latitudes about **26°C**; **22°C at 20°**, **14°C at 40°**, **0°C near the poles**; a global
average annual range of about **12°C**, with the "highest annual range … in North Atlantic Ocean";
and the important rider that "the annual range of temperature is **higher in the inland seas** as
compared to the open oceans".

⚠️ **The inland-sea rider is the lesson's quiet gem.** An enclosed sea is smaller, shallower, less
freely mixed and more surrounded by land — so it behaves more like land. The same reasoning that
makes the **Red Sea** and the **Persian Gulf** thermal extremes in this lesson makes them salinity
extremes in Lesson 6 and density extremes in Lesson 7. ✅ Leong, printed 87, gives the Red Sea as
"**85° to 100°F**" (≈29°C to ≈38°C), an enormous range for a sea.

### Majid's seven determinants of ocean temperature — ✅ printed 4.4-4.6

1. **Latitude** — the first-order control on insolation.
2. **Prevailing winds** — offshore winds drive warm surface water away and allow cooler water up;
   onshore winds pile warm water against the coast. (This is upwelling in embryo; Lesson 11 derives
   it properly.)
3. **Unequal distribution of land and water** — "the oceans of the **Northern Hemisphere are warmer
   than that of the Southern Oceans**", with a hemispheric mean of **19.4°C** north against
   **16.1°C** south. ⚠️ The causal chain is genuinely examinable: the Northern Hemisphere has more
   land, more enclosed and semi-enclosed seas, and no circumpolar current, while the Southern Ocean
   has an unobstructed zonal flow around Antarctica that keeps it cold.
4. **Rate of evaporation** — "equivalent to **350,000 cubic kilometers**" a year, a latent-heat
   export that cools the surface.
5. **Density of water** — denser water sinks and is replaced.
6. **Ocean currents** — "the coast along with a cold water current flows record more **fog**, but
   less **precipitation**". This is the cold-current coastal-desert mechanism of Lessons 9 and 10,
   stated here first.
7. **Local factors** — basin shape, enclosure, river input, shelter.

**✗ V-05, a non sequitur to flag.** ✅ Majid printed 4.5 writes, correctly and load-bearingly, that
"the density of water is mainly a function of its **salinity and temperature**" — and then
immediately adds "In the areas of high salinity, there is relatively high temperature and
vice-versa". The second sentence is a **correlation claim inserted into a density paragraph** and
supports nothing in it; it is also the seed of the physical-sign error that Lesson 6 corrects
(N-02). **Teach the first clause; flag the second.**

### 📄 CA-5 — the official warming pair, and the unit trap inside it

📄 **Ministry of Earth Sciences, Lok Sabha Starred Question No. *273, answered 19 March 2025**, parts
(b) and (d), citing the MoES report **"Assessment of Climate Change over the Indian Region"**:

- "the **surface air temperature over India** has risen by about **0.7°C during 1901-2018**"
- "the **sea surface temperatures in the tropical Indian Ocean** increased by about **1°C during
  1951-2015**"

```text
   ⚠️ N-35 — THE UNIT TRAP, SHOWN EXPLICITLY

   Two warming figures, TWO DIFFERENT WINDOWS. They are not comparable as stated.

   OCEAN : 1.0°C over 1951-2015 = 64 years = 6.4 decades
           1.0 ÷ 6.4  ≈ 0.156 °C per decade   ← DERIVED BY DIVISION
   LAND  : 0.7°C over 1901-2018 = 117 years = 11.7 decades
           0.7 ÷ 11.7 ≈ 0.060 °C per decade   ← DERIVED BY DIVISION

   RATIO : 0.156 ÷ 0.060 ≈ 2.6                ← DERIVED, and INDICATIVE ONLY,
                                                 because the windows do not overlap

   ╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
   ║  NO MoES DOCUMENT RETRIEVED THIS PASS STATES A PER-DECADE RATE.       ║
   ║  Both rates above are ARITHMETIC PERFORMED HERE and are labelled so.  ║
   ║  They may be used to make the comparison; they may NEVER be cited as  ║
   ║  MoES figures. Quoting "0.156°C per decade (MoES)" is a fabrication.  ║
   ╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
```

⚠️ **Why the comparison is still worth making.** The ocean has warmed faster than the land surface
over the windows reported, which inverts the intuition people carry from the thermal-flywheel
argument. The resolution is that the flywheel argument is about **variability**, not about
**trend**: a high heat capacity damps the annual swing while simultaneously allowing a very large
total heat uptake. The ocean's range is small and its accumulated heat is enormous — and that
accumulated heat is what Lesson 19's marine heatwaves and Lesson 20's sea-level series are made of.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — two seas, one latitude, different behaviour

```text

   │ mixed layer         │  ventilated mixed    │  STRATIFIED — heat  │

   ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤

   │ forcing             │  west-coast          │  forcing            │

   │                     │  → UPWELLING         │                     │
   ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤

   │ behaviour in the    │  the monsoon season  │  under its cap      │
```
