```text
   temperature value exists anywhere in the evidence base for either sea, and
   none is stated here or anywhere in this edition.
```

📄 The one dated observation available on this pair is the IMD bulletin's: in **August 2026**,
"notably warmer SSTs prevailing over the **Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal**" — *as of the IMD
ENSO and IOD Bulletin of September 2026, on August 2026 data*. That is a **dated snapshot of both**,
not a statement about the contrast between them, and Lesson 19 shows why it is decisive for a
completely different reason.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "If 80 per cent of the ocean is between about 1.7°C and 4.4°C, why does surface
temperature matter at all?"

**Reply.** Because the ocean interacts with the atmosphere **only through its skin**, and because the
things that matter — evaporation, convection, cyclone energy, coral tolerance, index definitions —
are all surface or near-surface processes. The deep ocean's role is to be the **reservoir**: it is
where heat goes and where it comes back from, on timescales of centuries. The thermocline is the
valve between the two, which is why every mechanism in this edition that changes climate on a human
timescale works by **moving the thermocline** rather than by changing the deep ocean's temperature.

**Residual.** The warming pair (**CA-5**) is stated over non-overlapping windows and no per-decade
rate exists in any retrieved document (**N-35**). The ratio is indicative and is labelled as derived.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Quoting a per-decade warming rate as an MoES figure.** No retrieved MoES document states one;
  any such rate is division performed by the writer and must say so (N-35).
- ❌ **Comparing 1°C and 0.7°C as though they covered the same period.** They cover 1951-2015 and
  1901-2018.
- ❌ **Reproducing "in the areas of high salinity, there is relatively high temperature."** It is a
  correlation claim smuggled into a density paragraph (V-05) and it seeds the Lesson 6 error.
- ❌ **Treating the thermocline as a depth.** It is a **gradient** and therefore a barrier; its depth
  varies with basin, season and phase, which is the entire content of Lesson 18.
- ❌ **Writing a land-sea thermal contrast or isotherm argument.** ↪ That is
  `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` — **2023 Prelims Q64** on continental-oceanic temperature
  contrast by season and **2025 Prelims Q27** on January isotherms bending over land and ocean are
  both Topic 13's.
- ❌ **Stating an Arabian Sea or Bay of Bengal temperature or salinity value.** None exists in the
  base (U-31).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography (the ocean's thermal structure), and the
geophysical-phenomena clause's water-body limb (**CA-5** is a measured change in the ocean as a
critical feature).

**Owned demand served.** **O5 — 2021 Prelims GS-I Q58, "Ocean surface temperature patterns trade
winds westerlies."** The capability tested is how the **planetary wind belts organise surface
temperature** and the currents that redistribute it. This lesson supplies the temperature side;
Lessons 8, 9 and 10 supply the wind and current side. **The key is not available locally and none is
inferred.**

**Answer use.** For any ocean-temperature question, give the **vertical structure first and the
horizontal pattern second**, because the vertical structure is what makes the horizontal pattern
possible — a thin, mobile warm lid is what allows latitude and wind to organise a surface pattern at
all. Then date any change figure.

### Revision points

1. Three vertical zones: **mixed/photic (≈100 m, ≈2 % of volume)**, **thermocline/pycnocline
   (100-1000 m, ≈18 %)**, **deep zone (below 1000 m, ≈80 %, ≈1-2°C near the bottom)**. ✅ Majid 4.6-4.7.
2. **2 + 18 + 80 = 100 ✓ closes exactly.**
3. ✅ Leong printed 87 converges independently: "over **80 per cent** of ocean water" lies between
   **35° and 40°F ≈ 1.7-4.4°C** — the same share by a different method.
4. ✅ Leong's vertical gradient: ≈1°F per 10 fathoms to 200 fathoms; slower to 500; under 1°F per
   100 fathoms below; **uniformly cold below 2,000 fathoms (12,000 ft ≈ 3,658 m)**.
5. Annual range "under 10°F (≈5.6°C) for most open seas" (Leong); global average range **≈12°C**,
   highest in the North Atlantic (Majid); **higher in inland seas than open oceans** (Majid).
6. ✅ Leong: equatorial ≈**70°F (≈21°C)**, 45°N and S ≈**55°F (≈13°C)**; **Red Sea 85-100°F (≈29-38°C)**.
7. ✅ Majid horizontal: ≈**26°C** low latitudes, **22°C at 20°**, **14°C at 40°**, **0°C near the
   poles**; **N hemisphere 19.4°C vs S hemisphere 16.1°C**.
8. ✅ Majid's seven determinants: latitude · prevailing winds · unequal land-water distribution ·
   evaporation (**≈350,000 km³/yr**) · density · ocean currents (**more fog, less precipitation** on
   cold-current coasts) · local factors.
9. **✗ V-05:** "density is a function of salinity and temperature" is correct; "in areas of high
   salinity there is relatively high temperature" is a non sequitur and seeds N-02.
10. 📄 **CA-5, 19 March 2025:** tropical Indian Ocean SST **+≈1°C, 1951-2015**; India surface air
    temperature **+≈0.7°C, 1901-2018**; both from "Assessment of Climate Change over the Indian
    Region".
11. **N-35:** 1 ÷ 6.4 ≈ **0.156°C/decade** and 0.7 ÷ 11.7 ≈ **0.060°C/decade** are **derived by
    division**; the ratio ≈2.6 is indicative because the windows do not overlap. No MoES document
    retrieved states a per-decade rate.
12. 🇮🇳 The Arabian Sea mixes more readily; the Bay of Bengal carries a **freshwater cap** that traps
    heat in a thin surface film. **Direction and mechanism only — U-31 forbids values.**
13. The ocean's **small annual range** and its **large accumulated heat** are not in conflict: the
    first is about variability, the second about trend.

### Concept check

**Question:** The ocean's annual temperature range is far smaller than any land surface's, yet
official Indian figures show the tropical Indian Ocean warming faster than India's land surface air
temperature. Reconcile the two statements.

**Model answer:** They describe different quantities. The small annual range is a statement about
**variability** and follows from water's high heat capacity, its transparency, its mobility and its
evaporative cooling: ✅ Leong, printed 87, gives an annual range "under 10°F" (≈5.6°C) for most open
seas, against tens of degrees on land. The warming comparison is a statement about **trend**, and a
high heat capacity does not prevent a trend — it means a very large quantity of heat must be
absorbed to produce a given temperature rise, which is exactly what has happened. 📄 MoES, in Lok
Sabha Starred Question No. *273 answered **19 March 2025**, reports tropical Indian Ocean SST rising
by about **1°C during 1951-2015** and India's surface air temperature by about **0.7°C during
1901-2018**, both from "Assessment of Climate Change over the Indian Region". The two cannot be
compared as printed because the **windows differ**: normalising gives about **0.156°C per decade**
for the ocean and **0.060°C per decade** for the land, a ratio of roughly 2.6 — but both rates are
**derived here by division**, no retrieved MoES document states a per-decade rate, and the windows do
not overlap, so the ratio is indicative only. A damped annual cycle and a substantial long-term
uptake are therefore consistent.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a large heat capacity makes the ocean resistant to warming. It makes
the ocean resistant to **rapid fluctuation** while making it the planet's principal heat **sink** —
which is why ocean heat content, not ocean temperature range, is the quantity that matters for
sea-level rise and marine heatwaves.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 4

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Examine the vertical and horizontal temperature
structure of the oceans, and assess the care required when comparing official ocean and land warming
figures.

**Model:** Vertically the ocean has three zones. ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.6-4.7, gives a **surface or
mixed zone** of about **100 m** holding about **2 per cent** of ocean volume with temperature and
salinity "relatively constant"; a **thermocline** from **100 m to 1000 m** holding about **18 per
cent**, across which there is "a steep fall of temperature"; and a **deep zone** below 1000 m holding
about **80 per cent**, with bottom temperatures "around one or two degree C". The split closes
exactly at **2 + 18 + 80 = 100**, and ✅ Leong, printed 87, converges independently by reporting that
"over **80 per cent** of ocean water" lies between **35° and 40°F (≈1.7-4.4°C)**.

Horizontally, ✅ Majid gives about **26°C** in low latitudes, **22°C at 20°**, **14°C at 40°** and
**0°C near the poles**, with the Northern Hemisphere ocean averaging **19.4°C** against the
Southern's **16.1°C** — an asymmetry produced by the unequal distribution of land and water. Annual
range is small, about **12°C** globally, and **larger in inland seas**.

Comparison of warming figures demands care. 📄 MoES, answering Lok Sabha Starred Q.*273 on **19 March
2025**, gives tropical Indian Ocean SST **+≈1°C during 1951-2015** and India's surface air
temperature **+≈0.7°C during 1901-2018**. The windows differ, so the printed figures are not
comparable; normalising yields about **0.156** and **0.060°C per decade**, but both are **derived by
division**, not cited, and the non-overlapping windows make the ratio indicative only. *(229 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Three-zone structure with volume shares **6** · horizontal controls **4** ·
vertical gradient reasoning **3** · arithmetic closure shown **2**

Progress: 5 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **composition stack with a residual audit strip**

### The intuition, before any term

Sea water is not "water with salt in it" in the way tea is water with sugar in it. It is a solution
whose **proportions are nearly constant everywhere in the open ocean**, even though its total
concentration varies from place to place. Take a litre from the Bay of Bengal and a litre from the
South Atlantic: they will differ in how much dissolved material they contain, but the **ratio** of
sodium to magnesium to sulphate will be, for practical purposes, identical.

That constancy is the single most useful fact about salinity, because it means **one measurement
stands for all of them**. You do not have to assay seven salts; you measure one property —
historically chlorinity, now conductivity — and the rest follows. The ocean is well-mixed on the
timescale over which salts are added and removed, and the constancy of composition is the evidence.

### The primary visual — the composition stack, with its residual audited

```text
 WHAT IS DISSOLVED IN SEA WATER
 ✅ Majid IWG printed 4.7 (scan PDF p.135): "In water of the ocean, 96.5% is
    water, and 3.5% dissolved salts." Standard sea water = 35‰ at 0°C.

 ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │                         1 kg OF SEA WATER                              │
 │ ██████████████████████████████████████████████████████████████████░░░░ │
 │ │←──────────────── 96.5 % WATER ──────────────────────────────→│←3.5%→│ │
 └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                                                     │
                      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                      ▼
 ╔══ TABLE 4.1 "Chemical Composition of Sea Water", ✅ Majid printed 4.7 ══╗
 ║                                                                         ║
 ║  Sodium chloride      ████████████████████████████████████████  2.60 %  ║
 ║  Magnesium chloride   █████                                     0.30 %  ║
 ║  Magnesium sulphate   ███                                       0.20 %  ║
 ║  Calcium sulphate     ██                                        0.10 %  ║
 ║  Potassium chloride   ██                                        0.10 %  ║
 ║  Potassium bromide    ▏                                         0.01 %  ║
 ║  Other elements       ▏                                         0.01 %  ║
 ║  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── ║
 ║  TABULATED SUM                                                  3.32 %  ║
 ║  STATED TOTAL ("3.5% dissolved salts")                          3.50 %  ║
 ║  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════║

 ║  Majid's own table, taken on its own terms: 2.60 ÷ 3.32 = 78.3 %        ║
 ║  → 78.3 % IS "more than 77 per cent". ✓ The two books AGREE on the       ║
 ║    dominance of NaCl even though the table does not sum to its own total.║
 ║  → A table can be internally defective and still carry a sound ratio.    ║

```

**Caption.** The stack is the composition; the strip beneath it is the audit. **The residual is not
rounding** — 0.18 per cent of 3.5 is more than 5 per cent of all the dissolved material, and the
table's own smallest entries are 0.01 per cent. Showing the gap is better teaching than quietly
adding a row.

### Definition and units — and the sign discipline that follows

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 4.7:** "**Salinity is a measure of dissolved solids in seawater, usually
expressed in grams per kilogram or parts per thousand by weight. The standard sea water has a
salinity of 35[‰] at 0°C.**"

✅ **Leong, printed 86:** the ocean's average is "**35.2‰**", and lines of equal salinity on a map
are **isohalines** — a term Leong both defines and sets as an exercise at printed 90, question 5(c).

```text
  ✗ N-24 — THE SIGN TRAP, STATED ONCE AND ENFORCED EVERYWHERE

  Salinity is a ratio of mass to mass, and it is written:
      ‰  (per mille, parts per thousand)   or   ppt   or   g/kg
  ALL THREE MEAN THE SAME THING.

  ✗ Majid IWG renders the per-mille sign as a PER-CENT sign throughout his
    salinity section — "35%", "39%", "21%". Read literally, "35%" would be ten
    times the actual salinity of the ocean and would make sea water one-third
    salt by weight.
  ✓ C1 line 32 writes "About 35 ppt", which is correct.
  ✓ Leong writes ‰ consistently.

  THIS EDITION USES ‰ OR ppt AND NEVER "%" FOR SALINITY. Where a book's own
  symbol is quoted, the substitution is marked in square brackets, thus: 35[‰].

  WHY IT MATTERS FOR MARKS: a candidate who writes "ocean salinity is 35%" has
  written a physically absurd quantity. The examiner cannot tell whether it is a
  typographical slip or a misunderstanding, and must assume the latter.
```

### Where the salt comes from — ✅ Majid printed 4.7, three pathways

| Pathway | Majid's statement | What it implies |
|---|---|---|
| **Land runoff** | Salts carried by "rain, running water, ground water, wind, sea-waves and glaciers" — the weathering products of continental rock | The **dominant** long-term supply, and the reason the ocean is salty at all |
| **Deep-Earth / volcanic** | "Some of the salts, however, have their origin in the **deeper layers of the Earth**. The volcanic lava adds to the salinity" | Hydrothermal and volcanic input at ridges and vents — the same setting that makes Lesson 2's **black smokers** and Lesson 16's **polymetallic sulphides** |
| **Decomposed organic life** | Material returned to solution when marine organisms decay | A recycling term rather than a net source |

⚠️ **The question the pathway list raises and the books do not answer.** If rivers have been
delivering salts for billions of years, why is the ocean not steadily getting saltier? Because
**removal balances supply**: salts are taken out by biological uptake and burial, by precipitation in
evaporite basins, by adsorption onto sinking particles, and by hydrothermal exchange at ridges. The
ocean's salinity is a **steady state, not an accumulation**. This is ⚠️ standard oceanography; no
local book states it, and the edition says so rather than attributing it.

### The salinity classes, as Majid gives them — ✅ printed 4.8

| Class | Named cases and values |
|---|---|
| **Above normal** | **Red Sea about 39[‰]** · **Persian Gulf 38[‰]** · **Mediterranean 37[‰]** |
| **Normal** (35-36[‰]) | Caribbean · Gulf of Mexico · Gulf of California · Yellow Sea |
| **Below normal** | Arctic · Antarctic · Bering · Japan · **Baltic, 21[‰]** |

⇄ **Note the Baltic divergence immediately.** ✅ Majid gives the Baltic at **21[‰]**; ✅ Leong, printed
86 and again in his own exercise at printed 90, gives "the average salinity of the **Baltic Sea is
only 7‰**"; and C1 line 34 follows Leong with "**Baltic ~7 ppt**". ⚠️ Both can be true of different
parts of a sea whose salinity falls steeply from its mouth to its head — which is itself the
teaching point — but **neither book says which part it is describing**, so neither figure is asserted
as *the* Baltic salinity here.

### ✗ The two intra-source self-contradictions this lesson must carry

**✗ N-04 — Leong on the Caspian Sea, by a factor of nearly five, two pages apart.**

| Location | What Leong writes |
|---|---|
| ✅ printed 86 (scan PDF p.89) | "In areas of inland drainage … such as the **Caspian Sea, the salinity is very high, 180‰**, and in the Dead Sea a salinity of **250‰** has been recorded." |
| ✅ printed 87 (scan PDF p.90) | "In wholly or partially enclosed seas such as the **Caspian Sea**, Mediterranean Sea, Red Sea and Persian Gulf … **Salinity is high, often over 37‰**." |

**The same sea is 180‰ on one page and a member of a class described as "often over 37‰" on the
next.** ⚠️ **U-03:** no primary source for the Caspian was retrieved. **Both locations are quoted and
neither figure is asserted.**

**✗ N-05 — Leong on the Dead Sea, between his body text and his own exercise.**

| Location | What Leong writes |
|---|---|
| ✅ printed 86 (scan PDF p.89), body | "in the Dead Sea a salinity of **250‰** has been recorded" |
| ✅ printed 90 (scan PDF p.93), QUESTIONS AND EXERCISES 4(b) | "the average salinity of the Baltic Sea is only 7‰ whereas that of the Dead Sea is **240‰**" |

**A 10‰ discrepancy inside one chapter.** ⚠️ **U-03:** unverified. **Both quoted, neither asserted.**
Leong's third inland figure, **Lake Van at 330‰** (printed 87), is likewise attributed and flagged.

↪ **Route-out, and it is the sharpest one in this lesson.** The **Caspian Sea**, the **Dead Sea**,
**Lake Van**, the **Aral Sea**, **Sambhar**, **Didwana**, the **Great Salt Lake** and the **Rann of
Kachchh** are **inland water bodies**. "Salinity" in Topic 12 means **ocean salinity**. Inland saline
lakes belong to `Geography/basic/09_Lakes`, which owns **2021 Prelims Q54** (saline lakes of
Rajasthan — Didwana, Kuchaman, Sargol, Khatu) and **2018 Prelims Q13** (Aral Sea and Lake Baikal
shrinkage). They appear in this lesson **only** because Leong uses them to illustrate the
inland-drainage end-member of the ocean salinity scale — and because they are the site of two of his
self-contradictions.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why Indian bulletins write ‰ and not per cent

Every Indian operational ocean product — the salinity fields assimilated at INCOIS, the hydrographic
sections run from Indian research vessels, the profiles returned by the **302 Argo floats** that
📄 MoES reports for 2014-2025 — reports salinity in **practical salinity units or g/kg**, never in
per cent. The reason is not stylistic. A per-cent figure for a 3.5 per cent solution forces three
decimal places to resolve a difference that matters: the Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal differ by
amounts that are legible in ‰ and become 0.0x in per cent.

⚠️ **This is also the lesson's hardest discipline.** **U-31** records that **no salinity value exists
anywhere in the evidence base for either the Arabian Sea or the Bay of Bengal** — not in C1, not in
C2 (which carries no quantity of any kind), not in Leong, not in Majid, not in Khullar and not in any
retrieved official document. The contrast between the two seas is therefore taught throughout this
edition as **a direction and a mechanism, never as a pair of numbers**. The nearest book support is
✅ Majid printed 4.8's "comparatively low salinity is found along the mouth of the **Amazon, Congo,
and Ganga deltas**", which is cited for the **Bay side only** and is a statement about deltas, not
about basin means.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison — what each source actually supplies.**

| | C1 (owner) | ✅ Leong printed 86 | ✅ Majid IWG printed 4.7-4.8 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Definition | None | None, but gives the average | **Full**: dissolved solids, g/kg or ppt, 35‰ at 0°C |
| Average | "About 35 ppt" | **35.2‰** | **35‰** |
| Composition | None | NaCl "**more than 77 per cent**" | **Table 4.1**, seven entries, **summing to 3.32 against 3.5** |
| Sources of salt | None | None | **Three pathways** |
| Map term | None | **Isohalines**, defined | None |
| Unit discipline | ✓ writes **ppt** | ✓ writes **‰** | ✗ writes **%** throughout |

**Objection.** "If composition is nearly constant, why does the table matter?"

**Reply.** Because the constancy is exactly what the table lets you test, and because it is the
reason the ocean can be measured at all. It also matters because **the constancy fails in precisely
the places this topic cares about**: in enclosed basins where evaporation concentrates some salts to
the point of precipitation, in sea ice where brine is expelled selectively, and near river mouths
where river chemistry differs from sea chemistry. The rule and the exceptions are both examinable.

**Residual.** The composition residual (**N-16**) is unexplained by either book; the Caspian
(**N-04**) and Dead Sea (**N-05**) figures are self-contradictory and unverified (**U-03**); the
Baltic figure diverges between books. **Four open items in one short lesson** — and that density of
defect is itself the lesson's finding about the book layer.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing salinity with a per-cent sign.** 35‰ ≠ 35%. The books' own symbols are corrected and
  the substitution is marked (N-24).
- ❌ **Quoting Table 4.1 without the residual.** It sums to **3.32** against a stated **3.5** (N-16).
- ❌ **Asserting 180‰ for the Caspian or 250‰ / 240‰ for the Dead Sea.** Self-contradictory and
  unverified (N-04, N-05, U-03).
- ❌ **Treating a sea's salinity as a single number.** The Baltic is 7‰ in one book and 21‰ in the
  other because salinity falls steeply from mouth to head.
- ❌ **Arguing inland saline lakes here.** ↪ `Geography/basic/09_Lakes`.
- ❌ **Supplying an Arabian Sea or Bay of Bengal value.** None exists (U-31).
- ❌ **Saying the ocean is getting saltier because rivers keep adding salt.** Supply is balanced by
  removal; salinity is a steady state.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography. Salinity is one of the two defining
physical properties of sea water and the one that distinguishes it from fresh water entirely.

**Owned demand prepared.** This lesson is the **first half** of the preparation for **O7 — 2024
Prelims GS-I Q89, Red Sea, low precipitation and absence of major river inflow**. It establishes
what salinity *is*; Lesson 6 establishes what *controls* it, which is where the demand actually
lives. ⚠️ **U-24:** the ledger records that a Set-A key is present locally and records no answer
letter; **this edition records none either. Key availability is not key disclosure.**

**Answer use.** Open any salinity answer with the definition and the unit, then the composition, then
the controls. A candidate who writes "salinity is dissolved solids in g/kg, averaging about 35‰, of
which sodium chloride is more than three-quarters" has established competence in one sentence.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Definition (Majid 4.7):** "a measure of **dissolved solids in seawater**, usually expressed in
   **grams per kilogram or parts per thousand by weight**"; standard sea water **35[‰] at 0°C**.
2. ✅ Ocean average: **35‰** (Majid) / **35.2‰** (Leong) / "about 35 ppt" (C1 line 32).
3. ✅ **96.5 % water, 3.5 % dissolved salts** (Majid 4.7).
4. ✅ Table 4.1: NaCl **2.6** · MgCl₂ **0.3** · MgSO₄ **0.2** · CaSO₄ **0.1** · KCl **0.1** · KBr
   **0.01** · other **0.01**.
5. **✗ N-16:** the table sums to **3.32**, leaving **0.18 per cent unaccounted** against the stated
   3.5. Always disclose.
6. **Cross-check that closes:** 2.6 ÷ 3.32 = **78.3 %**, agreeing with ✅ Leong's "NaCl … **more than
   77 per cent**".
7. **✗ N-24:** salinity is **‰ / ppt / g/kg** and **never %**. Majid prints a per-cent sign
   throughout; the substitution is marked.
8. **Isohaline** = a line of equal salinity (✅ Leong printed 86, exercise 5(c) at printed 90).
9. ✅ Three salt pathways: **land runoff** (six agents), **deep-Earth and volcanic**, **decomposed
   organic life**. ⚠️ Removal balances supply; salinity is a **steady state**.
10. ✅ Majid's classes: above normal — **Red Sea ≈39[‰], Persian Gulf 38, Mediterranean 37**; normal
    — Caribbean, Gulf of Mexico, Gulf of California, Yellow Sea at **35-36**; below normal — Arctic,
    Antarctic, Bering, Japan, **Baltic 21[‰]**.
11. ⇄ Baltic: **21[‰]** (Majid) against **7‰** (Leong, twice; C1 follows Leong). Neither asserted.
12. **✗ N-04:** Caspian **180‰** (Leong printed 86) against "often over 37‰" as a class member
    (Leong printed 87). **U-03** unverified; **assert neither**.
13. **✗ N-05:** Dead Sea **250‰** in Leong's text, **240‰** in Leong's own exercise. **Assert
    neither.** Lake Van **330‰** likewise attributed and flagged.
14. ↪ Inland saline lakes — Caspian, Dead Sea, Aral, Sambhar, Didwana, Great Salt Lake, Rann of
    Kachchh — are `Geography/basic/09_Lakes`.
15. **U-31:** no Arabian Sea or Bay of Bengal salinity value exists anywhere in the base.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Ocean salinity averages 35%, of which sodium chloride forms 2.6%,
and the Dead Sea reaches 250%." Identify every defect, and give the corrected statement with its
sourcing.

**Model answer:** There are three defects and one unverifiable claim. **First, the unit sign.**
Salinity is a ratio in **parts per thousand**, written ‰, ppt or g/kg; "35%" would be ten times the
actual value and would make sea water about a third salt by weight. ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.7, prints
a per-cent sign throughout his salinity section, and reproducing it uncorrected imports the error
(N-24). **Second, the mixing of bases.** "2.6" is a **per cent of the whole water**, not of the salt;
expressed as a share of dissolved material it is about **78 per cent**, which is what ✅ Leong,
printed 86, means by "sodium chloride … more than 77 per cent". Quoting 2.6 beside 35 without saying
which base each uses is incoherent. **Third, the missing residual.** Majid's Table 4.1 sums to
**3.32 per cent** against his own stated **3.5 per cent**, leaving **0.18 per cent unaccounted**
(N-16), and any use of the table must disclose it. **Fourth, the Dead Sea figure is unverified and
self-contradictory**: Leong gives **250‰** at printed 86 and **240‰** in his own exercise at printed
90 (N-05, U-03), and the Dead Sea is in any case an inland lake routed to
`Geography/basic/09_Lakes`. Corrected: ocean salinity averages about **35‰** (✅ Majid 4.7) or
**35.2‰** (✅ Leong printed 86), with sodium chloride more than **77 per cent** of the dissolved
material.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a per-mille and a per-cent sign are interchangeable because both
are "parts of a whole". They differ by a factor of ten, and in a 3.5 per cent solution that factor
is the difference between sea water and brine.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 5

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Define ocean salinity, describe its composition and
sources, and comment on the reliability of standard tabulations.

**Model:** ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.7, defines salinity as "a measure of **dissolved solids in
seawater**, usually expressed in **grams per kilogram or parts per thousand**", standard sea water
being **35[‰] at 0°C**, with **96.5 per cent water and 3.5 per cent dissolved salts**. Composition is
dominated by sodium chloride — ✅ Leong, printed 86, puts it at "**more than 77 per cent**" of the
dissolved material, and Majid's Table 4.1 gives **2.6 ÷ 3.32 = 78.3 per cent**, which agrees. Salt
arrives by **land runoff** through rain, rivers, ground water, wind, waves and glaciers; from the
**deeper layers of the Earth** through volcanic and hydrothermal input; and by **organic
decomposition**. ⚠️ Removal by burial, evaporite precipitation and ridge exchange balances supply, so
salinity is a steady state. Tabulations need auditing: Majid's own table sums to **3.32 per cent**
against his stated **3.5**, leaving **0.18 per cent unaccounted**. *(142 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Definition and units **3** · composition and its residual **3** · source
pathways **2** · unit-sign discipline **2**

Progress: 6 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **latitudinal salinity profile against an evaporation-minus-precipitation ribbon**

### The intuition, before any term

Everyone's first guess is that the hottest sea is the saltiest, because heat evaporates water and
evaporation leaves salt behind. The first half of that is right. The second half ignores the other
side of the ledger — **rain**. The equator is the hottest ocean on Earth and it is also the
**rainiest**, and the rain wins. Move twenty-five degrees north or south, into the belt of
descending air where it almost never rains and the sun is still fierce, and nothing is replacing
what evaporates.

**Salinity is a budget, not a temperature.** The controlling quantity is **evaporation minus
precipitation** — E − P — and once you hold that, the entire latitudinal pattern falls out, the Red
Sea becomes obvious, and the commonest error in the topic becomes visible as an error.

### The primary visual — the profile over the budget

**Caption.** Two maxima, three minima, and a ribbon beneath that explains all five. **The salinity
curve is the E − P curve with the sign of the high-latitude term modified by melt and runoff.**

### ✅ Leong's three controls, in his own numbering — the lesson's spine

✅ **Leong, printed 87 (scan PDF p.90)**, gives three numbered controls, and they are the correct
ones:

1. **"The rate of evaporation."** "The waters fringing the High Pressure Belts of the Trade Wind
   Deserts, **between 20° and 30°N. and S.**, have high salinity because of the high rate of
   evaporation caused by **high temperature and low humidity**."
2. **"The amount of fresh water added by precipitation, streams and icebergs."** "Salinity is lower
   than the average 35‰ in equatorial waters because of the **heavy daily rainfall and high relative
   humidity** … The **Baltic, Arctic and Antarctic** waters have a salinity of **less than 32‰**",
   fed by the **Ob, Lena, Yenisey and Mackenzie**.
3. **"The degree of water mixing by currents."** "In wholly or partially enclosed seas such as the
   Caspian Sea, Mediterranean Sea, **Red Sea and Persian Gulf**, the waters do not mix freely …
   **Salinity is high, often over 37‰**."

⚠️ **Note what control 1 actually says, because it is the whole answer.** It does **not** say "where
it is hot". It says where it is **hot *and* dry** — high temperature **and low humidity**, under the
descending limb of the subtropical high-pressure belts. Temperature is one term in the evaporation
rate; humidity is the other; and precipitation is the opposing term. Leong gets all three right.

### ✗ N-02 — the physical-sign error, stated, located, and refuted

**✗ Majid IWG, printed folio 4.7 (scan PDF p.135), writes:**

> "(ii) **Temperature:** There is a **direct relationship between temperature and salinity** of the
> oceans. In general, the higher, the temperature, the higher, the salinity of the oceans. **The
> Torrid Zone has higher salinity as compared to the Frigid Zone.**"

**✗ And on the very next page, printed 4.8 (scan PDF p.136), the same author writes:**

> "**Salinity is low at the surface water in the equatorial water**" … "the oceans of the equatorial
> region, recording the **heavy rainfall throughout the year, have relatively low salinity**."

```text
   ✗ N-02 — THE DEFECT, ITS SELF-CONTRADICTION, AND THE CORRECTION

   THE CLAIM (Majid printed 4.7)
     "higher temperature → higher salinity"; Torrid Zone > Frigid Zone
                    │
                    ▼
   WHAT IT PREDICTS: a salinity MAXIMUM AT THE EQUATOR, where the ocean
   is hottest, falling monotonically to the poles.
                    │
                    ▼
   WHAT IS OBSERVED: a salinity MINIMUM at the equator, TWIN MAXIMA at
   20°-30°N and S, and minima again at high latitude. The predicted curve
   has ONE peak in the wrong place; the real curve has TWO peaks and a dip
   exactly where the claim puts its peak.
                    │
                    ▼
   REFUTED BY ITS OWN AUTHOR, ONE PAGE LATER (Majid printed 4.8):
     "salinity is low at the surface water in the equatorial water"
                    │
                    ▼
   THE CORRECT CONTROL (✅ Leong printed 87):
     EVAPORATION MINUS PRECIPITATION, E − P.
     Temperature enters only as ONE TERM in the evaporation rate, and is
     OUTWEIGHED at the equator by humidity and rainfall.
                    │
                    ▼
   WHY THE CLAIM SURVIVES IN STUDENT ANSWERS: because the Torrid/Frigid
   comparison is TRUE AS STATED — the tropics are saltier than the polar
   seas. The claim is right about the END POINTS and wrong about the
   MECHANISM, and therefore wrong about the MIDDLE. A rule that works at
   the extremes and fails in between is the most dangerous kind.

   ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
   ║  THIS EDITION RECORDS MAJID'S SENTENCE AS A SOURCE DEFECT WITH   ║
   ║  ITS FOLIO AND TEACHES THE E − P CONTROL. THE CLAIM IS NEVER     ║
   ║  ASSERTED, PARAPHRASED APPROVINGLY, OR USED IN ANY MODEL ANSWER. ║
   ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
```

✅ **Majid's own five determinants, printed 4.7, are otherwise sound** and are kept: **evaporation ·
temperature · precipitation · ocean currents · influx of fresh water**, with his supporting
observations — "the highest rate of evaporation is recorded along the **Tropic of Cancer**, especially
in the **Red Sea and the Persian Gulf**"; "the **Gulf Stream and the North Atlantic Drift increase the
salinity in the North Sea** and the North east Atlantic"; and "comparatively low salinity is found
along the mouth of the **Amazon, Congo, and Ganga deltas**". **The list is right; the second item's
explanation is wrong.**

### 📘 O7 worked — the Red Sea, the owned 2024 Prelims demand

**The demand, verbatim from the ledger:** `| 2024 | 89 | Red Sea - low precipitation and absence of
major river inflow | Geography | … | Key available locally (official Set-A answer key present);
answer not recorded here |`

**The capability tested:** the **evaporation-minus-precipitation control on salinity in an enclosed
subtropical sea**. Not a fact about the Red Sea — a mechanism, applied to it.

✅ **Leong, printed 86 (scan PDF p.89), supplies the demand almost verbatim:**

> "**In the Red Sea where there is much surface evaporation and fewer rivers to bring in fresh water,
> the average salinity increases to 39‰.**"

```text
   ┌─ E, the evaporation term ────────────────────────────────────────┐

   │    pressure band at its northern end, under descending air.      │

   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

⚠️ **The discrimination the demand rewards.** Three enclosed subtropical seas are available — Red Sea
≈39‰, Persian Gulf 38‰, Mediterranean 37‰ — and they are ordered. A candidate who can say **why they
are ordered that way** (degree of enclosure, aridity of the surrounding land, presence or absence of
any river) has understood the budget rather than memorised a value. The Mediterranean, note, receives
the Nile, the Po, the Rhône and the Ebro and connects through a wider strait; the Red Sea receives
none and connects through a narrower one.

✅ **C2 §3** is the owner statement being used here: Arabian Sea higher salinity from "high
evaporation, fewer large rivers"; Bay of Bengal lower, "diluted by Ganga-Brahmaputra system and heavy

kind anywhere in its 121 lines.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "The Torrid Zone *is* saltier than the Frigid Zone. So Majid's sentence is true."

**Reply.** It is true as a comparison of two end-points and false as a statement of control, and the
distinction is the whole marks. Compare the tropics with the poles and temperature and E − P happen
to point the same way, because polar seas are both cold **and** freshened by melt and great rivers.
Compare the equator with 25°N — both inside the Torrid Zone — and they point in **opposite**
directions: the equator is hotter and **less** saline. A control that fails inside its own named zone
is not a control. The honest formulation is that temperature **contributes to** evaporation, and
evaporation net of precipitation and runoff determines salinity.

**Unresolved residual.** The Baltic divergence (7‰ against 21[‰]) is unresolved; the enclosed-sea
inland end-members are unverified (**U-03**); and **U-31** leaves the Indian basin contrast
permanently unquantified in this edition.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **"Higher temperature means higher salinity."** The equator refutes it (N-02). The control is
  **E − P**, with runoff and mixing as the third and fourth terms.
- ❌ **Placing the salinity maximum at the equator.** It is a **minimum**; the maxima are at
  **20°-30°N and S**.
- ❌ **Explaining the Red Sea by heat alone.** Four terms push together: high E, negligible P, no
  river inflow, restricted mixing.
- ❌ **Treating the Mediterranean, Persian Gulf and Red Sea as interchangeable.** They are **ordered**
  — 37, 38, ≈39‰ — and the ordering is explained by enclosure and river inflow.
- ❌ **Giving an Arabian Sea or Bay of Bengal number.** None exists (U-31).
- ❌ **Discussing Sambhar, Didwana or the Aral Sea.** ↪ `Geography/basic/09_Lakes`.
- ❌ **Recording an answer, option or elimination for the 2024 demand.** Key availability is not key
  disclosure (U-24).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography (the latitudinal salinity pattern is a
canonical salient feature) and the Prelims GS-I clause.

**Owned demand served — O7, 2024 Prelims GS-I Q89.** This lesson is its home. The capability tested
is the E − P control in an enclosed subtropical sea; ✅ Leong printed 86 supplies the case almost
verbatim. ⚠️ **No answer, option letter, key or elimination chain appears here or anywhere in this
edition.**

**Answer use.** The three-sentence spine that answers any salinity-distribution question: *"Surface
salinity is governed by evaporation minus precipitation, modified by runoff and by the freedom of
mixing. Hence twin maxima under the subtropical high-pressure belts at 20°-30°N and S, where
descending air makes evaporation high and rainfall negligible; an equatorial minimum under the heavy
daily rainfall of the ITCZ despite the highest temperatures; and high-latitude minima from melt and
great-river discharge. Enclosed subtropical basins — the Mediterranean, the Persian Gulf, the Red Sea
— exceed the open-ocean maximum because restricted exchange adds a fourth term."*

### Revision points

1. The control on surface salinity is **evaporation minus precipitation (E − P)**, modified by
   **runoff** and by **freedom of mixing**. Temperature enters only through the evaporation term.
2. ✅ **Leong's three controls, printed 87:** rate of evaporation · fresh water added by
   precipitation, streams and icebergs · degree of water mixing by currents.
3. ✅ **The maximum is SUBTROPICAL**: "waters fringing the **High Pressure Belts of the Trade Wind
   Deserts, between 20° and 30°N. and S.**", because of "high temperature **and low humidity**".
4. ✅ **The equator is a MINIMUM**: below 35‰ because of "**heavy daily rainfall and high relative
   humidity**".
5. ✅ **High latitudes are minima**: Baltic, Arctic and Antarctic "**less than 32‰**", fed by the
   **Ob, Lena, Yenisey and Mackenzie**.
6. **✗ N-02:** Majid printed 4.7's "**direct relationship between temperature and salinity … The
   Torrid Zone has higher salinity as compared to the Frigid Zone**" is a physical-sign error,
   refuted by Majid himself at printed 4.8 and by Leong at printed 87. **Never asserted.**
7. The claim is **right at the end-points and wrong in the middle** — which is why it survives.
8. ✅ Majid's five determinants (printed 4.7) are sound: **evaporation · temperature · precipitation ·
   ocean currents · influx of fresh water**.
9. ✅ Majid: highest evaporation **along the Tropic of Cancer**, especially the **Red Sea and Persian
   Gulf**; the **Gulf Stream and North Atlantic Drift raise North Sea salinity**; low salinity at the
   **Amazon, Congo and Ganga deltas**.
10. **Enclosed subtropical ordering:** Mediterranean **37[‰]** < Persian Gulf **38[‰]** < Red Sea
    **≈39‰**, explained by enclosure, aridity and river inflow.
11. 📘 **O7:** ✅ Leong printed 86 — "In the **Red Sea** where there is **much surface evaporation and
    fewer rivers to bring in fresh water**, the average salinity increases to **39‰**."
12. The Red Sea's four terms: high E (desert shores, 85-100°F water), negligible P (subsidence), nil
    R (no perennial river), restricted M (**Bab-el-Mandeb**).
13. 🇮🇳 **Arabian Sea E − P − R positive → higher; Bay of Bengal negative → lower.** Direction and
    mechanism only; **U-31** forbids any value.
14. **U-24:** the 2024 Set-A key is recorded as locally available; no answer is recorded anywhere.

### Concept check

**Question:** The equatorial ocean is the warmest on Earth and the least saline of the tropical
latitudes. Explain why, and state precisely what this shows about the control on salinity.

**Model answer:** Because salinity is set by a **budget**, not by a temperature. At the equator the
evaporation term is high — the water is warm — but the precipitation term is higher still: ✅ Leong,
printed 87, attributes sub-35‰ equatorial salinity to "**heavy daily rainfall and high relative
humidity**", the second clause being decisive because humid air evaporates less efficiently from a
water surface even when that surface is hot. Net freshwater gain therefore dilutes the surface. Move
to **20°-30°N and S**, into the descending limb of the subtropical high-pressure belts, and the
position reverses: ✅ Leong records that "the waters fringing the **High Pressure Belts of the Trade
Wind Deserts** … have **high salinity** because of the high rate of evaporation caused by **high
temperature and low humidity**". Rainfall is negligible there, so E − P is strongly positive and the
twin maxima form. What this shows is that **temperature is a term inside the evaporation rate, not
the control itself**. It follows that ✗ Majid IWG, printed 4.7 — "there is a direct relationship
between temperature and salinity … The Torrid Zone has higher salinity as compared to the Frigid
Zone" — is a sign error: it is accidentally right about tropics-versus-poles and wrong within the
tropics, where its predicted maximum is in fact a minimum. Majid himself contradicts it at printed
4.8.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a rule verified at two extreme points is thereby established. The
Torrid-versus-Frigid comparison works only because polar seas are simultaneously cold and freshened;
the mechanism being asserted fails inside the Torrid Zone itself.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 6

**Original question (20 marks, 250 words).** "Surface salinity is a budget, not a temperature."
Examine this proposition with reference to the latitudinal distribution of ocean salinity and to
enclosed seas.

**Model:** Surface salinity is governed by **evaporation minus precipitation**, modified by runoff
and by freedom of mixing. The latitudinal curve demonstrates it. ✅ Leong, printed 87, locates the
maxima where "the waters fringing the **High Pressure Belts of the Trade Wind Deserts, between 20°
and 30°N. and S.**, have high salinity because of the high rate of evaporation caused by **high
temperature and low humidity**" — descending air, negligible rain, strongly positive E − P. At the
equator the same source records salinity below the 35‰ average "because of the **heavy daily rainfall
and high relative humidity**", so the **hottest ocean is a salinity minimum**. At high latitude,
Baltic, Arctic and Antarctic waters fall "**less than 32‰**" under melt and the discharge of the
**Ob, Lena, Yenisey and Mackenzie**.

Enclosed seas add a fourth term. ✅ Leong's third control — "the waters do not mix freely" — produces
an ordered set: Mediterranean **37[‰]**, Persian Gulf **38[‰]**, Red Sea **≈39‰** (✅ Majid 4.8). The
Red Sea maximises every term: desert on both shores, negligible rainfall, no perennial river — ✅
Leong, printed 86, "**much surface evaporation and fewer rivers to bring in fresh water**" — and
exchange restricted to Bab-el-Mandeb.

The proposition therefore holds, and refutes ✗ Majid printed 4.7's claim of a "direct relationship
between temperature and salinity", which his own printed 4.8 contradicts. *(212 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Subtropical-maximum reasoning from E−P **7** · equatorial and high-latitude
minima **5** · enclosed-sea case **4** · the Red Sea worked case **3** · refusal of the
direct-temperature claim **1**

Progress: 7 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **temperature-salinity diagram with a water-mass envelope**

### The intuition, before any term

A current is a **verb**. A water mass is a **noun**.

Point at the Gulf Stream and you are pointing at a motion — a transport of water past a place, which
would still be happening if every molecule in it were replaced. Point at Mediterranean Outflow Water
and you are pointing at a **body of water with an identity**: a particular combination of temperature
and salinity, acquired in a particular place, which that body keeps as it spreads thousands of
kilometres and sinks below water that is warmer and fresher. You can **label** a water mass. You can
only **trace** a current.

That distinction is the entire directive of the owned 2019 demand, and C1 supplies only one half of
it. This lesson supplies the other half, and the evidence for it exists in **exactly one passage** in
the whole base.

### The primary visual — the T-S plane and what lives on it

```text
  THE TEMPERATURE-SALINITY DIAGRAM
  ✅ Majid IWG printed 4.8: the T-S diagram is "advocated by HELLAND-HENSEN";
     Fig. 4.8 shows the HALOCLINE, THERMOCLINE and PYCNOCLINE.
  ⚠️ The envelope reading below is standard oceanography; no book plots it.

  TEMPERATURE °C
   30 ┤ ╲       ╲       ╲       ╲       ╲       ╲        ← isopycnals
      │  ╲       ╲   ┌───────┐   ╲       ╲       ╲         (lines of equal
   25 ┤   ╲  LIGHT ╲ │ WARM, │    ╲       ╲       ╲         DENSITY) run
      │    ╲ WATER  ╲│ FRESH │     ╲       ╲       ╲        DOWN-RIGHT:
   20 ┤     ╲        ╲ BAY-OF-╲     ╲       ╲       ╲       warm+fresh = LIGHT
      │      ╲        ╲BENGAL- ╲     ╲       ╲       ╲      cold+salty = DENSE
   15 ┤       ╲        ╲ TYPE   ╲     ╲    ┌──────────┐
      │        ╲        ╲ CAP    ╲     ╲   │ WARM AND │ ← the Mediterranean
   10 ┤         ╲        ╲________╲     ╲  │  SALTY   │   signature: WARM
      │          ╲        ╲         ╲     ╲│  OUTFLOW │   (so not dense by
    5 ┤           ╲        ╲  ┌──────────┐ │   TYPE   │   temperature) but
      │            ╲        ╲ │  COLD,   │ └──────────┘   SO SALTY that it
    0 ┤             ╲        ╲│ FRESHER  │  ╲       ╲     sinks anyway
      │              ╲        │ POLAR    │   ╲       ╲

      └────┬──────┬────┴──────┴──┬───────┴──┬──────┬───────
          32     33      34      35        36     37     38  SALINITY ‰
                                            ▲
                               THE OPEN-OCEAN MEAN, ≈35‰

  ╔═══ HOW TO READ THE PLANE — THREE RULES ════════════════════════════════╗
  ║ 1. DENSITY IS A JOINT FUNCTION. ✅ Majid printed 4.5: "The density of  ║
  ║    water is mainly a function of its SALINITY AND TEMPERATURE."        ║
  ║    Cold RAISES density; salt RAISES density. Either can win.          ║
  ║ 2. A WATER MASS IS A REGION OF THIS PLANE, not a point on a map.       ║
  ║    It is formed at the SURFACE, where the atmosphere sets its T and S, ║
  ║    then SINKS to the depth at which it is neutrally buoyant and        ║
  ║    SPREADS, CARRYING ITS SIGNATURE WITH IT.                            ║
  ║ 3. THEREFORE: two parcels at the SAME DEPTH in the SAME PLACE can      ║
  ║    belong to DIFFERENT water masses, and the same water mass can be    ║
  ║    found at very different depths in different oceans. Depth does not  ║
  ║    identify a water mass; the T-S SIGNATURE does.                      ║
  ╚════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  THE THREE "-CLINES" — ✅ Majid printed 4.8, Fig. 4.8
  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │ THERMOCLINE   │ steep vertical gradient  │ 100-1000 m (Lesson 4)     │
  │               │ of TEMPERATURE           │                           │
  │ HALOCLINE     │ steep vertical gradient  │ depth and SIGN vary by    │
  │               │ of SALINITY              │ region — see ✗ N-03       │
  │ PYCNOCLINE    │ steep vertical gradient  │ the RESULTANT of the two; │
  │               │ of DENSITY               │ THE barrier to mixing     │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘
  ⚠️ The pycnocline is the one that matters physically, because it is density
  that resists vertical motion. A thermocline and a halocline can REINFORCE
  each other (warm+fresh over cold+salty → very strong pycnocline, as in the
  Bay of Bengal) or OPPOSE each other (warm+salty over cold+fresh → weak).
```

**Caption.** Three rules and three gradients. The plane is the lesson: once density is a **joint**
function, a water mass becomes definable, the Mediterranean exchange becomes predictable, and the
Bay of Bengal's cap becomes a density statement rather than a weather observation.

### ✗ N-03 — Majid contradicts himself on vertical salinity inside one page

✗ **Majid IWG, printed folio 4.8 (scan PDF p.136)** writes, on vertical salinity distribution:

> "**no definite trend is found and, therefore, no generalisation can be made**"

— and then, immediately:

> "**(i) The salinity of the oceans increases with increasing depth.**"

**An intra-page contradiction: a refusal to generalise, followed by a generalisation.** ⚠️ **The
correct teaching, and it is the more interesting one:** vertical salinity is **regionally
contingent**, and the halocline's **sign** depends on which surface forcing dominates.

```text

   (a) FRESHWATER-CAPPED BASIN            (b) EVAPORATIVE BASIN
       e.g. BAY OF BENGAL, polar seas         e.g. the RED SEA, subtropical
                                                   enclosed seas
    0 m ░░░░░░░░ LOW salinity                0 m ████████ HIGH salinity
        ░░░░░░░░ (river + rain)                  ████████ (evaporation)
        ────────  ← HALOCLINE: salinity         ────────  ← HALOCLINE: salinity
        ████████     INCREASES downward          ░░░░░░░░     DECREASES downward
        ████████ HIGHER salinity                 ░░░░░░░░ LOWER salinity
        ████████ (ocean water below)             ░░░░░░░░ (ocean water below)

    ⇒ Majid's "(i)" holds HERE          ⇒ Majid's "(i)" FAILS HERE
    ⇒ and it REINFORCES the thermocline ⇒ and it OPPOSES the thermocline,
      (warm+fresh over cold+salty) →       so the pycnocline is WEAKER and
      a VERY STRONG pycnocline             the surface water can SINK —
                                           which is precisely the mechanism
                                           of the Mediterranean outflow below.

   ⇒ THE GENERALISATION MAJID REFUSES AND THEN MAKES IS THE WRONG ONE.
     The defensible generalisation is about the SIGN RULE, not the direction:
     "the halocline's sign follows the sign of the surface freshwater budget."
```

### The owned demand's second term — ✅ the only water-mass evidence in the base

✅ **Leong, printed 88 (scan PDF p.91), factor 3**, is the single passage in the entire evidence base
that describes a two-layer exchange:

> "The salinity of ocean water varies from place to place. **Waters of high salinity are denser than
> waters of low salinity.** Hence waters of low salinity flow on the surface of waters of high
> salinity while waters of high salinity flow at the bottom towards waters of low salinity. For
> example in the Mediterranean region, there is great difference in salinity between the waters of
> the open Atlantic and those of the partially enclosed Mediterranean Sea. **The less saline water of
> the Atlantic flows on the surface into the Mediterranean, and this is compensated for by an outflow
> of denser bottom water from the Mediterranean.**"

```text
  THE MEDITERRANEAN EXCHANGE — TWO ARROWS, TWO DEPTHS, ONE SILL
  ✅ Leong printed 88. The ONLY concrete water-mass evidence in the base.

        ATLANTIC                 ║ STRAIT OF        ║        MEDITERRANEAN
                                 ║ GIBRALTAR (sill) ║
   ────────────────────────────┐ ║                  ║ ┌──────────────────────
   LESS SALINE SURFACE WATER   │ ║ ═══════════►     ║ │  E − P strongly
   ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │ ║   INFLOW,        ║ │  POSITIVE: a dry
   ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │ ║   SURFACE        ║ │  subtropical basin
   ───────────────────────────┐│ ║                  ║ │  loses more water
   ███████████████████████████││ ║ ◄═══════════     ║ │  than it receives
   DENSER MEDITERRANEAN WATER ││ ║   OUTFLOW,       ║ │  → surface becomes
   SPREADING AT DEPTH ████████││ ║   DEEP           ║ │  SALTY and SINKS
   ───────────────────────────┘│ ║                  ║ └──────────────────────
                               │ ║                  ║
   ⇒ WHY THIS IS A WATER MASS AND NOT MERELY A CURRENT:
     the outflowing water is WARM — so it is NOT dense by temperature — but
     it is SO SALTY that it is dense anyway. It therefore sinks to a depth
     where it is neutrally buoyant and SPREADS WESTWARD ACROSS THE ATLANTIC
     AS A RECOGNISABLE WARM, SALTY TONGUE, thousands of kilometres from the
     strait, IDENTIFIABLE BY ITS T-S SIGNATURE ALONE.
   ⇒ The INFLOW is a current: a transport through a strait.
     The OUTFLOW, once it has left the strait, is a WATER MASS: a labelled
     body of water with an acquired identity.
     THE SAME SILL PRODUCES ONE OF EACH. That is the cleanest possible
     answer to "How do they differ?"
```

### The differentiation itself — built for O1

| | **OCEAN CURRENT** | **WATER MASS** |
|---|---|---|
| **What it is** | A **horizontal transport of water** past a place | A **body of water with a characteristic temperature-salinity signature** |
| **What defines it** | **Direction and speed** — a vector field | **T and S** — a position on the T-S plane |
| **What drives it** | ⚠️ Wind stress, Coriolis deflection, pressure gradient, basin shape and friction (surface); density contrast (deep) | ⚠️ **Surface formation**: the atmosphere sets T and S where the water is last in contact with it |
| **Where it is "made"** | Nowhere — it is a motion, continuously driven | At a **specific surface region**, after which it sinks and keeps its label |
| **How you identify it** | By tracking motion — drift bottles, current meters, ADCPs, altimetry | By **measuring T and S** and plotting the point |
| **Dimensionality** | Essentially a **flow along a path** | Essentially a **volume occupying a layer** |
| **Lifetime of identity** | None — replace the water and the current persists | **Long** — the signature persists for years to decades as it spreads and slowly mixes |
| **Book example in this base** | ✅ The Gulf Stream, "35 to 100 miles wide, 2,000 feet deep, three miles an hour" (Leong printed 88) | ✅ Mediterranean outflow water — "an outflow of denser bottom water from the Mediterranean" (Leong printed 88) |
| **Relationship between them** | ⚠️ **Not opposites.** A current can **transport** a water mass; a water mass can **drive** a current by its density contrast. The deep limb of Lesson 13's overturning is a current **made of** identifiable water masses | |

⚠️ **The single sentence that wins the directive:** *a current is defined by its **motion**, a water
mass by its **properties**; the Strait of Gibraltar carries one of each, in opposite directions, at
different depths, simultaneously.*

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the Bay of Bengal's freshwater cap as a density object

✅ **C2 §3** states the contrast and its cause: the Bay of Bengal is "diluted by Ganga-Brahmaputra
system and heavy rainfall", and its "**freshwater cap affects stratification and cyclones**".

```text
  THE BAY OF BENGAL CAP — A DENSITY ARGUMENT, NOT A SALINITY ONE

  SURFACE  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  WARM + FRESH = VERY LIGHT
           ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  ← thermocline and halocline REINFORCE
  ═════════════════════════════════════════  STRONG PYCNOCLINE
           ████████████████████ cooler + saltier = denser
           ████████████████████

  FOUR CONSEQUENCES, ALL MECHANISM, NO NUMBERS (U-31)

  1. VERTICAL MIXING IS SUPPRESSED. The wind must work against a strong
     density step. A given wind stress mixes a SHALLOWER layer here than
     in the Arabian Sea.
  2. THE SURFACE LAYER HEATS EFFICIENTLY. The same solar input is spread
     through LESS water, so the skin warms more for a given forcing —
     and is slower to be cooled by mixing from below.
  3. THEREFORE UPWELLING IS LESS EFFECTIVE EVEN WHERE WIND FORCING EXISTS.
     Water drawn up from below the cap may still be within the warm,
     nutrient-poor surface layer. ⚠️ This is the same "right wind, wrong
     water" failure mode that Lesson 11 derives for El Niño conditions.
  4. ⚠️ MORE OCEAN HEAT REMAINS AVAILABLE AT THE SURFACE.
     ↪ ROUTED AT THIS EXACT POINT: the consequence for cyclone
     intensification is Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements (2024 GS-I Q4,
     sea surface temperature rise and tropical cyclone formation) and
     Disaster-Management/basic/07_Cyclones-Storm-Surge-and-Coastal-
     Preparedness. TOPIC 12 STATES THE OCEAN STRUCTURE AND STOPS.

  ╔═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║ U-31 ENFORCED: NO SALINITY, DENSITY OR MIXED-LAYER-DEPTH VALUE IS   ║
  ║ GIVEN FOR THE BAY OF BENGAL OR THE ARABIAN SEA. C2 SUPPLIES THE     ║
  ║ DIRECTION AND NO QUANTITY; NO OTHER SOURCE SUPPLIES ONE EITHER.     ║
  ╚═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
```

### OPTIONAL ADVANCED ENRICHMENT — the Arabian Sea oxygen minimum zone, as physics only

> ⚠️ **Placement note.** C2 itself declares this block "beyond core books". It is carried here as a
> **short physical-mechanism enrichment** and is **not required for a core answer**. Its ecological
> and biogeochemical content is routed out in full.

⚠️ The **physical** mechanism of an oxygen minimum zone has exactly three terms, and all three are
Lesson 4 and Lesson 7 material:

1. **Supply from above is cut off.** Oxygen enters the ocean only at the surface. A strong pycnocline
   limits how much of it is mixed downward — the same barrier that caps the Bay.
2. **Demand at depth is high.** Where surface productivity is high, sinking organic matter is
   respired at intermediate depth, consuming oxygen. ✅ C2: "monsoon-driven productivity and
   organic-matter decay".
3. **Ventilation is weak.** ✅ C2: the Arabian Sea is a basin whose intermediate water is **poorly
   renewed** — a geometry and circulation property, closed in the north by land (Lesson 1) and
   lacking a high-latitude source region for intermediate water.

⚠️ **Warming adds a fourth term that is pure physics:** warmer water **holds less dissolved gas**, and
a warmer surface **strengthens stratification**, so both the solubility term and the supply term move
the same way. C1 §9.5 states exactly this as the deoxygenation mechanism.

↪ **Routed in full, and this is the sharpest boundary in the lesson.** **Denitrification, anammox,
nitrous oxide (N₂O) emission, the replacement of diatoms by *Noctiluca*, fishery consequences, dead
zones as an ecological phenomenon and the 2018 GS-I Q7 demand on spreading marine dead zones** all
belong to `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy`. C1 carries
a ➕ dead-zone block; **Environment carries the demand**. Topic 12 states the stratification,
solubility and ventilation mechanism and stops. ↪ **Seawater intrusion in coastal aquifers (2025
GS-III Q8)** is the same owner's.

> 🔑 **C2's own trap, kept:** the Arabian Sea OMZ has a strong **natural** oceanographic basis, but
> human nutrient input and warming can modify it. Do **not** present "natural" and "anthropogenic" as
> mutually exclusive.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "A water mass is just deep water with a name. Why does the 2019 examiner want the
distinction?"

**Reply.** Because the distinction decides **what you can infer from a measurement**. If you measure
a velocity you have learned about the forcing — wind, pressure, rotation. If you measure a T-S pair
you have learned about the water's **history** — where it was last at the surface and what the
atmosphere did to it there. The two measurements answer different questions about the same ocean,
and an answer that treats "water mass" as a synonym for "deep current" has collapsed a distinction
the directive *How do they differ* exists to test. It also has a practical edge: water masses are how
oceanographers **trace circulation they cannot observe directly**, which is exactly the problem
Lesson 13 faces with the overturning return limb.

**Comparison — what each source supplies for this demand.**

| Source | Current | Water mass |
|---|---|---|
| C1 | ✓ §1, §2, §4 — drivers, warm/cold rule, gyres | ✗ **never defined** |
| C2 | ✓ §1-§2, the monsoon drifts | ✗ never defined; "stratification" named once |
| ✅ Leong | ✓ printed 88-89, a full named inventory | ✓ **printed 88, factor 3 — the Mediterranean exchange, the only instance** |
| ✅ Majid | ✓ printed 4.9, Table 4.2 | ◐ printed 4.8 — the **T-S diagram** and the three "-clines", but **no water mass is named or defined** |

**Unresolved residual.** No source in the base names a single Indian Ocean water mass. The
differentiation is therefore taught on the Mediterranean case, with the Indian application restricted
to **stratification** — which is what the sources actually support.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating "water mass" as a synonym for "deep current."** One is a body with properties; the
  other is a motion.
- ❌ **Identifying a water mass by its depth.** The T-S signature identifies it; the depth is where it
  happens to be neutrally buoyant, and that varies.
- ❌ **Reproducing "the salinity of the oceans increases with increasing depth" as a rule.** It holds
  in freshwater-capped basins and fails in evaporative ones (N-03).
- ❌ **Assuming dense means cold.** Mediterranean outflow is **warm and dense**, because salinity can
  outweigh temperature.
- ❌ **Writing an OMZ answer about denitrification, N₂O or *Noctiluca*.** ↪
  `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24`.
- ❌ **Arguing cyclone intensification from the Bay's cap.** ↪ `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements`
  and `Disaster-Management/basic/07`.
- ❌ **Giving any Indian basin salinity, density or mixed-layer value.** None exists (U-31).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography, and the geophysical-phenomena clause —
stratification change is one of the five measured changes in the ocean as a critical water body
(C1 §9.5).

**Owned demand served — O1, 2019 Mains GS-I Q17, *How do they differ*, 15 marks, 250 words.** This
lesson supplies the **missing term and the differentiation**; Lesson 12 supplies the **marine-life**
half; the full 250-word model sits in the final arc. The directive is a **differentiation**, so the
model must hold a **two-column discrimination throughout** rather than describing one term and then
the other.

**Answer use.** Define both terms by **what defines them**, not by examples, then give a single
setting that produces one of each — Gibraltar. One worked case beats four names.

### Revision points

1. A **current** is defined by **motion**; a **water mass** is defined by its **temperature-salinity
   signature**.
2. ✅ Density is a **joint** function of temperature and salinity (Majid printed 4.5). Either term can
   dominate.
3. A water mass is **formed at the surface**, sinks to its level of neutral buoyancy, and **spreads
   carrying its signature**.
4. ✅ The **T-S diagram** is "advocated by **Helland-Hensen**" (Majid printed 4.8).
5. ✅ **Thermocline** (temperature), **halocline** (salinity), **pycnocline** (density) — Fig. 4.8.
   The **pycnocline** is the physical barrier.
6. Thermocline and halocline can **reinforce** (Bay of Bengal: warm+fresh over cold+salty) or
   **oppose** each other (Red Sea: warm+salty over cooler+fresher).
7. **✗ N-03:** Majid printed 4.8 writes "no definite trend … no generalisation can be made" and then
   "(i) the salinity of the oceans increases with increasing depth" — **one page, two positions**.
   The defensible rule is that the **halocline's sign follows the surface freshwater budget**.
8. 📘 **O1's only evidence**: ✅ Leong printed 88, factor 3 — "**The less saline water of the Atlantic
   flows on the surface into the Mediterranean, and this is compensated for by an outflow of denser
   bottom water from the Mediterranean.**"
9. The Gibraltar sill carries a **current inward at the surface** and exports a **water mass at
   depth** — one sill, one of each.
10. Mediterranean outflow is **warm and dense**: salinity outweighs temperature.
11. 🇮🇳 The Bay of Bengal's **freshwater cap** gives a strong pycnocline → suppressed mixing, an
    efficiently heated thin surface layer, and less effective upwelling even under wind forcing.
12. ⚠️ OMZ physics, three terms: **cut-off supply from above**, **high demand at depth**, **weak
    ventilation**; warming adds **lower gas solubility** and **stronger stratification**.
13. ↪ All OMZ ecology — denitrification, anammox, N₂O, *Noctiluca*, dead zones, 2018 GS-I Q7 —
    is `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24`.
14. **U-31:** no Indian basin salinity, density or mixed-layer value exists in the base.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Ocean currents and water masses are two names for the same thing." Refute this with
one worked setting.

**Model answer:** They differ in **what defines them**. A current is a **transport** — a direction
and a speed past a place, which persists even though the water in it is continuously replaced; ✅
Leong, printed 88, characterises the Gulf Stream by exactly such quantities, "35 to 100 miles wide,
2,000 feet deep, three miles an hour". A water mass is a **body of water identified by its
temperature-salinity signature**, acquired at the surface where the atmosphere last acted on it, and
retained as it sinks to its level of neutral buoyancy and spreads. The **Strait of Gibraltar produces
one of each simultaneously**. ✅ Leong, printed 88, records that "the less saline water of the
Atlantic flows on the surface into the Mediterranean, and this is compensated for by an **outflow of
denser bottom water from the Mediterranean**." The inflow is a **current**: a transport through a
strait. The outflow, once beyond the sill, is a **water mass**: it is **warm**, so it is not dense by
temperature, but it is so saline that it is dense regardless, and it therefore sinks and spreads
westward as a recognisable warm, salty tongue identifiable by its T-S pair alone, far from any
visible motion. Measuring a velocity tells you about **forcing**; measuring a T-S pair tells you
about **history**. The two answer different questions.

**Misconception to avoid:** That density means cold. Mediterranean outflow is simultaneously warm and
dense because salinity can outweigh temperature — which is also why the deep ocean's circulation is
called **thermohaline** and not merely thermal.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 7

**Original question (20 marks, 250 words).** Distinguish a water mass from an ocean current, and
examine how the density structure of the Indian seas conditions vertical exchange.

**Model:** A **current** is a horizontal transport defined by direction and speed; a **water mass** is
a body of water defined by a characteristic **temperature-salinity signature** acquired at the
surface and retained as it sinks to neutral buoyancy and spreads. ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.5,
establishes the basis: "the density of water is mainly a function of its **salinity and
temperature**", and printed 4.8 supplies the **T-S diagram** "advocated by **Helland-Hensen**" with
its **halocline, thermocline and pycnocline**. The Strait of Gibraltar yields one of each: ✅ Leong,
printed 88, records "the less saline water of the Atlantic flows on the surface into the
Mediterranean, and this is compensated for by an **outflow of denser bottom water**" — an inflowing
current and an outflowing water mass that is **warm yet dense** because salinity outweighs
temperature.

In the Indian seas the density structure is the operative variable. The **Bay of Bengal** receives
the Ganga-Brahmaputra discharge and heavy monsoon rainfall, so its surface layer is warm **and**
fresh: thermocline and halocline reinforce, the pycnocline is strong, and vertical exchange is
suppressed. The **Arabian Sea**, with few large rivers and higher evaporation, mixes more readily.
⚠️ The contrast is a direction and a mechanism only — no salinity value for either basin exists in
any source consulted. The consequence is that identical wind forcing produces unequal mixing, which
conditions nutrient supply, surface heat retention and the effectiveness of upwelling. *(229 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Density from temperature and salinity **6** · water mass defined against
current **5** · T-S reading **4** · Mediterranean two-layer exchange **3** · India stratification case
**2**

Progress: 8 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **force-decomposition vector rosette beside a taxonomy tree**

### The intuition, before any term

Blow across a cup of tea and the surface moves the way you blew. Blow across an ocean and the surface
moves **at an angle** to the way you blew, and the angle changes with depth, and the whole thing
eventually piles water up against a continent until a pressure gradient pushes back. The ocean is a
thin film of fluid on a **rotating** sphere with **walls**, and every one of those three words adds a
force.

The owned 2022 demand begins "What are the forces influencing ocean currents?" It is a list question.
And the first thing to know about the list is that **the three authorities in front of us give three
different lists**.
