**Caption.** Left, the physics on one parcel; right, three authorities' attempts to enumerate that
physics. The divergence is not a defect to be repaired — it is the structure of the answer the 2022
demand wants.

### The forces, stated properly — C1's own process control as the spine

📗 **C1's process control, lines 394-397**, is the most accurate formulation available in any source
here, and is used as the spine: "**wind stress plus Coriolis, pressure gradient, basin shape and
friction organises surface flow; heat and freshwater budgets change density and mixing; coastal
wind-Ekman divergence drives upwelling**."

| Force | What it does | What depends on it later |
|---|---|---|
| **Wind stress** | Transfers momentum from air to water at the surface. ⚠️ **The primary driver of surface circulation**, and the only one with enough energy to organise basin-scale gyres | Lessons 9, 10, 11, 17, 18 |
| **Coriolis deflection** | An apparent deflection arising from the Earth's rotation: **to the right in the Northern Hemisphere, to the left in the Southern**. Zero at the equator, maximum at the poles | Gyre sense (L9, L10); Ekman transport (L11) |
| **Pressure gradient** | Once wind has piled water up — against a coast, or in a gyre centre — the resulting sea-surface slope drives flow down it | Sargasso Sea (L9); geostrophic balance |
| **Density contrast (thermohaline)** | Temperature and salinity set density; density contrasts drive **vertical** and **deep** motion rather than the surface flow | Lessons 7, 13 |
| **Basin shape — coastline and floor** | Continents block, split and steer flow; ridges and sills constrain deep exchange | Lessons 9, 10, 13, 16, 17 |
| **Friction** | Dissipates momentum, couples layer to layer, and is what makes the Ekman spiral **decay** with depth | Lesson 11 |

### ✗ N-20 — the hemisphere-blind sentence, and why it matters

✗ **Leong, printed 88 (scan PDF p.91)**, writes: "**The earth's rotation deflects freely moving
objects, including ocean currents, to the right.**" He then immediately gives the correct split
himself — **clockwise in the north, anti-clockwise in the south**.

⚠️ **The unqualified sentence is not reproduced.** Deflection is **to the right in the Northern
Hemisphere and to the left in the Southern**, and the consequence is the single most testable fact in
the circulation block: **Northern gyres turn clockwise, Southern gyres anti-clockwise**. 📗 C1 lines
22-23 and 82 state it correctly and are followed.

↪ **ROUTE-OUT, and it is the sharpest boundary in this topic.** **The Coriolis force as a named
phenomenon — its definition, its dependence on latitude and speed, its absence at the equator, its
role in atmospheric systems — belongs to `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements`, which owns 2024
Prelims Q14, "Coriolis force characteristics".** Topic 12 uses Coriolis **as a control on current
direction** and does not teach the force. The distinction is exact: *we use the deflection rule; we
do not derive or characterise the force.*

### ✗ N-28 — a numbering defect worth disclosing

✗ Majid IWG, printed 4.9, numbers his causes **1, 2, (unnumbered), 4, 4** — two items numbered 4 and
the Coriolis item left unnumbered. When citing the list, **renumber the final item to five and
disclose the defect**. It is a small thing, but it is exactly the kind of transcription error that
produces a four-item list in a student's notes when the author intended five.

### Why wind comes first — the demonstration, not the assertion

⚠️ Every authority lists wind, but none of them **proves** its primacy. The proof is available, and it
is Indian.

```text
  THE WIND-PRIMACY DEMONSTRATION

  CLAIM: wind is the dominant driver of SURFACE circulation.

  WHAT WOULD FALSIFY IT: if surface currents were primarily density-driven,
  they would be SLOW TO CHANGE, because density fields change slowly —
  heat and salt budgets integrate over seasons to decades.

  THE TEST: find an ocean where the WIND reverses on a short timescale and
  the DENSITY FIELD does not, and see what the current does.

  THE RESULT — ✅ LEONG, scan PDF p.93, VERBATIM:
     "In the North Indian Ocean, there is a COMPLETE REVERSAL of the
      direction of currents between summer and winter, DUE TO THE CHANGES
      OF MONSOON WINDS. In summer from June to October, when the dominant
      wind is the South-West Monsoon, the currents are blown from a
      south-westerly direction as the SOUTH-WEST MONSOON DRIFT. This is
      reversed in winter, beginning from December, when the North-East
      Monsoon blows the currents from the north-east as the NORTH-EAST
      MONSOON DRIFT. The currents of the North Indian Ocean demonstrate
      MOST CONVINCINGLY THE DOMINANT EFFECTS OF WINDS ON THE CIRCULATION
      OF OCEAN CURRENTS."

  ⇒ The basin's temperature and salinity structure does NOT reverse twice
    a year. The WIND does. The CURRENT follows the WIND.
  ⇒ Leong's own word is "DEMONSTRATE" — he is treating it as evidence,
    not as an example. Lesson 17 develops the case in full.
  ⇒ 🇮🇳 INDIA'S OWN OCEAN IS THE WORLD'S PROOF OF THE FIRST ITEM ON THE LIST.
```

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the lesson's India hook *is* the demonstration

This is one of only four lessons in which the India hook is not an application of a world process but
**the evidence for it**. The North Indian Ocean is not an exception that needs explaining away; it is
the controlled experiment that the rest of the world ocean cannot provide, because no other major
basin has a wind field that reverses completely and seasonally.

⚠️ **Boundary, enforced here and again in Lessons 17, 18 and 19.** Topic 12 uses the monsoon **wind
reversal** as the forcing that drives the current reversal. ↪ **The complete Indian monsoon
mechanism — why the wind reverses, onset, withdrawal, break and active spells, rainfall distribution
and any LPA or seasonal outlook — belongs to `Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism` and
`Geography/basic/16_Tropical-Monsoon-and-Marine-Climate`.** C1's own boundary clause at lines 414-416
says so. We take the wind as given and follow the water.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "Surely the right thing to do is pick the best list and teach it. Showing three lists
confuses a candidate."

**Reply.** It would, if the lists were equally good and arbitrarily different. They are not. Each
omission is **diagnostic**. C1 drops **differential heating**, which is the only item that names the
thermal **engine** rather than thermal **properties** — and without an engine the ocean's circulation
has no ultimate energy source other than the wind, which itself is driven by differential heating.
Leong omits **ocean-floor configuration**, which is why his account cannot address deep exchange over
sills — the mechanism Lesson 13 needs. Majid **duplicates** the thermal term, which inflates his list
to five without adding a fifth idea. **A candidate who can say which list is missing what has
understood the physics better than one who has memorised any single list** — and the 2022 demand
asks "What are the forces", which is an invitation to exactly that.

**Unresolved residual.** **U-13:** Leong PDF 91 carries a band of about four illegible lines that
closes the planetary-wind factor. Whatever qualification he attached to it is lost and is **not
reconstructed**.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Presenting any one list as "the" list of forces.** Three authorities, three lists (N-01).
- ❌ **Writing "the earth's rotation deflects currents to the right" without the hemisphere.** Leong's
  own next sentence corrects him (N-20).
- ❌ **Teaching the Coriolis force itself.** ↪ `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` owns **2024
  Prelims Q14**. We use the deflection rule only.
- ❌ **Confusing differential heating with temperature as a property.** One is the engine; the other is
  a density term. Dropping the first is C1's omission.
- ❌ **Treating density as a surface driver.** Density drives **deep and vertical** motion; wind drives
  the surface.
- ❌ **Explaining the monsoon's wind reversal.** ↪ `Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism`. We
  use it; we do not derive it.
- ❌ **Citing Majid's list as four items** because two are numbered 4 (N-28).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography, and the Prelims GS-I clause.

**Owned demands served.** **O2 — 2022 Mains GS-I Q14, first half.** The directive **What are** asks
for the enumeration, and the correct answer names the divergence rather than reciting one list.
**O5 — 2021 Prelims Q58**, "ocean surface temperature patterns trade winds westerlies", is also
served here: the planetary wind belts organise both the temperature pattern of Lesson 4 and the
current pattern of Lessons 9-10. **No key is available locally for O5 and none is inferred.**

**Answer use.** For *What are the forces*, give a **six-term physical list** (wind stress, Coriolis
deflection, pressure gradient, density contrast, basin geometry, friction) and then **one sentence on
the divergence** between standard enumerations. That converts a list question into an analysis
question, which is where the marks above 8 live.

### Revision points

1. 📗 C1's process control (lines 394-397) is the best formulation: **wind stress + Coriolis,
   pressure gradient, basin shape and friction** organise surface flow; **heat and freshwater
   budgets** change density and mixing; **coastal wind-Ekman divergence** drives upwelling.
2. ✅ **Leong's five (printed 87-88):** planetary winds · temperatures · salinity · the earth's
   rotation · land.
3. ✅ **Majid's five (printed 4.9):** differential heating of equatorial and polar oceans · trade and
   anti-trade winds · Coriolis effect · variations in salinity and temperature · configuration of
   the coast line and ocean floor.
4. 📗 **C1's four (lines 12, 70):** winds · Coriolis · salinity and temperature variations ·
   coastline and ocean-floor configuration.
5. **✗ N-01:** C1 **merges** Leong's items 2 and 3 and **drops** Majid's **differential heating**;
   Leong **omits** ocean-floor configuration; Majid **duplicates** the thermal term. **Teach all
   three; present none as "the" list.**
6. **✗ N-28:** Majid's list is mis-numbered **1, 2, (unnumbered), 4, 4**. Renumber to five and
   disclose.
7. **✗ N-20:** deflection is **right in the N hemisphere, left in the S**. Leong's unqualified "to
   the right" is not reproduced; his own next sentence gives the correct split.
8. **Northern gyres clockwise; Southern gyres anti-clockwise** (C1 lines 22-23).
9. **Wind drives the surface; density drives the deep.** Mixing them is the commonest structural
   error in a currents answer.
10. ✅ **The wind-primacy demonstration**, Leong scan PDF p.93: the North Indian Ocean's **complete
    reversal** "demonstrate[s] **most convincingly the dominant effects of winds**".
11. 🇮🇳 India's ocean is the **evidence** for item one, not merely an example of it: the wind reverses
    twice a year and the density field does not.
12. ↪ **The Coriolis force as a phenomenon (2024 Prelims Q14) is `Geography/basic/13`.** We use the
    deflection rule only.
13. ↪ **Why the monsoon wind reverses is `Geography/advanced/16`.** We take the wind as given.
14. **U-13:** Leong PDF 91's illegible band closes the planetary-wind factor; nothing is
    reconstructed from it.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate lists "winds, Coriolis effect, temperature and salinity, and coastline
configuration" as the forces influencing ocean currents, citing a standard text. What is missing, and
how should the answer be reframed?

**Model answer:** The list is 📗 C1's four-item set, and it is defensible but incomplete in two ways.
**Physically**, it omits the **pressure gradient** that develops once wind has piled water up, and
**friction**, which couples layer to layer and makes the Ekman spiral decay; C1's own process control
at lines 394-397 names both. **Bibliographically**, it is one of three non-identical lists. ✅ Leong,
printed 87-88, gives **five**: planetary winds, temperatures, salinity, the earth's rotation and
land. ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.9, gives a different **five**: differential heating of the equatorial
and polar oceans, trade and anti-trade winds, the Coriolis effect, variations in salinity and
temperature, and configuration of the coast line and ocean floor — mis-numbered 1, 2, unnumbered, 4,
4. C1's four **merges** Leong's separate temperature and salinity items and **drops Majid's
differential heating**, which is the only item naming the thermal **engine** as opposed to thermal
**properties**. The reframing is to give the physical set — wind stress, Coriolis deflection,
pressure gradient, density contrast, basin geometry, friction — and then to **name the divergence**
between standard enumerations. Since the owned 2022 demand's directive is *What are*, identifying
that the authorities disagree is the discriminating move, not a digression.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a list question has a single correct list. Here the three standard
enumerations differ by inclusion, merger and duplication, and the differences are diagnostic of what
each author thought a "force" was.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 8

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** "The forces that move the ocean are agreed in physics and
disputed in enumeration." Discuss, and demonstrate the primacy of wind with an example.

**Model:** Physically the set is settled. 📗 The controlling owner's process clause gives **wind stress
plus Coriolis, pressure gradient, basin shape and friction** for surface flow, with **heat and
freshwater budgets** changing density and driving deep and vertical motion. Wind transfers momentum;
rotation deflects it **to the right in the Northern Hemisphere and to the left in the Southern**, so
gyres turn clockwise in the north and anti-clockwise in the south; piled-up water generates a
pressure gradient; continents block and steer; friction dissipates and couples.

Enumeration is disputed. ✅ Leong, printed 87-88, lists **planetary winds, temperatures, salinity,
the earth's rotation, land**. ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.9, lists **differential heating of the
equatorial and polar oceans, trade and anti-trade winds, the Coriolis effect, variations in salinity
and temperature, configuration of the coast line and ocean floor** — mis-numbered 1, 2, unnumbered,
4, 4. The Core owner gives **four**, merging temperature with salinity and dropping differential
heating, the only term naming the thermal engine. Each omission is diagnostic.

Wind's primacy is demonstrable rather than assertable. ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93, records that "in the
North Indian Ocean, there is a **complete reversal** of the direction of currents between summer and
winter, **due to the changes of monsoon winds**", concluding that these currents "demonstrate **most
convincingly the dominant effects of winds**". The density field does not reverse twice a year; the
wind does, and the current follows. *(228 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Force inventory with reasons **6** · taxonomy divergence named **4** ·
Coriolis hemispheric correctness **3** · wind-primacy demonstration **2**

## Lesson 9 — The Atlantic circuit and what a current does to a coast

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 9 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : C1 §2 is a FIVE-ROW TABLE with NO NAMED CURRENT INVENTORY — it
                   gives the warm/cold rule, the gyre-sense rule and four example
                   names (Gulf Stream, Kuroshio, Labrador, Peru, Benguela) and
                   stops. Neither owner traces a single circuit.
📕 Book evidence : Leong printed 88 (scan PDF p.91) — the Atlantic circulation,
                   Gulf Stream dimensions, the TWO-THIRDS return-limb sentence ·
                   Leong printed 89 (scan PDF p.92) — Fig. 95, the chapter's only
                   world map, and the Sargasso, Irminger, Labrador and Benguela
                   paragraphs · Majid IWG Table 4.2, printed 4.11-4.12, READ FROM A
                   PAGE RENDER because the text layer separates every current name
                   from its effect cell (U-15)
🔍 Live check    : none owned by this lesson
📰 CA anchor     : none owned by this lesson
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/14_Climate-Classification-Koppen and
                   Geography/basic/25_Arctic-or-Polar-Climate (coastal deserts and
                   maritime coasts AS CLIMATIC REGIONS)
```

Progress: 9 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **Atlantic circuit plan with warm and cold limb coding**

### The intuition, before any term

Two coasts at the same latitude, one ice-free all winter and one frozen; one with a rainforest and
one with a desert. Latitude cannot explain the difference, because latitude is the same. What differs
is **which way the water beside them is going** — and the ocean's answer to Lesson 8's forces is a
closed loop per basin, with a **warm limb on one side and a cold limb on the other**.

The Atlantic is the place to learn it, because it is the smallest of the three great basins, the most
completely named, and the one where the consequences are sharpest. Learn the Atlantic properly and
the Pacific is the same logic at greater scale.

**Caption.** One basin, two counter-rotating gyres, and a systematic asymmetry: **west warm, east
cold**, in both hemispheres. That asymmetry is the whole of coastal climatology in one line.

### ✗ N-21 — the same quantity, two units, three paragraphs apart

✗ **Leong, printed 88 (scan PDF p.91)**, gives the Gulf Stream Drift at "**three miles an hour**" and
then, three paragraphs later, the North Atlantic Drift at "**10 miles per day**", with **no
reconciliation between the two units**.

```text
   UNIT RECONCILIATION — PERFORMED HERE, DISCLOSED AS DERIVED

   Gulf Stream       3 miles per hour  × 24 h =  72 miles per day
                                                 ≈ 116 km per day ≈ 1.34 m/s
   North Atlantic Drift                         10 miles per day
                                                 ≈ 16 km per day ≈ 0.19 m/s
   ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   RATIO  72 ÷ 10 = 7.2      The Gulf Stream figure is 7.2× the Drift figure.

   ⚠️ BOTH FIGURES ARE LEONG'S AND NEITHER IS CORRECTED. The conversion and
   the ratio are PERFORMED HERE and are labelled as derived. What the pair
   shows is REAL and important: a WESTERN BOUNDARY CURRENT is narrow, deep
   and FAST; an open-ocean DRIFT is broad, shallow and SLOW. The same water
   is transported by both.
   ⇒ ✅ Leong's own dimensions make the point: "35 to 100 miles wide, 2,000
     feet deep" — that is ≈56-161 km wide and ≈610 m deep. A gyre's
     return flow has no such confinement, so it is correspondingly slower.
```

### ✗ N-07 — the Irminger / East Greenland conflation

✗ **Leong, printed folio 89 (scan PDF p.92)**, writes "the **Irminger Current or East Greenland
Current**", treating the two names as one cold current. ⚠️ **They denote different flows on opposite
sides of the Denmark Strait.** The equation is **not reproduced**; in **M1** they are drawn
**separately**, and in any answer either they are named separately or the Irminger is omitted.

### ✗ N-08 — the Benguela-Kalahari claim, settled by the repository's own map bank

✗ **Majid IWG, printed folio 4.11**, Table 4.2, records the Benguela Current as having "**Helped in
the development of Namibian and Kalahari deserts**".

The repository's own `GEOGRAPHY-MAP-LOCATION-BANK.md` settles this at **line 271**, which carries the
row **"Namib Desert · Namibia-Angola coast · Cold-current coastal desert · Atlantic coast · 'Kalahari
is inland'"**, and at **line 459**, which carries the trap pair **`Namib` versus `Kalahari`**.

⚠️ **The correction, with its mechanism.** A cold eastern-boundary current produces aridity by a
**coastal** mechanism with a **short reach**: cold water chills the overlying air from below, creating
a surface inversion that suppresses convection, so moisture condenses as **fog rather than rain** and
the arid strip hugs the shore. The **Namib** is that strip. The **Kalahari** is an **interior
semi-arid basin**, hundreds of kilometres inland, whose dryness is a continental-interior and
rain-shadow matter, not a current effect. **The bank is honoured and is not edited.**

**✗ The same defect in a worse form.** ✗ Majid's Canaries row claims the current "**Helped in the
genesis of Sahara Desert**". This overstates a coastal fog strip into the cause of a **continental**
desert of some nine million square kilometres whose aridity is produced by the subtropical
high-pressure belt. **Recorded as a source defect; not reproduced.**

**✗ N-30** — the same table prints "**Arizona and Sonara deserts**"; the correct spelling is
**Sonoran**. Recorded.

### What a warm limb and a cold limb actually do — the mechanism and its limits

```text
   WARM LIMB (western side of a subtropical basin, poleward-flowing)
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   warm water → air above it is warmed FROM BELOW → unstable, convective
   → moisture evaporates readily → onshore winds arrive WARM AND MOIST
   ⇒ MILD WINTERS at high latitude; ICE-FREE PORTS; higher precipitation
   ✅ Majid Table 4.2: the North Atlantic Drift "keeps the ports of
      Norwegian Sea and Barents Sea like MURMANSK … situated at higher
      latitudes ice-free throughout the year."
   ⚠️ NOT REPRODUCED: the same cell also names SEVERODVINSK. It stands on
      the White Sea and is not ice-free year-round; MURMANSK is the
      standard case and is the only one used here.
   ✅ Leong printed 87 gives the contrast directly: the LABRADOR CURRENT
      leaves eastern Canadian ports ICE-BOUND AT 45°N — the same latitude
      band in which the Drift keeps European ports open.

   COLD LIMB (eastern side of a subtropical basin, equatorward-flowing)
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   cold water → air above it is COOLED FROM BELOW → a surface INVERSION
   → convection suppressed → moisture condenses as FOG, not rain
   ⇒ COOL, FOGGY, ARID COASTS with a NARROW inland reach
   ✅ Majid, determinant 6 (printed 4.5): "the coast along with a cold water
      current flows record MORE FOG, but LESS PRECIPITATION."
   ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93, on the Humboldt: "The cold water CHILLS ANY WIND
      that blows on-shore so that the Chilean and Peruvian coasts are
      PRACTICALLY RAINLESS."

   ╔═══ THE LIMIT, STATED EXPLICITLY ════════════════════════════════════╗
   ║ 📗 C1 §11.3 limitation clause: "currents are ONE OF SEVERAL          ║
   ║ controls; CONTINENTALITY, RELIEF and PREVAILING WIND DIRECTION      ║
   ║ operate simultaneously."                                            ║
   ║ ⇒ A current explains a COASTAL STRIP. It does not explain a         ║
   ║   CONTINENT. That is exactly the error of N-08 and the Canaries     ║
   ║   claim, and it is the discrimination the examiner is testing.      ║
   ╚═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
```

### 📗 C1's third evidence unit, used here in structure

📗 **C1 §11.3** supplies a Claim / Evidence / Significance / Limitation unit that this lesson is built
to deliver:

> **Claim:** ocean currents redistribute heat and thereby set climates far from the tropics.
> **Evidence:** warm poleward flow on western margins of the higher middle latitudes keeps those
> coasts ice-free and mild in winter, while cold equatorward flow on the opposite side produces cool,
> foggy, arid coasts. **Significance:** it explains why places at identical latitude can have wholly
> different climates — the central comparative fact in regional climatology. **Limitation:** currents
> are one of several controls; continentality, relief and prevailing wind direction operate
> simultaneously.

⚠️ **The structure is reused; the wording is not.** The four-part shape — claim, named evidence,
significance, qualification — is the answer-writing pattern this entire edition uses.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why India's west coast is wet where Namibia's and Peru's are desert

```text

   ┌──────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────┬────────────────┐
   │              │ NAMIBIA ~20°S   │ PERU ~15°S      │ INDIA'S WEST   │
   │              │                 │                 │ COAST ~15°N    │
   ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ Position in  │ EASTERN side of │ EASTERN side of │ NO ENCLOSING   │
   │ its basin    │ the S Atlantic  │ the S Pacific   │ GYRE AT ALL    │
   ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ Current      │ BENGUELA, cold, │ HUMBOLDT /      │ A WIND-DRIVEN  │
   │ beside it    │ equatorward     │ PERUVIAN, cold, │ DRIFT THAT     │
   │              │                 │ equatorward     │ REVERSES TWICE │
   │              │                 │                 │ A YEAR         │
   ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ Air over the │ COOLED from     │ COOLED from     │ WARMED; and in │
   │ water        │ below →         │ below →         │ the SW monsoon │
   │              │ inversion       │ inversion       │ forced to rise │
   │              │                 │                 │ over the       │
   │              │                 │                 │ WESTERN GHATS  │
   ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ Result       │ NAMIB — fog,    │ "PRACTICALLY    │ Among the      │
   │              │ almost no rain  │ RAINLESS"       │ wettest coasts │
   │              │ (✅ map bank     │ (✅ Leong PDF 93)│ on Earth       │
   │              │  line 271)      │                 │                │
   └──────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────┴────────────────┘

   ⇒ 🇮🇳 THE STRUCTURAL REASON: India's west coast is NOT the eastern margin
     of a closed subtropical gyre. The North Indian Ocean has NO FIXED GYRE
     (Lesson 17), because the basin is CLOSED IN THE NORTH BY LAND (Lesson 1)
     and its winds REVERSE (Lesson 8). There is therefore NO PERSISTENT COLD
     EQUATORWARD EASTERN-BOUNDARY CURRENT anywhere on India's coast —
     AND HENCE NO COLD-CURRENT COASTAL DESERT ANYWHERE IN INDIA.
   ⇒ India's one great desert, the THAR, is NOT a coastal desert and NOT a
     current effect. ↪ Geography/basic/07_Arid-Desert-Landforms owns it.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "Naming twenty currents is memory work, not geography."

**Reply.** It would be, if the names were the point. They are not: the **pattern** is. Every
subtropical gyre has a **narrow, deep, fast western boundary current** carrying heat poleward and a
**broad, slow, cold eastern limb** returning equatorward, and the entire comparative climatology of
coasts follows from that one asymmetry. A candidate who knows the asymmetry can reconstruct the names
basin by basin; a candidate who knows only the names cannot explain a single coast. Note too that the
names themselves carry information — **three of this topic's eight taught conflicts are naming
disputes** (N-06, N-07, N-34), which means the inventory is not as settled as a list implies.

**Unresolved residual.** **U-13:** Leong PDF 90 loses about six lines that described **how warm
currents warm their adjacent coasts** — the mechanism sentence itself — and PDF 92 loses about five
lines before the South Atlantic paragraph. The mechanism above is therefore ⚠️ standard physical
geography, stated as such, and **nothing is reconstructed from the illegible bands**.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Attributing the Kalahari to the Benguela Current.** The **Namib** is the cold-current coastal
  desert; the Kalahari is an interior basin (N-08, map bank line 271).
- ❌ **Attributing the Sahara to the Canaries Current.** A coastal fog strip is not the genesis of a
  continental desert.
- ❌ **Equating the Irminger and East Greenland Currents.** Different flows, opposite sides of the
  Denmark Strait (N-07).
- ❌ **Naming Severodvinsk as an ice-free Barents port.** It stands on the White Sea; **Murmansk** is
  the standard case.
- ❌ **Quoting Leong's Gulf Stream and Drift speeds without converting.** 3 mph = 72 miles/day,
  7.2× the Drift figure (N-21); the conversion is derived, not cited.
- ❌ **Explaining a whole climatic region by a current.** 📗 C1's own limitation clause forbids it.
  ↪ Climatic regions are `Geography/basic/14_Climate-Classification-Koppen`.
- ❌ **Writing "Sonara".** It is **Sonoran** (N-30).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography — the circulation of the Atlantic is the
canonical case.

**Owned demands served.** **O2 (2022 GS-I Q14)**: the **confluence** fishing mechanism of Lesson 12 is
located here, at the Gulf Stream-Labrador meeting over the **Grand Banks**. **O5 (2021 Prelims Q58)**:
the planetary wind belts organise this entire circuit — trades drive the equatorial limbs, westerlies
drive the poleward ones. **No key is available for O5 and none is inferred.**

**Answer use.** Trace the circuit as a **loop with a cause at each turn** — trades push west, the
continent deflects poleward, westerlies push east, the continent deflects equatorward — rather than
as a list of names. Then attach **one consequence per limb** and **one limitation to the whole**.

### Revision points

1. **North Atlantic gyre clockwise; South Atlantic gyre anti-clockwise** (📗 C1 lines 22-23).
2. **In both hemispheres the western limb is warm and poleward; the eastern limb is cold and
   equatorward.** Reverse the sense, keep the asymmetry.
3. North Atlantic named sequence: **North Equatorial → Cayenne → Florida → Gulf Stream → North
   Atlantic Drift → Irminger / East Greenland → Labrador → Canaries**, enclosing the **Sargasso Sea**.
4. South Atlantic sequence: **South Equatorial → Brazilian → South Atlantic Current / West Wind Drift
   → Benguela**, with the **Falkland** on the Patagonian side and the **Equatorial Counter Current**
   returning eastward in the trade-wind gap.
5. ✅ **Gulf Stream dimensions (Leong printed 88): "35 to 100 miles wide, 2,000 feet deep, three
   miles an hour"** ≈ 56-161 km wide, ≈610 m deep, ≈1.34 m/s. Deflected at **Cape Hatteras, 35°N**.
6. ✅ **North Atlantic Drift: "10 miles per day"** ≈ 0.19 m/s. **✗ N-21: 3 mph = 72 miles/day = 7.2×**
   — conversion derived here, both figures Leong's, neither corrected.
7. ✅ **Cape Sao Roque** splits the South Equatorial Current into the **Cayenne** (north-west) and the
   **Brazilian** (south-west) — Leong's worked example of basin geometry (C1's fourth control made
   concrete).
8. ✅ The **Labrador Current** reaches **50°N**, "where its icebergs melt", and leaves eastern Canadian
   ports **ice-bound at 45°N**; the **North Atlantic Drift** keeps **Murmansk** open at far higher
   latitude.
9. ✅ The **Benguela** is "the counterpart of the **Canaries** Current" (Leong printed 89).
10. **✗ N-07:** Irminger ≠ East Greenland. Drawn separately in M1.
11. **✗ N-08:** the **Namib** is the cold-current coastal desert; the **Kalahari is inland** (map bank
    line 271, honoured and not edited). Majid's Benguela-Kalahari claim is not reproduced.
12. **✗ The Canaries-Sahara genesis claim** overstates a coastal strip into a continental cause; not
    reproduced. **✗ N-30:** "Sonara" → **Sonoran**.
13. **Warm limb mechanism:** warmed from below → unstable, moist, mild, ice-free. **Cold limb
    mechanism:** cooled from below → inversion → fog, not rain → narrow arid strip.
14. 📗 **The limitation clause:** currents are one control among continentality, relief and prevailing
    wind direction. A current explains a **strip**, not a continent.
15. 🇮🇳 India's west coast has **no persistent cold eastern-boundary current** because the North Indian
    Ocean has **no fixed gyre** — hence **no cold-current coastal desert in India**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Murmansk lies far north of the Canadian ports of the Gulf of St Lawrence yet stays
ice-free while they freeze. Explain, and state the limit of the explanation.

**Model answer:** Because the two coasts sit on **opposite limbs of the North Atlantic gyre**. The
gyre turns clockwise, so its **western** side carries a warm poleward flow and its **eastern** side a
cold equatorward one. On the eastern limb the **North Atlantic Drift** — the continuation of the Gulf
Stream, which ✅ Leong, printed 88, measures at "**10 miles per day**" — carries warm water to very
high latitude; ✅ Majid IWG, Table 4.2 at printed 4.11, records that it "keeps the ports of Norwegian
Sea and Barents Sea like **Murmansk** … situated at higher latitudes **ice-free throughout the
year**". On the western side the **Labrador Current** brings cold polar water and ice south; ✅ Leong,
printed 87, notes that it leaves eastern Canadian ports **ice-bound at 45°N** and that it reaches
**50°N** before its icebergs melt. The air above each adopts the temperature of the water beneath it,
so the thermal contrast is transmitted to the coast. **The limit** is set by 📗 the Core owner's own
qualification: currents are **one of several controls**, and continentality, relief and prevailing
wind direction operate simultaneously. Murmansk also benefits from onshore westerlies that advect the
maritime air inland; a comparable current with offshore winds would warm a far narrower strip. A
current explains a coastal zone, not a region.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a current's thermal effect extends deep inland. The Benguela
produces the **Namib**, a coastal strip; the **Kalahari** is an interior basin and is not a
current effect, as the repository's own map bank records at line 271.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 9

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Trace the circulation of the Atlantic Ocean and examine
how its limbs produce contrasting climates at identical latitudes.

**Model:** The Atlantic carries two counter-rotating subtropical gyres. In the north, trade winds
drive the **North Equatorial Current** westward; the **Cayenne** and **Florida** Currents feed the
**Gulf Stream**, which ✅ Leong, printed 88, measures at "**35 to 100 miles wide, 2,000 feet deep,
three miles an hour**" and which is deflected at **Cape Hatteras (35°N)**; the westerlies carry it on
as the **North Atlantic Drift** at "**10 miles per day**"; and the **Canaries Current** returns cold
water equatorward, closing a **clockwise** gyre around the **Sargasso Sea**. The cold **Labrador**
Current enters from the north-west, reaching **50°N** where "its icebergs melt". In the south, the
**South Equatorial Current** is split at **Cape Sao Roque** into the Cayenne and the **Brazilian**;
the **West Wind Drift** carries it east; the **Benguela** returns equatorward, closing an
**anti-clockwise** gyre.

The climatic consequence follows from a single asymmetry: **the western limb is warm and poleward,
the eastern limb cold and equatorward, in both hemispheres**. Hence ✅ Majid's Table 4.2 records the
Drift keeping **Murmansk** ice-free, while ✅ Leong records eastern Canadian ports **ice-bound at
45°N**. On the eastern limbs, cold water chills the overlying air into an inversion, producing fog
rather than rain and the **Namib** coastal desert — though the repository's map bank records that
"**Kalahari is inland**", so the effect is coastal only.

📗 The qualification is binding: currents act alongside continentality, relief and wind direction.
*(226 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Atlantic circuit traced **5** · warm and cold limb logic **4** · coastal
climate consequence **4** · Sargasso closure **2**

## Lesson 10 — The Pacific and Southern circuits, and what a current is called

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 10 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : C1 §2 names Kuroshio and Peru as examples and supplies no circuit.
                   Neither owner names a single Southern Ocean current, and neither
                   names the West Wind Drift or the Antarctic Circumpolar Current.
📕 Book evidence : Leong scan PDF p.93 — the Pacific circulation, the Humboldt
```

Progress: 10 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **Pacific and Southern circuit plan with a naming-dispute margin**

### The intuition, before any term

The Pacific runs the same programme as the Atlantic on a larger machine: trades west, western
boundary current poleward, westerlies east, eastern limb equatorward. What is genuinely different is
the **south**. Below about 40°S there is no continent in the way, anywhere, for the whole
circumference of the planet. The westerlies therefore drive an **unobstructed eastward flow right
around Antarctica** — the one current on Earth that closes on itself rather than on a coast.

And the Pacific is where the **naming** falls apart. The same water, in two standard books, carries
two names and in one case two opposite temperatures.

### ⇄ Conflict 5 of 8 — the Alaska Current is cold in one book and warm in the other

This is the sharpest inter-book conflict in the topic, because it is a **sign conflict on a named
current**, not a divergence of measurement.

| Source | Statement | Location |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Leong | "The **cold Bering Current or Alaskan Current** creeps southwards from the narrow Bering Strait and is joined by the Okhotsk Current to meet the warm Japan Current as the **Oyashio**, off Hokkaido." | scan PDF p.93 |
| ✅ Majid IWG | "**Alaska Current — Warm — Keeps the seaports of Alaska open throughout the year.**" | printed 4.11, Table 4.2 |

```text
   ⇄ N-06 — THE CONFLICT, AND WHY IT IS RESOLVABLE WITHOUT CHOOSING

   INTERNAL EVIDENCE FROM LEONG HIMSELF, TWO SENTENCES EARLIER (PDF 93):
   he attributes the ICE-FREE ALASKAN PORTS to the NORTH PACIFIC DRIFT.

   But "keeping Alaskan seaports open" is EXACTLY the function Majid assigns
   to the ALASKA CURRENT. The two books are therefore describing the SAME
   PHYSICAL EFFECT and disagreeing only about WHICH NAME CARRIES IT.

   ⇒ WHAT THIS EDITION TEACHES:
       • the BERING – KAMCHATKA – OYASHIO limb is the COLD flow, entering
         from the Bering Strait and meeting the Kuroshio off Hokkaido;
       • the ALASKA CURRENT is the WARM limb, the northward continuation
         of the North Pacific Drift around the Gulf of Alaska;
       • and THE CONFLICT IS SHOWN, with both sources, rather than
         silently adjudicated.
   ⇒ WHY NOT JUST PICK ONE? Because §10.3's conflict rule governs: where two
     books disagree and NO OFFICIAL SOURCE SETTLES IT, the conflict IS the
     finding. No primary source on Alaskan current nomenclature was retrieved.
   ⇒ WHY IT MATTERS FOR MARKS: "Alaska Current" appears in objective-question
     lists of warm and cold currents. A candidate who knows that two standard
     Indian-market texts place it on opposite sides of that list knows
     something more useful than either answer.
```

### ⇄ Conflict 6 of 8 — Agulhas or Mozambique, one current or two?

✅ **Leong, Fig. 95 on printed folio 89**, labels a **single** feature on the map: "**Agulhas or
Mozambique Current**". ✅ **Majid IWG, printed 4.11-4.12, Table 4.2**, tabulates them as **two
separate rows**, both warm, in the Indian Ocean. ⚠️ **In M1 and M4 they are drawn separately and the
divergence is recorded** (N-34). The point recurs in Lesson 17, where **✗ N-33** records that Majid's
entire Indian Ocean row lists **only three currents — Mozambique (warm), Agulhas (warm), West
Australian (cold) — and no monsoon drift whatever**, and therefore may not be presented as a complete
inventory.

⚠️ **Leong's description is therefore a phenomenon in search of a mechanism**, and Lesson 11 supplies
the mechanism. Taught in that order, the Ekman derivation lands on a case the student already finds
puzzling, which is the correct pedagogical sequence and also the correct examination sequence: the
owned 2022 demand asks for the **mechanism**, and a candidate who can only narrate guano has
described Leong's paragraph rather than answered the question.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why India has no cold eastern-boundary current, and what it does have

```text
   THE WORLD'S FOUR GREAT COLD-CURRENT COASTS, AND INDIA'S ABSENCE

   CANARIES  → NW Africa  ✗ Majid's "genesis of Sahara" claim NOT reproduced
   BENGUELA  → Namibia    → the NAMIB (map bank line 271: "Kalahari is inland")
   CALIFORNIAN→ W N America → ✅ Majid: "Arizona and SONORAN deserts" (N-30)
   HUMBOLDT  → Peru/Chile → ✅ Leong: "PRACTICALLY RAINLESS"
   W. AUSTRALIAN → W Australia → ✅ Majid: "West Australian Desert"

   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   INDIA:  NONE.  And the absence is STRUCTURAL, not accidental.

   A cold eastern-boundary current requires a CLOSED SUBTROPICAL GYRE,
   which requires a basin OPEN AT BOTH ENDS of the latitude band.
   The North Indian Ocean is CLOSED IN THE NORTH BY ASIA (Lesson 1), so
   no gyre can close, so there is no persistent eastern limb (Lesson 17).
   ⇒ NO COLD-CURRENT COASTAL DESERT ANYWHERE IN INDIA.
   ⇒ India's aridity is CONTINENTAL and RAIN-SHADOW in origin.
     ↪ Geography/basic/07_Arid-Desert-Landforms owns the Thar.

   WHAT INDIA HAS INSTEAD: SEASONAL, WIND-REVERSING COASTAL CURRENTS —
   the EAST INDIA COASTAL CURRENT and the WEST INDIA COASTAL CURRENT,
   which reverse with the monsoon, and SEASONAL UPWELLING cells off
   SOMALIA and the SOUTH-WEST COAST (Lessons 11 and 17). India gets the
   PRODUCTIVITY of an eastern-boundary system for PART OF THE YEAR and
   none of the ARIDITY, because the forcing is seasonal and the current
   is not cold year-round.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison — the Southern Ocean limb against a subtropical gyre limb.**

| | **West Wind Drift / Antarctic Circumpolar Current** | **A subtropical gyre limb** |
|---|---|---|
| Closes on | **Itself** — it encircles the globe | A **continent** |
| Driven by | Unobstructed **westerlies** over an unobstructed ocean | Trades or westerlies, bounded |
| Boundary effect | None in longitude; it has no western boundary current | Western intensification: narrow, fast, deep |
| Role in the system | **Feeds the cold eastern limb of all three Southern Hemisphere basins** — the Benguela, the Peruvian and the West Australian | Returns water within one basin |
| Named in the local books | ✅ Leong Fig. 95 labels "West Wind Drift"; **neither owner names it at all** | Both books name several |
| Named in the repository map bank | **Absent**, along with every other named current | Absent |

**Objection.** "If the books disagree on names, should a candidate just avoid naming currents?"

**Reply.** No — the opposite. The disputes are **confined and enumerable**: three of them, all
identified here (N-06 Alaska, N-07 Irminger/East Greenland, N-34 Agulhas/Mozambique). Everything else
in the inventory is agreed between the two books and with Leong's Fig. 95. A candidate should name
currents confidently and, **where one of these three arises, say that the standard texts differ**.
That is a demonstration of source awareness, which is precisely what a Mains-focused edition is for.

**Unresolved residual.** **U-14:** the left margin of Leong PDF 93's lower-left column is physically
clipped, so the opening words of several lines of "The Indian Ocean Circulation" are lost. The
surviving text is sufficient for the South Indian circuit and for the complete monsoon-reversal
passage used in Lessons 8 and 17, **and the clipped words are not reconstructed**.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Asserting the Alaska Current as cold, or as warm, without the conflict.** Two standard texts
  place it on opposite sides (N-06).
- ❌ **Treating Agulhas and Mozambique as settled.** One feature in Leong's map, two rows in Majid's
  table (N-34).
- ❌ **Presenting Majid's Indian Ocean row as complete.** It lists three currents and **no monsoon
  drift** (N-33).
- ❌ **Narrating the Peruvian guano chain as an answer about fisheries.** It is a phenomenon without a
  mechanism; the mechanism is Lesson 11's and the demand asks for it.
- ❌ **Claiming any local book teaches upwelling, ENSO, the IOD or thermohaline circulation.** None
  does (H1).
- ❌ **Writing a sea-lane, chokepoint or Indo-Pacific-shift argument.** ↪ `Geography/basic/33` and
  `International-Relations/basic/04`.
- ❌ **Looking for an Indian cold-current desert.** There is none, and the absence is structural.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography; the Prelims GS-I clause.

**Owned demands served.** **O2 (2022 GS-I Q14)**: the **Oyashio-Kuroshio confluence off Hokkaido** is
the second of Lesson 12's three fishing mechanisms, and the **Humboldt** is the third. **O5 (2021
Prelims Q58)**: trades and westerlies organise both Pacific limbs. **No key is available for O5 and
none is inferred.**

**Adjacent demand NOT absorbed.** ↪ **2026 GS-I Q16**, "the centre of global trade shifting from the
Atlantic to the Indo-Pacific", belongs to `Geography/basic/33_Transport-Trade-and-Indian-Space-
Programme` and `International-Relations/basic/04`. We own where the water goes; we do not own where
the ships go.

**Answer use.** When naming currents, **name the system, not the item**: "the Bering-Kamchatka-Oyashio
cold limb" carries more information than "the Oyashio", and it protects against the Alaska Current
trap. And where one of the three disputes arises, spend a clause on it.

### Revision points

1. **North Pacific gyre clockwise; South Pacific gyre anti-clockwise.** Same asymmetry as the
   Atlantic: **west warm, east cold**.
2. North Pacific sequence: **North Equatorial → Kuroshio ("or Kuro Siwo or Japan Current") → North
   Pacific Drift → Alaska / Bering-Okhotsk-Oyashio → Californian**.
3. South Pacific sequence: **South Equatorial → East Australian (→ Tasman Sea) → South Pacific
   Current / West Wind Drift → Humboldt or Peruvian**.
4. **⇄ N-06:** ✅ Leong PDF 93 — "the **cold Bering Current or Alaskan Current**"; ✅ Majid printed
   4.11 — "**Alaska Current — Warm**". **Both shown; neither silently adopted.** Leong himself gives
   the ice-free Alaskan ports to the **North Pacific Drift**.
5. **Taught structure:** Bering-Kamchatka-Oyashio = **cold limb**; Alaska Current = **warm limb**.
6. **⇄ N-34:** "**Agulhas or Mozambique Current**" as one feature (Leong Fig. 95) against **two
   separate warm rows** (Majid 4.11). Drawn separately in M1 and M4.
7. **✗ N-33:** Majid's Indian Ocean row has **only Mozambique, Agulhas and West Australian — no
   monsoon drift**. Not a complete inventory.
8. ✅ The **Oyashio-Kuroshio confluence off Hokkaido** produces "heavy fog" and is "conducive for the
   development of fisheries" (Majid Table 4.2).
9. ✅ The **West Wind Drift / Antarctic Circumpolar Current** is unobstructed, closes on itself, and
   **feeds the Benguela, the Peruvian and the West Australian** — one current, three cold limbs.
10. ✅ **Southern Chile** diverts part of the West Wind Drift northward as the **Peruvian Current**
    (Leong printed 88) — basin geometry as a control, after Cape Sao Roque.
11. ✅ The **Humboldt** leaves the Chilean and Peruvian coasts "**practically rainless**" and supports
    the fish-and-**guano** chain — **without Leong ever naming upwelling**. **✗ N-30:** "Sonara" →
    **Sonoran**.
12. **H1:** across all four local books, **upwelling, Ekman, Walker, IOD, MJO, PDO, thermohaline,
    AMOC, marine heatwave, acidification, OMZ and dead zone return zero occurrences**. Majid mentions
    **El Niño exactly once**, in Table 4.2's Peru cell.
13. 🇮🇳 **India has no cold eastern-boundary current and therefore no cold-current coastal desert.**
    The absence is structural: the basin is closed in the north, so no gyre closes.
14. 🇮🇳 India has instead the **East and West India Coastal Currents**, which **reverse with the
    monsoon**, plus **seasonal upwelling** off Somalia and the south-west coast.
15. **U-14:** Leong PDF 93's clipped left margin loses the opening words of several lines; nothing is
    reconstructed.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate's note reads: "Alaska Current — cold." Another reads: "Alaska Current —
warm; keeps Alaskan ports open." Both cite a standard text. How should this be handled in an answer?

**Model answer:** By **showing the conflict and naming the system rather than the item**. ✅ Leong,
scan PDF p.93, writes of "the **cold Bering Current or Alaskan Current**", which "creeps southwards
from the narrow Bering Strait and is joined by the Okhotsk Current to meet the warm Japan Current as
the **Oyashio**, off Hokkaido". ✅ Majid IWG, Table 4.2 at printed 4.11, tabulates "**Alaska Current —
Warm — Keeps the seaports of Alaska open throughout the year**". The two are describing the **same
physical system** and disagreeing about which name carries which limb: Leong himself, two sentences
earlier, attributes the ice-free Alaskan ports to the **North Pacific Drift**, which is exactly the
function Majid gives the Alaska Current. The physically defensible description is therefore that the
**Bering-Kamchatka-Oyashio limb is the cold flow** entering from the Bering Strait, while the **Alaska
Current is the warm northward continuation of the North Pacific Drift** around the Gulf of Alaska.
Since **no official source settles the nomenclature** and none was retrieved, the correct treatment
is to teach the system, state that standard texts differ on the name, and **assert neither label**.
In an objective context the candidate should recognise that the item is contested rather than
guessing confidently.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a current's name is a fact in the way its temperature is. Three of
this topic's taught conflicts are **naming** disputes, and names are conventions subject to the same
instability as the ocean boundaries of Lesson 1.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 10

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Trace the Pacific and Southern Ocean circulation, and
examine the problem of current nomenclature in standard sources.

**Model:** The Pacific repeats the Atlantic pattern at greater scale. Trades drive the **North
Equatorial Current** west; it becomes the **Kuroshio**, which ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93, also calls
"**Kuro Siwo or Japan Current**"; the westerlies carry it east as the **North Pacific Drift**, which
Leong credits with keeping **Alaskan ports ice-free**; and the cold **Californian Current** returns
equatorward, closing a **clockwise** gyre. Cold water enters from the **Bering Strait** and, joined by
the **Okhotsk Current**, meets the Kuroshio as the **Oyashio** off **Hokkaido**, a confluence ✅ Majid
records as producing "heavy fog" and major fisheries. In the south, the **South Equatorial**, **East
Australian** and **South Pacific** Currents close an **anti-clockwise** gyre, with the cold
**Humboldt or Peruvian** Current as the eastern limb — which ✅ Leong says leaves the Chilean and
Peruvian coasts "**practically rainless**". Below 40°S the **West Wind Drift**, or Antarctic
Circumpolar Current, runs unobstructed around Antarctica and **feeds the cold eastern limbs of all
three Southern Hemisphere basins**; ✅ Leong notes that **southern Chile** diverts part of it
northward as the Peruvian Current.

Nomenclature is unreliable. ⇄ Leong writes "the **cold** Bering Current **or Alaskan Current**", while
✅ Majid tabulates "**Alaska Current — Warm**" — a sign conflict on one name. ⇄ Leong's Fig. 95 labels
a single "**Agulhas or Mozambique Current**" where Majid tabulates two. No official source settles
either, so both are shown. *(218 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Pacific circuit traced **6** · Southern limb and West Wind Drift **5** ·
naming-conflict handling **3** · India absence-of-cold-current point **1**

## Lesson 11 — Upwelling: how the deep ocean feeds the surface

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 11 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : C1 §9.4 (lines 200-220) is the ONLY source for this lesson. It
                   carries a fenced ASCII block giving the Ekman chain and a
                   four-row settings table, ALL AT ITS OWN ⚠️ STANDARD-KNOWLEDGE
                   TIER. C1's process control at line 396 names "coastal
                   wind-Ekman divergence" as owned core.
📕 Book evidence : NONE. ⚠️ H1 APPLIES AT FULL FORCE HERE. The words "upwelling" and
                   "Ekman" return ZERO occurrences across all four local books.
                   Leong scan PDF p.93 DESCRIBES the Peruvian upwelling fishery —
                   the plankton, the fish, the sea-birds, the guano — WITHOUT EVER
                   NAMING THE MECHANISM. That description is used as the phenomenon;
```

Progress: 11 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **Ekman spiral and offshore-transport block**

### The intuition, before any term

Everyone's first guess is that to bring deep water to the surface you need a wind blowing **away**
from the shore — push the surface water out to sea and something must come up to replace it. That
works, but it is not how the world's great upwelling systems operate, and it is not what the examiner
is testing.

The answer is stranger and better. **Wind blowing *along* a coast moves surface water *sideways*.**
Because of the Earth's rotation, the water does not go where the wind pushes it; it goes off at an
angle. Get the geometry right — wind along the shore with the coast on a particular side — and the
net transport is **offshore** even though the wind never blows offshore at all. Water leaves the
coastal strip, and water from below rises to take its place.

And that rising water is **cold, dark and loaded with nutrients**, because nutrients sink out of the
sunlit layer continuously and accumulate below. Upwelling is a **vertical nutrient pump** driven by a
**horizontal wind**, mediated by **rotation**. It is the most elegant mechanism in the topic, and no
local book contains it.

### The primary visual — the spiral and the block

**Caption.** Wind along; transport across; water up. The spiral is the mechanism, the nutricline is
the resource, and C1's causal control is the qualification that separates a 10-mark answer from a
15-mark one.

### The four settings — 📗 C1 §9.4's table, developed

| Setting | Mechanism | Named cases | What can go wrong |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Eastern-boundary coastal upwelling** | Equatorward wind along an eastern basin margin; Ekman transport offshore | **Peru/Humboldt**, **Benguela**, **Canaries**, **California**, **West Australian** — the classic five | Wind relaxation; a deepened thermocline (see below) |
| ⚠️ **Equatorial upwelling** | Trades blow westward **along** the equator; Ekman transport is to the **right north of the equator and to the left south of it**, so the surface **diverges away from the line itself** and water rises along it | The equatorial Pacific and Atlantic cold tongues | The trades weakening — which is precisely what an El Niño is (Lesson 18) |
| ⚠️ **Monsoonal upwelling** | Seasonal, driven by a **wind reversal** rather than a persistent wind | 🇮🇳 **Somali** and **south-west Indian coast** cells (Lesson 17) | It switches off when the wind reverses; productivity is **seasonal, not year-round** |
| ⚠️ **Upwelling failure** | The wind still blows and transport still occurs, but a **deepened thermocline** means the water raised is **warm and nutrient-poor** | The eastern Pacific during a warm ENSO phase | — this **is** the failure |

⚠️ **The fourth row is not a setting; it is a refutation**, and it is the most valuable row in the
table. C1 states it exactly: "During El Nino, a **deepened thermocline** means the upwelled water is
warm and nutrient-poor, so the fishery collapses **even though the wind may still blow**."

```text
   WHY THE FAILURE CASE IS THE WHOLE ARGUMENT

   WIND ──► EKMAN TRANSPORT ──► WATER RISES ──► NUTRIENTS? ──► BLOOM
                                      ▲                ▲
                                      │                │
                      THE WIND SETS THIS            THE THERMOCLINE
                                                     DEPTH SETS THIS

   ⇒ UPWELLING IS A PUMP. A PUMP RAISES WHATEVER IS BENEATH IT.
   ⇒ If the nutricline is SHALLOW, the pump raises nutrients.
     If the nutricline has been pushed DOWN — by a deepened thermocline,
     or by a strengthened surface stratification — THE SAME PUMP RAISES
     WARM, NUTRIENT-POOR WATER AND NOTHING BLOOMS.
   ⇒ THEREFORE: "there is upwelling here" is NOT a sufficient explanation
     of a fishery, and "the wind blew" is NOT a sufficient explanation of
     a bloom. 📗 C1's causal control, line 410, is the examinable sentence.
   ⇒ AND THIS IS THE LINK TO LESSON 18: an El Niño's effect on the Peruvian
     fishery is NOT that the wind stops. It is that the THERMOCLINE DEEPENS.
```

### The nutrient logic — why the deep ocean is rich and the sunlit ocean is poor

⚠️ This inversion surprises people and it is worth stating plainly, because it is the reason the
mechanism matters at all:

| Layer | Light | Nutrients | Productivity |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Surface / photic** | Abundant | **Depleted** — stripped out continuously by plankton | Limited by **nutrients** |
| **Below the nutricline** | **Absent** | **Abundant** — accumulated from sinking, decaying organic matter | Limited by **light** |

⚠️ **Production requires both, and they are in different places.** Every great fishery in the world is
therefore a place where some process **brings the two together**: upwelling raises nutrients into the
light; shelf shallowness lets light reach a nutrient-rich floor (Lesson 2 and Lesson 12's first
mechanism); a confluence mixes two water bodies (Lesson 12's second). **Three different ways to solve
one problem** — which is exactly why the owned 2022 demand has three answers and not one.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — Somali and south-west-coast upwelling, and why Indian productivity is seasonal

✅ **C2** states the India case: "**Seasonal upwelling off Somalia and western India during the SW
monsoon boosts nutrients and fisheries.**"

```text
   WHY INDIA'S UPWELLING IS DIFFERENT FROM PERU'S

   PERU                                 INDIA (SW monsoon season)
   ─────────────────────────────────    ───────────────────────────────────
   Wind: PERSISTENT equatorward          Wind: SEASONAL south-westerly,
         along-shore trade                     along the Somali and
                                                south-west Indian coasts
   Coast: EASTERN boundary of a          Coast: WESTERN boundary of a basin
          closed subtropical gyre                with NO closed gyre
   Duration: YEAR-ROUND                  Duration: ROUGHLY JUNE TO SEPTEMBER
   Interruption: by ENSO phase,          Interruption: EVERY YEAR, when the
          irregularly                           wind reverses in winter
   ─────────────────────────────────    ───────────────────────────────────
   RESULT: one of the world's most       RESULT: a PULSE of very high
   productive fisheries, continuously    productivity, then a long quiet
                                          season. 📗 C1 §9.4: "which is why
                                          productivity off parts of the
                                          Indian coast is STRONGLY SEASONAL
                                          RATHER THAN YEAR-ROUND."

   ⇒ 🇮🇳 THE STRUCTURAL REASON, TRACED BACK THROUGH THIS EDITION:
     basin closed in the north (L1) → no closed gyre (L10, L17) → no
     persistent eastern-boundary wind → upwelling must be driven by the
     MONSOON REVERSAL (L8, L17) → and a reversal is BY DEFINITION seasonal.
   ⇒ India's fishery calendar is therefore a CONSEQUENCE OF BASIN GEOMETRY.
```

and **zooplankton biomass**, in one instrument programme. That pairing is this lesson's causal chain

mooring**, because the whole claim is that one drives the other.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "If no book in the repository teaches upwelling, is it safe to write about it?"

**Reply.** It is **necessary** to write about it, and the edition is explicit about where the
knowledge comes from. The controlling owner carries the mechanism at its own ⚠️ standard-knowledge
tier, having **promoted it into Core on 13 August 2026** precisely because it was trapped in the
optional tier; its process control at line 396 names "coastal wind-Ekman divergence" as owned core;
and the owned 2022 Mains demand asks for mechanisms of fishing-ground formation, of which this is one
of three. What is **not** safe is to pretend a book citation exists. ✅ Leong's Peruvian paragraph is
the **phenomenon** — plankton, fish, sea-birds, guano — and it is cited as such; the **mechanism** is
⚠️ and is marked ⚠️ every time it appears.

**Unresolved residual.** No primary measurement of any Indian upwelling cell — its seasonal depth,
its nutrient flux, its productivity — was retrieved. The India case is taught as a **mechanism with a
season**, not as a quantity. **U-08** additionally forbids any RAMA count.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Saying upwelling needs an offshore wind.** It needs an **along-shore** wind with the correct
  geometry; the transport is offshore, the wind is not.
- ❌ **Using the Northern Hemisphere rule in the Southern.** Transport is 90° **left** of the wind
  south of the equator; the required wind direction reverses (N-20).
- ❌ **Expecting coastal-style upwelling at the equator.** Coriolis vanishes there; equatorial
  upwelling works by **divergence across** the line, not by a coastal boundary.
- ❌ **Treating upwelling as sufficient for a fishery.** 📗 C1 line 410 forbids it; the failure case is
  a deepened **thermocline**, not an absent wind.
- ❌ **Saying El Niño stops the Peruvian wind.** It **deepens the thermocline**, so the same pump
  raises warm, nutrient-poor water.
- ❌ **Writing about phytoplankton as organisms, food webs or primary producers.** ↪
  `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01_Ecosystem-Structure` owns **2021 Prelims Q22** and **2025 Prelims
  Q40**. We supply the **physical nutrient supply** and stop.
- ❌ **Stating a RAMA count.** It is not named in either retrieved answer (U-08).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Salient features of world physical geography; and the geophysical-phenomena clause,
since upwelling strength is one of the ways a change in the ocean becomes a change in a critical
feature.

**Owned demand served — O2 (2022 GS-I Q14), second half, in part.** Upwelling is **one of three**
mechanisms, and Lesson 12 makes the separation explicit. A candidate who offers upwelling as the whole
answer has answered a third of the question — which is the single most expensive error available on
this demand.

**Adjacent demands NOT absorbed.** ↪ **2021 Prelims Q22** (primary producers in ocean food chains)
and **2025 Prelims Q40** (sources of the planet's oxygen — rainforests, phytoplankton) belong to
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01_Ecosystem-Structure`. The physics that makes both answerable is
taught here; the ecology is named and routed.

**Answer use.** Derive the mechanism in **four arrows** — wind along-shore, Ekman transport offshore,
divergence, replacement from below — then give **one setting and one failure**. The failure is worth
more than a fourth setting.

### Revision points

1. **Upwelling is driven by wind blowing ALONG a coast, not onto or off it.**
2. **Ekman transport** is the depth-integrated surface transport, at **90° to the right of the wind
   in the Northern Hemisphere and 90° to the left in the Southern**.
3. The **Ekman spiral**: each layer drags the one below, turning further and weakening; **friction**
   makes it decay with depth.
4. Where the transport is **offshore**, a **divergence** forms at the coast and deeper water rises.
5. The rising water is **cold, dark and nutrient-rich**, because nutrients **accumulate below the
   nutricline** and are **depleted above it**.
6. Production needs **light and nutrients**, which are in **different layers** — so every great
   fishery is a mechanism for bringing them together.
7. 📗 **Four settings (C1 §9.4):** eastern-boundary coastal · equatorial divergence · **monsoonal
   (seasonal)** · **upwelling failure**.
8. **Equatorial upwelling** works because Coriolis acts in **opposite directions** across the line;
   it needs no coast. Coastal-style Ekman reasoning **fails at the equator**.
9. 📗 **The failure case:** during a warm ENSO phase a **deepened thermocline** means the upwelled
   water is **warm and nutrient-poor**, "so the fishery collapses **even though the wind may still
   blow**".
10. 📗 **The causal control, C1 line 410, binding: "upwelling raises nutrient supply but does not
    guarantee a fishery."**
11. **H1:** the words **upwelling** and **Ekman** appear **nowhere** in any of the four local books.
    ✅ Leong PDF 93 describes the Peruvian fishery — plankton, fish, sea-birds, **guano** — without
    naming the mechanism. Phenomenon from the book; mechanism from 📗 C1's ⚠️ tier.
12. 🇮🇳 India's cells: **Somali** and the **south-west Indian coast**, driven by the **SW monsoon**,
    roughly **June to September**, so productivity is **seasonal rather than year-round** (C1 §9.4).
13. 🇮🇳 The seasonality is **structural**: basin closed in the north → no closed gyre → no persistent
    eastern-boundary wind → forcing must come from the monsoon reversal.
14. 📄 **CA-2, 29-30 July 2026:** **19 subsurface moorings (16 mainland EEZ + 3 equatorial Indian
    Ocean)** for "**monitoring ocean currents and zooplankton biomass**". **16 + 3 = 19 ✓ closes.**
15. **U-08:** **RAMA is in neither answer; no RAMA count may be stated.**

### Concept check

**Question:** During a strong warm ENSO phase the equatorward wind along the Peruvian coast continues
to blow, yet the fishery collapses. Explain, and state the general principle.

**Model answer:** Because upwelling is a **pump, and a pump raises whatever lies beneath it**. The
wind-driven part of the system is unchanged: an along-shore equatorward wind still produces **Ekman
transport at 90° to the left of the wind** in the Southern Hemisphere, that transport is still
offshore, a **divergence** still forms at the coast, and water still rises to replace what has left.
What changes is **what is down there to be raised**. 📗 The Core owner states it directly: during an
El Niño "a **deepened thermocline** means the upwelled water is **warm and nutrient-poor**, so the
fishery collapses even though the wind may still blow." The warm pool spreads eastward, the
thermocline — and with it the nutricline — is pushed down below the depth the upwelling reaches, and
the pump therefore lifts warm, nutrient-depleted water into the photic zone. No nutrients means no
phytoplankton bloom, which means no food chain, and the fishery that ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93,
describes through its "huge shoals of fish" and its "thick deposits of **guano**" fails. The general
principle is 📗 the owner's binding causal control: **upwelling raises nutrient supply but does not
guarantee a fishery.** Two conditions must hold simultaneously — the physical transport **and** a
shallow nutricline — and only the first is set by the wind.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the wind is the whole of upwelling. The wind sets the **transport**;
the stratification sets the **payload**. A mechanism that delivers nothing useful is still operating.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 11

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Derive the mechanism of coastal upwelling and examine why
its occurrence does not by itself explain a fishery.

**Model:** Coastal upwelling begins with wind blowing **along** a coast. Because the Earth rotates,
the wind-driven surface layer does not move with the wind: each successive layer drags the one below
and turns further, forming the **Ekman spiral**, which **friction** causes to decay with depth. The
depth-integrated **Ekman transport** is therefore at **90° to the right of the wind in the Northern
Hemisphere and 90° to the left in the Southern**. Where that transport is **offshore**, surface water
is removed, a **divergence** forms at the coast, and deeper, colder water rises to replace it. Because
nutrients are continuously stripped from the sunlit layer by plankton and accumulate below the
**nutricline**, the rising water is nutrient-rich; combined with surface light it supports a
phytoplankton bloom and an exceptionally productive food chain. Four settings follow: **eastern-
boundary coastal**, **equatorial divergence** where Coriolis acts oppositely across the line,
**monsoonal** and seasonal, and **failure**.

Occurrence is not sufficient, because the mechanism has **two independent conditions**. The wind sets
the transport; the **stratification sets what is available to be raised**. 📗 The Core owner records
that during a warm ENSO phase "a **deepened thermocline** means the upwelled water is warm and
nutrient-poor, so the fishery collapses even though the wind may still blow", and states the control
directly: "**upwelling raises nutrient supply but does not guarantee a fishery.**" 🇮🇳 India's Somali
and south-west-coast cells add a third qualification — monsoon forcing makes them **seasonal rather
than year-round**. *(238 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Ekman mechanism derived **5** · four upwelling settings **5** ·
nutrient-to-productivity chain **3** · failure case under a deepened thermocline **2**
