# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

Sixteen routing and paper ledgers covering 2018-2026 were swept with a 106-alternative ocean,
circulation, tide, salinity, coupled-mode, basin, fisheries, observation and adjacent-domain term
pattern, closed with an **exact-owner-filename** regular expression. **163 matching lines; 100
identified as lexical false positives with the method disclosed; 63 genuine; seven exact-owner
hits.** There are **zero CSAT hits** — the three CSAT matches are lexical, inside "Two walkers
meeting point", "Periodic signal synchronisation" and "Periodic-sequence sum". **Topic 12 has no
CSAT surface.**

**The owned profile, stated plainly.** **Two owned Mains demands, both 15 marks and 250 words**, from
**2019 and 2022**; **five owned Prelims demands**, from **2020 (×1), 2021 (×3) and 2024 (×1)**.
**Nothing is owned in 2018, 2023, 2025 or 2026** (U-23). This is the richest owned profile in the
lane so far and it is **entirely pre-2025**. Both facts are stated here and recency is **not
manufactured** by claiming adjacent 2025-2026 questions.

**A recorded discrepancy in the owner's own clause (U-29).** C1's control clause at lines 417-420
enumerates its verified PYQ ownership as **two** demands — the 2019 currents-versus-water-masses
demand and the 2022 forces-and-fishing demand — and omits all five Prelims demands. The clause is
**incomplete rather than wrong**; the band below supersedes it for routing purposes. **C1 is not
edited.**

## ANSWER-NEUTRALITY RULE — BINDING THROUGHOUT THIS SECTION AND THIS FILE

1. This edition contains **no option letters, no answer keys and no elimination chains** anywhere,
   for any objective demand, owned or adjacent.
2. **O3, O4, O5 and O6 have no locally available key.** The ledgers state so in terms: "Routed; key
   unavailable locally". **None is inferred, reconstructed or implied.**
3. For **O7** the ledger records that a Set-A key **is** present locally. The ledger itself records
   no answer letter. **This edition records none either. Key availability is not key disclosure**
   (U-24).
4. **O1 and O2 are subjective Mains demands authored by UPSC and therefore do receive full 250-word
   models. The five objective demands never do.** The distinction is absolute: this edition may
   answer what UPSC asked in essay form and may never answer what UPSC asked in objective form.
5. The edition runs in **concept-check mode**. No compiled four-option corpus exists anywhere in it.

## BAND A — OWNED DEMANDS, ROUTED BY EXACT FILENAME TO THE TOPIC 12 OWNERS

### A·1 — O1 · 2019 Mains GS-I, Question 17

**Ledger row, verbatim:**
`| 2019 | GS-I | 17 | Ocean currents and water masses and their impact on marine life | Geography | How do they differ with examples · 15 marks · 250 words | [Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity](Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity.md) | Routed to owning topic |`

**Directive decoded.** ***How do they differ*, with examples. 15 marks, 250 words.** The directive is
a **differentiation**, and the two terms it differentiates are *ocean currents* and *water masses*.
C1 defines the first and **never defines the second** (structural gap 2). D-09 — ✅ Leong printed 88's
Mediterranean passage — is the only water-mass evidence in the entire base. **Lesson 7** supplies the
missing term; **Lesson 12** supplies the marine-life half.

**Construction required.** A differentiation must hold a **two-column discrimination throughout** and
converge on a **shared consequence** at the end. It must **not** describe one term, then the other.

**Model answer (15 marks, 250 words).**

An **ocean current** is defined by **motion**; a **water mass** by **properties**. A current is a
horizontal transport past a place, persisting though its water is continuously replaced — ✅ Leong,
printed 88, characterises the Gulf Stream by exactly such quantities, "**35 to 100 miles wide, 2,000
feet deep, three miles an hour**". A water mass is a body identified by its **temperature-salinity
signature**, acquired at the surface and retained as it sinks to neutral buoyancy and spreads. A
current is **driven** — by wind stress, Coriolis deflection, pressure gradient and basin shape; a
water mass is **formed**, by the atmosphere acting on water where it is last in contact. A current is
traced; a water mass is **labelled**. A velocity reveals **forcing**; a T-S pair reveals **history**.

One setting yields both. ✅ Leong, printed 88, records that at Gibraltar "the less saline water of the
Atlantic flows on the surface into the Mediterranean, and this is compensated for by an **outflow of
denser bottom water from the Mediterranean**". The inflow is a **current**; the outflow — **warm yet
dense, because salinity outweighs temperature** — spreads westward as an identifiable **water mass**.

Both shape marine life through **one shared consequence**: they govern where nutrients meet light.
Currents do it by **mixing at confluences** — ✅ Majid's Gulf Stream–**Labrador** and
**Oyashio–Kuroshio** fisheries — and by **wind-driven upwelling**; water masses do it by **setting
stratification**, which decides whether upwelled water is nutrient-rich at all. *(234 words)*

**Why the model is constructed this way.** Three paragraphs: a **sustained two-column contrast**
across six axes; a **single setting** producing one of each; and a **convergence** on the shared
consequence the question's second clause demands. **It shares no paragraph skeleton with the O2 model
below.**

### A·2 — O2 · 2022 Mains GS-I, Question 14

**Ledger row, verbatim:**
`| 2022 | GS-I | 14 | Forces influencing ocean currents and their role in fishing | Geography | What are and Describe · 15 marks · 250 words | [Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity](Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity.md) | Routed to owning topic |`

**Directive decoded.** ***What are*** and ***Describe***. **15 marks, 250 words.** A **two-part**
demand: an **enumeration** of forces — on which the three authorities disagree (N-01) — followed by a
**described mechanism** for fishing, which has **three** distinct forms that must not be run together
(D-08). **Lesson 8** is the first half; **Lesson 12** the second.

**Construction required.** **Enumeration first, then mechanism.** A list that becomes a sequence —
structurally the opposite of O1's sustained contrast.

**Model answer (15 marks, 250 words).**

**The forces.** Surface circulation is organised by **wind stress**, **Coriolis deflection**
(rightward in the north, leftward in the south), the **pressure gradient** that develops once wind
piles water up, **density contrast** from temperature and salinity, **basin geometry** —
coastline and ocean floor — and **friction**. Standard enumerations differ, and the divergence
matters: ✅ Leong, printed 87-88, lists **planetary winds, temperatures, salinity, the earth's
rotation, land**; ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.9, lists **differential heating of the equatorial and polar
oceans, trade and anti-trade winds, the Coriolis effect, variations in salinity and temperature,
configuration of the coast line and ocean floor**. Wind's primacy is demonstrable: ✅ Leong, scan PDF
p.93, records the North Indian Ocean's **complete seasonal reversal** as evidence of "the **dominant
effects of winds**".

**The role in fishing — three mechanisms, not one.** **First, the shelf**: ✅ Leong, printed 85,
"limited depth allows **sunlight to penetrate**", making shelves "**the richest fishing grounds in the
world, e.g. the Grand Banks off Newfoundland, the North Sea and the Sunda Shelf**" — a **geometry**
effect. **Second, confluence**: ✅ Majid, Table 4.2, the **Gulf Stream–Labrador** meeting off
Newfoundland and the **Oyashio–Kuroshio** off Hokkaido, where frontal mixing supplies nutrients.
**Third, upwelling**: along-shore wind drives Ekman transport offshore, and nutrient-rich water rises
— the Peruvian coast ✅ Leong describes through its fish and **guano** without naming the mechanism.

Currents are **not** their common cause: the shelf is a depth effect. And 📗 "upwelling raises nutrient
supply but does not guarantee a fishery." *(234 words)*

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

Cross-lesson synthesis. Each check requires material from **two or more** lessons and tests a joint
capability that no single lesson delivers alone.

### CC-1 · Conventions across four scales

**Prompt.** Lessons 1, 5, 19 and 20 each turn on a convention. Name the convention in each and state
the single idea they share.

**Expected response.** **Lesson 1** — the **ocean's boundary**: 60°S (proposed), 55°S (withdrawn), the
Antarctic coast, or the moving Antarctic Convergence, each producing a different Indian Ocean.
**Lesson 5** — the **unit**: ‰ or ppt and never per cent, and the composition's own base, since 2.6
is a share of water and 78.3 per cent is the share of salt. **Lesson 19** — the **threshold**: a DMI
of **±0.4** against an ENSO threshold of **±0.5**, so the same number yields opposite verdicts.
**Lesson 20** — the **base period**: 📄 anomalies "computed with respect to **30-year (1991-2020) long
term mean**", and three sea-level series over three different windows.
**The shared idea:** every quantity in this topic is a **measurement passed through a declared
convention**, and a change in the convention is indistinguishable from a change in the ocean unless
the convention is stated.

### CC-2 · One basin geometry, five consequences

**Prompt.** The North Indian Ocean is closed in the north by land. Trace five consequences established
across Lessons 1, 10, 12 and 17.

**Expected response.** ① **No closed subtropical gyre** can form, because there is no northern limb —
hence ✅ C2's trap against drawing a fixed gyre. ② **No persistent cold equatorward eastern-boundary
current** exists on India's coast. ③ Therefore **no cold-current coastal desert in India**, unlike the
Namib, the Atacama, the Sonoran and the West Australian. ④ **No persistent warm-cold confluence**, so
India has no Grand Banks or Hokkaido analogue, and only **two of the three** fishing mechanisms
operate. ⑤ Circulation must instead be driven by the **monsoon wind reversal**, which makes **upwelling
seasonal rather than year-round**. **One geometric fact; five independent consequences.**

### CC-3 · The same failure mode in two owners

**Prompt.** C1 and C2 each hedge correctly in prose and over-claim in a bullet list. Identify both
instances and state the resolution rule.

**Expected response.** **C2**: lines 96-97 state that a positive IOD "**does not mechanically
guarantee** a strong all-India monsoon", and line 106 then asserts "**Positive IOD → stronger Indian
monsoon; Negative → weaker**" as a Must-Know Fact (✗ N-C2-01). **C1**: lines 106-107 describe the
Indian-monsoon response to AMOC change as "**model-dependent rather than a settled one-direction
forecast**", and line 113 then lists "Risk: southward ITCZ shift → weaker/delayed Indian monsoon" as a
Must-Know Fact. **The resolution rule in both cases: the hedged prose governs the deterministic
bullet**, supported by the answer-worthiness audit's prohibition at line 345. **Neither owner is
edited.**

### CC-4 · Why the thermocline is the pivot of the whole topic

**Prompt.** The thermocline appears in Lessons 4, 7, 11, 18 and 19. State its role in each and the
unifying function.

**Expected response.** **Lesson 4** — it is the **steep temperature gradient** from 100 m to 1000 m
holding about 18 per cent of ocean volume, separating a thin warm lid from a cold deep. **Lesson 7** —
combined with the halocline it produces the **pycnocline**, the density barrier that actually resists
motion. **Lesson 11** — it sets the depth of the nutricline, and therefore whether upwelling raises
nutrients or warm, nutrient-poor water. **Lesson 18** — its **tilt** across the Pacific is what the
trades maintain and what El Niño flattens; its deepening in the east is why the fishery fails.
**Lesson 19** — its displacement is the subsurface signature the bulletin reports relative to the 20°C
isotherm. **Unifying function: the thermocline is the valve between the surface ocean, which responds
in seasons, and the deep ocean, which responds in centuries.** Every human-timescale mechanism in this
topic works by **moving it**.

### CC-5 · Three fishing mechanisms against two tests

**Prompt.** Apply the tests "what if the wind stops?" and "what if the thermocline deepens?" to the
three mechanisms of Lesson 12, and say what this proves about the owned 2022 demand.

**Expected response.** **Wind stops:** the **shelf** fishery is unaffected — the floor is still
shallow and sunlit; the **confluence** is little affected immediately — the currents are
basin-forced; the **upwelling** fishery **stops** — the wind is the pump. **Thermocline deepens:** the
shelf is unaffected, since shelf water is shallow and well mixed; the confluence is weakened but not
abolished; **upwelling collapses**, because 📗 "the upwelled water is warm and nutrient-poor … even
though the wind may still blow". **What it proves:** the three have **different sensitivities**, so
they are **different arguments, not three examples of one**. The 2022 directive is *Describe*, and
offering upwelling alone answers one-third of the question.

### CC-6 · What each book cannot do

**Prompt.** For each of the four local books, state one thing it supplies that no other does, and one
thing it cannot supply at all.

**Expected response.** **Leong** supplies the only **water-mass evidence** in the base (the
Mediterranean exchange, printed 88), the only **overturning return-limb** statement (two-thirds,
printed 88), the complete **monsoon-reversal passage**, and a **world current map** with both drifts
seasoned. It cannot supply **any tide theory** (✗ N-23) and never names **upwelling**. **Majid IWG**
supplies the only **tide theory**, the only **salinity composition table**, the only **hypsographic
curve** and the only usable **text layer**. It cannot supply a correct **temperature-salinity control**
(✗ N-02) and its Table 4.2 cannot be read from its own text layer (U-15). **Khullar** supplies the
**Indian Ocean basin section** and the **seabed-resource inventory**. It has **no oceanography chapter
at all**. **Majid *Geography of India*** supplies **nothing to Topic 12**; its only ocean-coupling
section is **Topic 16's**.

### CC-7 · Eight conflicts, located

**Prompt.** Name the eight taught inter-source conflicts and the lesson in which each is resolved by
being shown.

**Expected response.** ① **The ocean inventory** — five (Majid) against four implied (Khullar) against
a de-recognised-and-proposed Southern Ocean (IHO) — **Lessons 1 and 16**. ② **The Japan Trench** —
10,554 m (Majid) against 28,000 ft ≈ 8,534 m (Leong) — **Lesson 2**. ③ **The abyssal-plain extent** —
two-thirds of the ocean floor (Leong) against 80-85 per cent of the **Pacific** floor (Majid) —
**Lesson 2**. ④ **The *Challenger* start year** — 1873-1876 (Leong) against 1872-76 (Khullar) —
**Lessons 2 and 16**. ⑤ **The Alaska Current's sign** — cold (Leong) against warm (Majid) — **Lesson
10**. ⑥ **Agulhas or Mozambique** — one feature (Leong Fig. 95) against two rows (Majid) — **Lessons 10
and 17**. ⑦ **The forces taxonomies** — five against five against four — **Lesson 8**. ⑧ **36 against
50 tide gauges** — within the same official answers — **Lesson 20**. **In every case both sides appear
with their location, and none is adjudicated where the conflict rule forbids it.**

### CC-8 · Six self-contradictions, located

**Prompt.** Name the six intra-source self-contradictions and state how each is handled.

**Expected response.** ① **Leong on the Caspian** — 180‰ at printed 86 against "often over 37‰" as a
class at printed 87 (✗ N-04). ② **Leong on the Dead Sea** — 250‰ in the body at printed 86 against
240‰ in his own exercise at printed 90 (✗ N-05). ③ **Majid on temperature and salinity** — a direct
relation at printed 4.7 against low equatorial salinity at printed 4.8 (✗ N-02). ④ **Majid on vertical
salinity** — "no generalisation can be made" immediately followed by a generalisation, at printed 4.8
(✗ N-03). ⑤ **Majid on ocean area** — 367 million km² against 70.8 per cent, inside one paragraph
(✗ N-15). ⑥ **C2 on the IOD-monsoon relation** — hedged at lines 96-97, deterministic at line 106
(✗ N-C2-01). **Handling: both locations are shown and neither side is asserted — except for ⑥, which
is resolved against C2 by C2's own hedged lines and the audit's prohibition, and except for ③, where
the physics is settled by Leong's E − P control.**

### CC-9 · The gradient-and-level distinction, twice

**Prompt.** Lessons 1 and 19 both separate a **difference** from a **level**. Show the parallel.

**Expected response.** **Lesson 19:** the **DMI is a difference** — west minus east — so a basin that
warms **uniformly** leaves it unchanged. 📄 In August 2026 both Indian Ocean poles showed positive SST
anomalies and the IOD was **neutral**. Warm is a level; the index is a gradient. **Lesson 1:** an
**ocean's area is a difference** between two boundaries, not a property of water. Move the southern
limit and the area changes though **not one litre of ocean has moved**. **The parallel:** in both
cases a quantity people treat as a property of the ocean is in fact a **relation defined by a
convention**, and in both cases the error is to read a relational quantity as an absolute one.

### CC-10 · Assembling an unseen answer

**Prompt.** A question asks: "Examine how ocean physics determines the geography of marine
productivity in India." Assemble the answer from this edition's lessons and name the route-outs.

**Expected response.** **Lesson 2** supplies the shelf — ✅ Khullar's **3.2 lakh sq km** of Indian
offshore shelf to 200 m — and the light argument. **Lesson 7** supplies the Bay of Bengal's
**freshwater cap** and the suppression of mixing. **Lesson 11** supplies **Ekman divergence** and the
Somali and south-west-coast cells. **Lesson 12** supplies the **three-mechanism separation** and the
finding that India has ① abundantly, ③ seasonally and ② essentially not at all. **Lesson 17** supplies
the **seasonal reversal** that makes ③ a pulse. **Lesson 19** supplies **marine heatwaves and
stratification** as physical changes. **Route-outs, by exact path:** fisheries economics, catch and
Blue Transformation → `Economy/basic/30`; primary producers and food webs → `Environment-and-Ecology/
basic/01`; dead zones as ecology → `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24`; the monsoon's rainfall response
→ `Geography/advanced/16`. **Topic 12 answers where the fish are and why, and stops.**

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Authored practice. **Every model respects the caps: 10-mark models at most 150 words; 15- and 20-mark
models at most 250 words.** These are additional to the twenty lesson-local questions, and no rubric
below repeats a lesson rubric.

### P-1 (10 marks, 150 words) — *Comment on* the proposition that the ocean's inventory is an instrument rather than a fact.

**Model:** Oceans are enumerated by instruments, and instruments have dates. 📄 IHO Assembly-3
**Proposal** PRO-3.3 records that the Southern Ocean "**became an official ocean in 1937, but lost its
official status in 1953**", and proposes recognising it with a northern limit at **60°S** expressly
superseding **S-23 Ed.3, 1953** for the southern limits of three oceans at once. Appendix 1 records
**Argentina** objecting on technical grounds, the **United Kingdom** withdrawing **55°S**, **Chile**
and **Ecuador** accepting, and **CCAMLR** using the moving **Antarctic Convergence** instead. ✅ Majid
IWG lists **five** oceans; ✅ Khullar's Antarctic-coast formulation leaves room for four. Meanwhile
📄 MoES used "**Southern Ocean swells**" as settled language in July 2026. ⚠️ The proposal's adoption
was not verified here. An inventory that can be created, revoked and contested is an instrument.
*(123 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Instrument-versus-fact framing **3** · the 1937 and 1953 record **3** ·
national positions named **2** · adoption status flagged **2**

### P-2 (15 marks, 250 words) — *Analyse* why salinity, temperature and density must be taught in that order.

**Model:** Because **density is a joint function of the other two**, and a joint function cannot be
derived before its arguments exist. ✅ Majid IWG, printed 4.5, states the dependence: "the density of
water is mainly a function of its **salinity and temperature**."

**Temperature first**, because it establishes the vertical structure within which everything else
sits: ✅ Majid, printed 4.6-4.7, gives a **mixed zone** of about **100 m** holding **2 per cent** of
volume, a **thermocline** from **100 to 1000 m** holding **18 per cent**, and a **deep zone** holding
**80 per cent** at about **1-2°C** — a split closing exactly at **2 + 18 + 80 = 100**, and
independently corroborated by ✅ Leong's "over **80 per cent** … between **35° and 40°F**".

**Salinity second**, because its latitudinal pattern is governed by an independent budget: ✅ Leong,
printed 87, locates maxima at "**between 20° and 30°N. and S.**" under "high temperature **and low
humidity**", and a minimum at the equator under "**heavy daily rainfall**". ✗ Majid's claim of a
"direct relationship between temperature and salinity" at printed 4.7 is refuted by his own printed
4.8.

**Density third**, because only then can a **water mass** be defined as a T-S signature, a
**pycnocline** as the resultant of thermocline and halocline, and ✅ Leong's Mediterranean outflow be
understood as **warm yet dense**. Reverse the order and density becomes a word rather than a
derivation. *(221 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Dependency argument stated **4** · thermal structure with closure **4** ·
salinity budget with its correction **4** · density and water-mass payoff **3**

### P-3 (20 marks, 250 words) — *Critically examine* the claim that ocean currents govern coastal climates.

**Model:** The claim is substantially true and routinely over-extended.

**True**, because the limbs are thermally opposite. ✅ Majid IWG, Table 4.2, records the **North
Atlantic Drift** keeping "the ports of Norwegian Sea and Barents Sea like **Murmansk** … **ice-free
throughout the year**", while ✅ Leong, printed 87, records the **Labrador Current** leaving eastern
Canadian ports **ice-bound at 45°N**. On the cold limbs the mechanism is an **inversion**: ✅ Majid's
sixth temperature determinant notes that cold-current coasts "record **more fog, but less
precipitation**", and ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93, finds the Humboldt leaves the Chilean and Peruvian
coasts "**practically rainless**".

**Over-extended**, in two documented ways. ✗ Majid attributes the "**Namibian and Kalahari**" deserts
to the Benguela; the repository's own map bank records at line 271 that the **Namib** is the
cold-current coastal desert and that "**Kalahari is inland**". ✗ He likewise credits the Canaries with
"the genesis of **Sahara** Desert", converting a coastal fog strip into the cause of a continental
desert produced by subtropical subsidence.

📗 The qualification is the owner's own: currents are "**one of several controls; continentality,
relief and prevailing wind direction operate simultaneously**". 🇮🇳 India is the controlled case — with
**no persistent cold eastern-boundary current**, because its northern basin has no closed gyre, it has
**no cold-current coastal desert at all**. A current governs a **coastal strip**; it does not govern a
**region**. *(216 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Warm-limb and cold-limb mechanisms **6** · named evidence with attribution
**5** · the two over-extensions corrected **5** · the owner's limitation clause **2** · India's
controlled case **2**

### P-4 (10 marks, 150 words) — *Explain* why an anomaly requires a base period.

**Model:** An anomaly is a **departure from a stated normal**, so changing the normal changes the
anomaly even when the ocean does not change. 📄 The IMD ENSO and IOD Bulletin of **September 2026**
states its convention: SSTs from **ERSSTv5 (NCEP-NOAA)**, with anomalies "computed with respect to
**30-year (1991-2020) long term mean**", subsurface fields from **INCOIS-GODAS** referenced to the
**20°C isotherm**, and forecasts from **MMCFS** — **AGCM T382L64, ~38 km**; **OGCM ~25 km**; **42**
members; bias-corrected on **1999-2008** hindcasts. Each choice is a decision. Measure the same water
against a different thirty years and the published anomaly moves. The same logic governs a **marine
heatwave**, defined 📗 "relative to its own **normal seasonal range**", and the **DMI**, whose
**±0.4** threshold is meaningless without a stated climatology. State the base, then the number.
*(128 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Departure-from-normal definition **3** · the published conventions named **4**
· the marine-heatwave and DMI parallels **2** · the "state the base first" rule **1**

### P-5 (15 marks, 250 words) — *Discuss* how Topic 12's boundaries with four other owners should be drawn.

**Model:** Four boundaries, each with a stated stopping point.

**With Topic 13, Weather Elements.** Topic 12 uses **Coriolis as a deflection rule** controlling
current direction, and **sea-surface temperature as an ocean property**. It stops at the Coriolis
**force** as a phenomenon — **2024 Prelims Q14** is Topic 13's — at the land-sea thermal contrast, at
isotherm cartography, and at **cyclogenesis from SST**, which is **2024 GS-I Q4** and also Topic 13's.

**With Topic 16, the Indian monsoon.** Topic 12 teaches the **coupled mechanism**: the Walker cell,
the Bjerknes feedback, the DMI as a west-minus-east contrast, and the fact that each index shifts a
**probability**. It stops at the monsoon's **rainfall response** — no LPA figure, no seasonal outlook,
no onset or withdrawal. The IMD seasonal release of 30-09-2026 was retrieved during the audit and
**deliberately not used**.

**With Environment.** Topic 12 teaches **nutrient supply as a physical process** and the **physical**
basis of low oxygen — warming's effect on gas solubility, stratification's effect on ventilation. It
stops at food webs, denitrification, N₂O, acidification chemistry, blue carbon and restoration.
**2018 GS-I Q7** on dead zones is Environment's.

**With Economy.** Topic 12 owns **where the fish are and why the ocean puts them there**. It stops at
catch, fleet, aquaculture, livelihood and trade; **2026 Prelims Q38** is Economy's.

The test is uniform: **Topic 12 owns the ocean's physics and the geography of its measurement.**
*(227 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Four boundaries named with stopping points **6** · owned demands correctly
placed **4** · the deliberately-unused source disclosed **3** · uniform test stated **2**

### P-6 (20 marks, 250 words) — *Assess* the reliability of the standard textbook account of this topic.

**Model:** Reliable on structure, unreliable on quantity, and silent on the analytical core.

**Structure holds.** ✅ Leong's relief traverse, salinity controls and current circuits, and ✅ Majid's
seven-feature relief scheme, three thermal zones and tide block, are internally coherent, and several
of their arithmetics close exactly: **100 fathom = 600 ft**; **2 + 18 + 80 = 100**; **11.4 − 6.4 =
5.0**; **24 h 50 min ÷ 2 = 12 h 25 min**; **27 + 7 + 12 = 46**.

**Quantity fails repeatedly.** ✗ Majid's Table 4.1 sums to **3.32 per cent** against his stated
**3.5**; ✗ his ocean area and water-coverage share diverge by about **6 million km²** inside one
paragraph; ✗ Leong gives the Caspian at **180‰** and then as a class member "often over 37‰"
(**N-04**); ✗ his Dead Sea is **250‰** in text and **240‰** in his own exercise (**N-05**) — and
**U-03** records that none of these was verified, so **none is asserted**; ⇄ the Japan Trench differs
by about **2,000 m** between books.

**Positions fail where the world has moved.** ✗ Khullar's "United Nations … permission … to
**exploit** … **1,50,000 sq km**" is corrected on three counts by 📄 PIB 2159345 of **21 AUG 2025**,
which gives the **International Seabed Authority**, **exploration**, and **75,000 sq km**;
✗ Majid's present-tense tidal-production claim is corrected by 📄 PIB/MNRE of **08-March-2013**.

**And the core is absent.** **Upwelling, Ekman, Walker, IOD, MJO, PDO, thermohaline, AMOC,
acidification and OMZ return zero occurrences in all four books** — the very mechanisms graded highest
in answer value. *(231 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Structural reliability with closing arithmetic **5** · quantitative failures
with locations **6** · superseded positions corrected with dates **5** · the absent analytical core
**3** · balanced verdict **1**

### P-7 (15 marks, 250 words) — *Examine* the proposition that India's ocean is the best evidence for the primacy of wind.

**Model:** The proposition is strong because the North Indian Ocean approximates a **controlled
experiment**, which no other basin provides.

**The claim.** Wind stress dominates **surface** circulation; density dominates the **deep**.
📗 The Core owner's process clause gives "wind stress plus Coriolis, pressure gradient, basin shape and
friction" for surface flow and "heat and freshwater budgets" for density and mixing.

**The test.** Find a basin where the wind reverses on a short timescale while the density field does
not, and observe the current. The density field integrates heat and freshwater budgets over seasons to
centuries and **cannot reverse twice a year**; the monsoon wind does.

**The result.** ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93: "In the North Indian Ocean, there is a **complete reversal**
of the direction of currents between summer and winter, **due to the changes of monsoon winds**." **In
summer from June to October** the **South-West Monsoon Drift**; **in winter beginning from December**
the **North-East Monsoon Drift**. His conclusion is evidential: these currents "**demonstrate most
convincingly the dominant effects of winds**". ✅ Fig. 95 labels both drifts **with their seasons**.

**The control.** The **South** Indian Ocean, open at both ends of its latitude band, retains a regular
anti-clockwise gyre — so the reversal is not a property of "the Indian Ocean" but of its **closed
northern half**.

**The limit.** ↪ Why the wind reverses is `Geography/advanced/16`'s. We take the wind as given.
*(226 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Claim and its two-regime structure **4** · the controlled-test logic **5** ·
named evidence with seasons **4** · the southern-gyre control **2**

# REMEDIATION

Targeted repair paths for every failure mode named across the twenty lessons. Each entry states the
**symptom**, the **diagnosis**, the **repair**, and the **exact return path** into this edition.

## R·A — Errors of determinism

| # | Symptom | Diagnosis | Repair | Return to |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | Writing "El Niño causes drought in India" | A probabilistic teleconnection converted into a causal law | State the **tendency**, then name the **three offsets** — the Indian Ocean state, the longitude of the warming (Modoki), and intra-seasonal variability (MJO). 📗 The audit prohibits the deterministic form at line 345 | Lesson 18 |
| A2 | Writing "Positive IOD → stronger Indian monsoon" | C2's line 106, contradicted by **C2's own lines 96-97** | Use the hedged formulation: a positive IOD **can partly offset** an El Niño's drying tendency; a negative IOD **can compound** it; forecasting requires **both** indices | Lesson 19, ✗ N-C2-01 |
| A3 | Writing "a weakened AMOC will weaken the Indian monsoon" | 📗 C1's own lines 106-107 call the response "**model-dependent rather than a settled one-direction forecast**" | State the **mechanism** and route the **monsoon response** to `Geography/advanced/16` | Lesson 13, ✗ N-32 |
| A4 | Writing "upwelling means a fishery" | Ignoring the second condition | 📗 "Upwelling raises nutrient supply but **does not guarantee a fishery**." The wind sets the transport; the **stratification sets the payload** | Lessons 11, 12 |

## R·B — Errors of sign and physics

| # | Symptom | Diagnosis | Repair | Return to |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | Placing the salinity maximum at the equator | ✗ Majid printed 4.7's direct temperature-salinity claim | The control is **E − P**. Maxima at **20°-30°N and S** under the subtropical highs; the **equator is a minimum** under heavy daily rainfall | Lesson 6, ✗ N-02 |
| B2 | "Currents are deflected to the right" | ✗ Leong printed 88's hemisphere-blind sentence | **Right in the Northern Hemisphere, left in the Southern.** Northern gyres clockwise, southern anti-clockwise | Lesson 8, ✗ N-20 |
| B3 | "Salinity increases with depth" as a rule | ✗ Majid printed 4.8's intra-page contradiction | The **halocline's sign follows the surface freshwater budget**: it increases downward in a freshwater-capped basin and decreases downward in an evaporative one | Lesson 7, ✗ N-03 |
| B4 | "Dense means cold" | Ignoring the salinity term | Density is a **joint** function. ✅ Mediterranean outflow is **warm and dense** because salinity outweighs temperature | Lesson 7 |
| B5 | "Upwelling needs an offshore wind" | Confusing wind direction with transport direction | The wind blows **along** the coast; **Ekman transport** is at 90° to it and is what goes offshore | Lesson 11 |
| B6 | Expecting coastal-style upwelling at the equator | Applying a Coriolis-dependent mechanism where Coriolis vanishes | **Equatorial upwelling** works by divergence **across** the line, because deflection is opposite on either side | Lesson 11 |

## R·C — Errors of unit, base and arithmetic

| # | Symptom | Diagnosis | Repair | Return to |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| C1 | Writing "salinity 35%" | ✗ Majid prints a per-cent sign for per mille throughout | Salinity is **‰ / ppt / g/kg**, never %. Mark the substitution when quoting | Lesson 5, ✗ N-24 |
| C2 | Quoting Table 4.1 without its residual | The table sums to **3.32** against a stated **3.5** | Disclose the **0.18 per cent unaccounted** whenever the table is shown. Note that **2.6 ÷ 3.32 = 78.3 %** does agree with Leong's "more than 77 per cent" | Lesson 5, ✗ N-16 |
| C3 | Citing a per-decade warming rate as an MoES figure | Division performed by the writer presented as a citation | **1 ÷ 6.4 ≈ 0.156** and **0.7 ÷ 11.7 ≈ 0.060 °C/decade** are **derived**. No retrieved MoES document states a per-decade rate | Lessons 4, 20, ✗ N-35 |
| C4 | Differencing the three sea-level series | Three periods, three extents, three methods treated as one series | **They may not be differenced.** Say all three are positive and that the differences are **confounded** | Lesson 20, U-16 |
| C5 | Averaging 367 million km² and 70.8 per cent | Repairing a source contradiction | **Show the ≈6 million km² divergence**; note Leong's internally consistent "140 million square miles" | Lesson 1, ✗ N-15 |
| C6 | Quoting Leong's two current speeds unconverted | Two units, three paragraphs apart | Convert: **3 mph = 72 miles/day = 7.2×** the Drift's 10 miles/day. Label the conversion as derived | Lesson 9, ✗ N-21 |

|---|---|---|---|---|

| D2 | Attributing the monsoon drifts to Khullar or Majid | ✗ C2's grounding line overclaims | **Khullar has no oceanography chapter**; **Majid's Indian Ocean row has no monsoon drift**. The content is **Leong's**, scan PDF p.93 | Lesson 17, ✗ N-22, ✗ N-33 |
| D3 | Asserting the Alaska Current as warm or as cold | A sign conflict between two standard texts | Teach the **Bering-Kamchatka-Oyashio** limb as cold and the **Alaska Current** as warm, **and show both sources** | Lesson 10, ✗ N-06 |
| D4 | Equating the Irminger and East Greenland Currents | ✗ Leong printed 89 | **Different flows, opposite sides of the Denmark Strait.** Name separately or omit the Irminger | Lesson 9, ✗ N-07 |

| D8 | Claiming a local book teaches upwelling, ENSO or the IOD | The H1 finding ignored | **Zero occurrences across four books.** The mechanisms rest on 📗 C1's ⚠️ tier plus dated official sources, and this edition says so | Lessons 10, 11, 13, 18, 19 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

`GEOGRAPHY-MAP-LOCATION-BANK.md` was measured during the governing audit and **re-measured at
generation time, unchanged at SHA-256 `EE823ED7…`**: of **134** ocean assets demanded by the two
owners, the seven owned demands and the planned visuals, **35 are present and 99 are absent**. **Not
one named ocean current appears anywhere in the bank. Not one coupled mode. Not one tide anchor. Not
one observing platform. Not one ocean-floor relief term.** The bank supplies **basin containers**
and nothing that moves inside them. Two apparent hits — `IOD` and `RAMA` — proved to be **lexical
false positives inside "period" and "Ramganga"** and are excluded (U-22).

**The bank is not owned by this lane and has not been edited.** The gap is closed **inside this
edition**, through the seven self-contained map assets below.

---

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

**The final teaching section.** Compressed revision notes under **topic-specific headings derived from
the twenty lessons**, not a fixed template. Every lesson is covered. Definitions, measurement and
limit conventions, dated official series, causal chains, comparisons, conflicts, traps, PYQ routes
and answer frameworks are prioritised; the teaching introduction is **not** repeated. Positions 7 and
8 that follow are **apparatus, not teaching**.

---

## § A — CONVENTION BEFORE QUANTITY: THE RULE THAT GOVERNS THE TOPIC

- **Every quantity in this topic is a measurement passed through a declared convention.** The
  convention is the examinable part.
- **Four such conventions recur:** the **ocean's boundary** (§B), the **unit and base** of a
  composition (§E), the **threshold** of an index (§R), and the **climatology** an anomaly is
  measured against (§T).
- **Consequence:** a change in a critical water body cannot be reported without stating the
  convention, because a change in the convention is otherwise indistinguishable from a change in the
  ocean. This is why the geophysical-phenomena clause makes this topic a measurement topic.

## § B — THE CONTESTED SOUTHERN LIMIT AND THE OCEAN INVENTORY

- **Four live conventions for the Indian Ocean's southern limit:** **[A] the Antarctic coast**
  (✅ Khullar printed 21); **[B] 60°S** (📄 IHO Assembly-3 **Proposal** PRO-3.3); **[C] 55°S** (the UK,
  **withdrawn** — "does not apply anymore"); **[D] the Antarctic Convergence** (CCAMLR, "despite the
  seasonal and annual changes of its latitude" — **a moving line, so no fixed area**).
- 📄 The Southern Ocean "**became an official ocean in 1937, but lost its official status in 1953**".
  **An ocean can be de-recognised.**
- 📄 The proposed 60°S limit "**supersedes those mentioned in Publication S-23, Ed. 3, 1953**" for the
  southern limits of the **Atlantic, Indian and Pacific** simultaneously. **Argentina objects**
  ("no technical grounds"); **Chile and Ecuador accept**; the **USA supports "without prejudice"**.
- ⚠️ **U-01: adoption was NOT retrieved.** The word **proposal** is mandatory. Never write that the
  IHO *has* recognised the Southern Ocean.
- ⇄ **Inventory conflict:** ✅ Majid IWG lists **five** oceans including the Southern; ✅ Khullar's
  Antarctic-coast formulation implies **four**; 📄 MoES used "**Southern Ocean swells**" as settled
  operational language on **29-30 July 2026**.
- ✅ **Khullar printed 21:** area **≈73,442,700 sq km**, **third largest**, **the only ocean named
  after a country**; limits **147°E (Cape of Tasmania)** and **18°22′E (Cape Town)**; **27 littoral
  (incl. 7 island) + 12 landlocked = 46 ✓**.
- ✗ **N-14:** "Tropic of Cancer forms its northernmost limit" is a **defect**; the margin is the
  **Asian coast**. ⚠️ **U-02:** no official area under any other convention; **no difference computed**.
- ✗ **N-15:** ✅ Majid's "**367 million sq km**" against "**70.8 %**" diverges by ≈**6 million km²**
  inside one paragraph; ✅ Leong's "140 million square miles" (≈71.1 %) is internally consistent.
  **Show the divergence; do not average.**
- ✗ **N-36:** the **98/2** water split **cannot rank** rivers, groundwater and polar ice. **Ordering:
  ocean → ice → groundwater → surface fresh water, with rivers among the smallest stores.** Rank by
  **stock** and ice leads; by **flow** and rivers lead.

## § C — THE SHELF AS THE PLANET'S ECONOMIC FRINGE

- **Sequence:** shelf → slope → rise → abyssal plain → ridge and seamounts → trench.
- **Shelf edge: two conventions for one gradient break** — **100 fathom (600 ft) ≈ 183 m** (✅ Leong
  printed 85) and **200 m** "conveniently used" (✅ Majid 4.2).
- ✅ Shelf gradient **1 in 500**; slope **1 in 20**, "an abrupt change". Width **32-161 km** usual,
  max **≈1,207 km (750 mi)** off Arctic Siberia, **mean ≈70 km**.
- ✅ Shares (Majid 4.2): shelf **≈5 %** of the Earth's surface; shelf + slope **≈11.4 %**, slope
  **≈6.4 %**, rise **≈3.8 %**. **11.4 − 6.4 = 5.0 ✓**.
- ⇄ **N-19:** abyssal extent — "two-thirds of the **ocean** floor" (✅ Leong 85) against "80-85 % of
  the **Pacific** floor" (✅ Majid 4.4). **Different denominators; U-27 neither verified; assert
  neither.**
- ⇄ **N-18:** **Japan Trench 10,554 m** ("Ramapo Deep", ✅ Majid 4.3) against **28,000 ft ≈ 8,534 m**
  (✅ Leong) — **≈2,000 m conflict**.
- ✗ **N-17:** Everest **8,848 m / 29,028 ft** and the Mariana pair are **book-era**; no modern value
  retrieved. **Challenger Deep** named, not quantified.
- ✅ Ridges **≈22.1 %** of the ocean surface, **≈66,000 km**, **>1,400 km** wide, peaks **≈3,000 m**;
  **Mid-Atlantic Ridge ≈14,450 km**. Hypsographic means **land +840 m**, **ocean −3,790 m**.
- ✅ **Seamount** >1 km with a 20-25° slope; **guyot** flat-topped by **wave planation then
  subsidence**, mostly west-central Pacific; **black smokers** → **polymetallic sulphides** (§Q).
- ✅ **Leong's four conversions all close:** 100 fathom = 600 ft; 5,000 fathom = 30,000 ft; 2,000
  fathom = 12,000 ft; 36,000 > 29,028.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ Khullar printed 23: India has **3.2 lakh sq km offshore sedimentary shelf to 200 m** — the
  **Kachchh Shelf, Gulf of Khambhat, Mumbai High**, and the **Krishna-Godavari** gas basin.
  ⚠️ **U-30:** "half of the world's total output of oil and gas … offshore" is **undated; not
  reproduced**.

## § D — DEPOSITS: TWO TAXONOMIES THAT CUT ACROSS EACH OTHER

- **Three sorting filters:** **distance from a terrigenous source** · **surface production** ·
  **dissolution with depth**.
- ✅ **Leong printed 86, descriptive, three classes:** **muds** (terrigenous, shelves, blue/green/red)
  · **oozes** (pelagic, calcareous or siliceous micro-organism remains) · **clays** (red clay in the
  deepest basins, "particularly abundant in the **Pacific Ocean**", attributed to **volcanic dust**).
- ✅ **Majid 4.8-4.9, provenance, four classes:** **terrigenous** (six transport agents) ·
  **volcanic** · **biotic** (neretic, pelagic ooze, **Globigerina**, **Radiolarian**, red clay) ·
  **cosmogenous** ("dust, **tektites** and particles", **absent from Leong entirely**).
- ⇄ **The divergence:** **red clay** is a class for Leong, a **biotic** member for Majid, and
  **volcanic** in origin on Leong's own account. **Do not harmonise.**
- ⚠️ **Calcareous remains dissolve during the fall** in cold, high-pressure deep water; siliceous
  remains and insoluble dust survive. **Leong states the result; no book states the mechanism.**
- 🇮🇳 The **Bay of Bengal** floor is **terrigenous** (Ganga-Brahmaputra); the **Central Indian Ocean
  Basin** is **pelagic and slowly accumulating** — **which is why nodules are viable there** (§Q).
- ✅ Khullar printed 22 (book-era): **seafloor phosphorite** on margins and upper slopes at **<500 m**,
  **40°N-40°S**, first found on the **Agulhas Bank**, reserves **≈450 million tonnes**.

## § E — THE SALINITY BUDGET AND THE SIGN TRAP

- ✅ **Definition (Majid 4.7):** "a measure of **dissolved solids in seawater**, usually expressed in
  **grams per kilogram or parts per thousand by weight**"; standard sea water **35[‰] at 0°C**;
  **96.5 % water, 3.5 % dissolved salts**.
- ✅ Average **35‰** (Majid) / **35.2‰** (Leong printed 86). **Isohaline** = line of equal salinity.
- ✅ Table 4.1: NaCl **2.6** · MgCl₂ **0.3** · MgSO₄ **0.2** · CaSO₄ **0.1** · KCl **0.1** · KBr
  **0.01** · other **0.01**. ✗ **N-16: the table sums to 3.32 against a stated 3.5 — 0.18 per cent
  unaccounted. Always disclose.**
- ✓ **Cross-check that closes:** 2.6 ÷ 3.32 = **78.3 %**, agreeing with ✅ Leong's NaCl "**more than
  77 per cent**". **A table can be internally defective and still carry a sound ratio.**
- ✗ **N-24: salinity is ‰ / ppt / g/kg and NEVER %.** ✅ Majid prints a per-cent sign throughout; the
  substitution is marked when quoting.
- ✅ **Three salt pathways:** land runoff (rain, running water, ground water, wind, sea-waves,
  glaciers) · the **deeper layers of the Earth**, volcanic and hydrothermal · decomposed organic life.
  ⚠️ **Removal balances supply; salinity is a steady state, not an accumulation.**
- ✅ **Classes (Majid 4.8):** above normal — **Red Sea ≈39[‰], Persian Gulf 38, Mediterranean 37**;
  normal — Caribbean, Gulf of Mexico, Gulf of California, Yellow Sea at **35-36**; below normal —
  Arctic, Antarctic, Bering, Japan, **Baltic 21[‰]**.
- ⇄ **Baltic: 21[‰]** (Majid) against **7‰** (Leong, twice; C1 line 34 follows Leong). **Neither
  asserted.**
- ✗ **N-04:** Caspian **180‰** (Leong 86) against "often over 37‰" as a class member (Leong 87).
  ✗ **N-05:** Dead Sea **250‰** (text) against **240‰** (Leong's own exercise). **U-03: unverified;
  assert neither.** Lake Van **330‰** likewise attributed and flagged.
- ↪ **Inland saline bodies — Caspian, Dead Sea, Lake Van, Aral, Sambhar, Didwana, Great Salt Lake,
  Rann of Kachchh — are `Geography/basic/09_Lakes`.** "Salinity" in Topic 12 means **ocean** salinity.

## § F — WHY THE SALTIEST SEA IS NOT THE HOTTEST

- **The control is EVAPORATION MINUS PRECIPITATION (E − P)**, modified by **runoff** and by **freedom
  of mixing**. **Temperature enters only through the evaporation term.**
- ✅ **Leong's three controls (printed 87):** rate of evaporation · fresh water added by precipitation,
  streams and icebergs · degree of mixing by currents.
- ✅ **The maximum is SUBTROPICAL:** "the waters fringing the **High Pressure Belts of the Trade Wind
  Deserts, between 20° and 30°N. and S.**", because of "high temperature **and low humidity**".
- ✅ **The equator is a MINIMUM:** below 35‰ from "**heavy daily rainfall and high relative
  humidity**". ✅ **High latitudes are minima:** Baltic, Arctic and Antarctic "**less than 32‰**", fed
  by the **Ob, Lena, Yenisey, Mackenzie**.
- ✗ **N-02 — the physical-sign error.** ✅ Majid printed 4.7 asserts a "**direct relationship between
  temperature and salinity … The Torrid Zone has higher salinity as compared to the Frigid Zone**",
  and **contradicts himself at printed 4.8** ("salinity is low at the surface water in the equatorial
  water"). **The claim is right at the end-points and wrong in the middle, which is why it survives.
  Never asserted.**
- ✅ Majid's five determinants are otherwise sound; highest evaporation **along the Tropic of Cancer**,
  especially the **Red Sea and Persian Gulf**; the **Gulf Stream and North Atlantic Drift raise North
  Sea salinity**; low salinity at the **Amazon, Congo and Ganga deltas**.
- 📘 **O7, 2024 Prelims Q89 — the Red Sea, four terms pushing together:** high **E** (desert on both
  shores; ✅ Leong gives Red Sea water at **85-100°F ≈ 29-38°C**) · negligible **P** (subtropical
  subsidence) · nil **R** (no perennial river) · restricted **M** (**Bab-el-Mandeb**). ✅ Leong printed
  86: "**In the Red Sea where there is much surface evaporation and fewer rivers to bring in fresh
  water, the average salinity increases to 39‰.**" ⚠️ **U-24: a Set-A key is recorded as locally
  available; no answer is recorded anywhere.**
- **Enclosed subtropical ordering:** Mediterranean **37** < Persian Gulf **38** < Red Sea **≈39‰** —
  explained by **enclosure, aridity and river inflow**.

## § G — DENSITY, WATER MASSES AND THE LAYERED OCEAN

- ✅ **Density is a joint function of salinity and temperature** (Majid 4.5). **Dense does not mean
  cold.**
- **A current is defined by MOTION; a water mass by its TEMPERATURE-SALINITY SIGNATURE.** A current is
  **driven**; a water mass is **formed at the surface**, sinks to neutral buoyancy, and **spreads
  carrying its label**. A velocity reveals **forcing**; a T-S pair reveals **history**.
- ✅ **Thermocline** (temperature) · **halocline** (salinity) · **pycnocline** (density, the actual
  barrier) — Majid 4.8, Fig. 4.8; the **T-S diagram** "advocated by **Helland-Hensen**".
- ✗ **N-03:** Majid printed 4.8 writes "no definite trend … no generalisation can be made" and then
  "(i) the salinity of the oceans increases with increasing depth". **The defensible rule: the
  halocline's SIGN follows the SIGN of the surface freshwater budget.**
- 📘 **O1's only evidence — ✅ Leong printed 88:** "**The less saline water of the Atlantic flows on
  the surface into the Mediterranean, and this is compensated for by an outflow of denser bottom
  water from the Mediterranean.**" **One sill, Gibraltar, yields a CURRENT inward and a WATER MASS
  outward** — the outflow **warm yet dense**, because salinity outweighs temperature.
- 🇮🇳 The **Bay of Bengal's freshwater cap**: thermocline and halocline **reinforce**, the pycnocline
  is strong, mixing is suppressed, the thin surface layer heats efficiently, and **upwelling is less
  effective even under wind forcing**. ⚠️ **U-31: no value for either Indian basin anywhere.**
- ⚠️ **OMZ physics, three terms:** supply from above cut off by stratification · high oxygen demand at
  depth from sinking organic matter · weak ventilation. **Warming adds lower gas solubility and
  stronger stratification.** ↪ **All OMZ ecology — denitrification, anammox, N₂O, *Noctiluca*, dead
  zones, 2018 GS-I Q7 — is `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24`.**

## § H — THE FORCES, AND WHY THREE AUTHORITIES GIVE THREE LISTS

- 📗 **The best formulation (C1 lines 394-397):** **wind stress + Coriolis, pressure gradient, basin
  shape and friction** organise surface flow; **heat and freshwater budgets** change density and
  mixing; **coastal wind-Ekman divergence** drives upwelling.
- ✅ **Leong's five:** planetary winds · temperatures · salinity · the earth's rotation · land.
- ✅ **Majid's five (printed 4.9):** differential heating of the equatorial and polar oceans · trade and
  anti-trade winds · Coriolis effect · variations in salinity and temperature · configuration of the
  coast line and ocean floor. ✗ **N-28:** mis-numbered **1, 2, (unnumbered), 4, 4**.
- 📗 **C1's four:** winds · Coriolis · salinity and temperature variations · coastline and ocean-floor
  configuration.
- ✗ **N-01 — the divergence, which is the answer:** C1 **merges** Leong's items 2 and 3 and **drops
  Majid's differential heating** (the only term naming the thermal **engine**); **Leong omits
  ocean-floor configuration**; **Majid duplicates** the thermal term. **Present none as "the" list.**
- ✗ **N-20:** deflection is **right in the N, left in the S**. Leong's unqualified "to the right" is
  not reproduced. **Northern gyres clockwise; southern anti-clockwise.**
- 🇮🇳 **The wind-primacy demonstration:** ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.93 — the North Indian Ocean's **complete
  reversal** "demonstrate[s] **most convincingly the dominant effects of winds**". **The wind reverses
  twice a year; the density field does not.**
- ↪ **The Coriolis FORCE as a phenomenon (2024 Prelims Q14) is `Geography/basic/13`.** We use the
  deflection rule only.

## § J — THE CIRCUITS, AND WHAT A CURRENT DOES TO A COAST

- **The universal asymmetry: in BOTH hemispheres the WESTERN limb is warm and poleward and the
  EASTERN limb cold and equatorward.** Reverse the gyre sense; keep the asymmetry.
- **North Atlantic:** North Equatorial → Cayenne → Florida → **Gulf Stream** → **North Atlantic
  Drift** → Irminger / East Greenland → **Labrador** → **Canaries**, enclosing the **Sargasso Sea**.
  **South Atlantic:** South Equatorial → **Brazilian** → South Atlantic / **West Wind Drift** →
  **Benguela**, with the **Falkland** and the **Equatorial Counter Current**.
- ✅ **Gulf Stream "35 to 100 miles wide, 2,000 feet deep, three miles an hour"**, deflected at **Cape
  Hatteras, 35°N**; **North Atlantic Drift "10 miles per day"**. ✗ **N-21: 3 mph = 72 miles/day =
  7.2×** — **conversion derived here; both figures Leong's; neither corrected.**
- ✅ **Cape Sao Roque** splits the South Equatorial into **Cayenne** and **Brazilian**; ✅ **southern
  Chile** diverts the West Wind Drift into the **Peruvian**. **Two worked cases of basin geometry.**
- **North Pacific:** North Equatorial → **Kuroshio** ("Kuro Siwo or Japan Current") → **North Pacific
  Drift** → **Alaska / Bering-Okhotsk-Oyashio** → **Californian**. **South Pacific:** South Equatorial
  → **East Australian** → **South Pacific Current** → **Humboldt or Peruvian**.
- **The West Wind Drift / Antarctic Circumpolar Current** is unobstructed, **closes on itself**, and
  **feeds the Benguela, the Peruvian and the West Australian** — **one current, three cold limbs**.
- ⇄ **N-06, the Alaska Current:** "**cold** Bering Current **or Alaskan Current**" (✅ Leong PDF 93)
  against "**Alaska Current — Warm**" (✅ Majid 4.11). **Taught structure:
  Bering-Kamchatka-Oyashio = cold limb; Alaska Current = warm limb — with the conflict shown.**
- ⇄ **N-34:** "**Agulhas or Mozambique**" as one feature (Leong Fig. 95) against **two warm rows**
  (Majid). **Drawn separately.** ✗ **N-33:** Majid's Indian Ocean row — Mozambique, Agulhas, West
  Australian — is **not a complete inventory**.
- ✗ **N-07:** Irminger ≠ East Greenland (opposite sides of the Denmark Strait).
- **Warm limb:** warmed from below → unstable, moist, mild, **ice-free ports** (✅ Majid: the Drift
  keeps **Murmansk** open; **Severodvinsk is not reproduced**). **Cold limb:** cooled from below →
  **inversion** → **fog, not rain** → a **narrow** arid strip (✅ Leong: the Humboldt leaves the coasts
  "**practically rainless**").
- ✗ **N-08:** the **Namib** is the cold-current coastal desert; "**Kalahari is inland**" (map bank line
  271, honoured and not edited). ✗ The **Canaries-Sahara genesis** claim is not reproduced.
  ✗ **N-30:** "Sonara" → **Sonoran**.
- 📗 **The limitation clause:** currents act alongside **continentality, relief and prevailing wind
  direction**. **A current explains a strip, not a continent.**
- 🇮🇳 **India has no cold eastern-boundary current and therefore no cold-current coastal desert.** The
  absence is **structural** (§N).

## § K — UPWELLING: A VERTICAL PUMP DRIVEN BY A HORIZONTAL WIND

- **Wind blows ALONG the coast → Ekman transport at 90° (RIGHT in the N, LEFT in the S) → where that
  is OFFSHORE, a coastal DIVERGENCE forms → deeper, colder, NUTRIENT-RICH water rises → bloom.**
- **The Ekman spiral:** each layer drags the next, turning further and weakening; **friction** makes
  it decay.
- **The nutrient inversion:** the **photic layer has light and no nutrients**; **below the nutricline
  there are nutrients and no light**. Every great fishery is a mechanism for **bringing the two
  together**.
- 📗 **Four settings:** eastern-boundary coastal · **equatorial divergence** (works **without a
  coast**, because deflection is opposite across the line; coastal Ekman reasoning **fails at the
  equator**) · **monsoonal, seasonal** · **failure**.
- 📗 **The failure case:** during a warm ENSO phase a **deepened thermocline** means the upwelled water
  is **warm and nutrient-poor**, "so the fishery collapses **even though the wind may still blow**".
- 📗 **The binding causal control: "upwelling raises nutrient supply but does not guarantee a
  fishery."** The wind sets the **transport**; the stratification sets the **payload**.
- **H1:** **"upwelling" and "Ekman" return zero occurrences across all four books.** ✅ Leong PDF 93
  describes the Peruvian fishery — plankton, fish, sea-birds, **guano** — **without naming the
  mechanism**.
- 🇮🇳 **Somali** and **south-west Indian coast** cells, SW monsoon only, so productivity is "**strongly
  seasonal rather than year-round**". 📄 **CA-2: 19 subsurface moorings (16 mainland EEZ + 3
  equatorial Indian Ocean) for "ocean currents and zooplankton biomass"; 16 + 3 = 19 ✓.**
  ⚠️ **U-08: RAMA is in neither answer; no count.**

## § L — THREE FISHING MECHANISMS, NOT ONE

| | ① **SHELF** | ② **CONFLUENCE** | ③ **UPWELLING** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Driver | **Basin geometry** | **Circulation geometry** | **Wind + rotation** |
| Mechanism | **Light reaches a shallow floor** | **Mixing at a front** | **Ekman divergence lifts nutrients** |
| Named grounds | ✅ **Grand Banks, North Sea, Sunda Shelf** (Leong 85) | ✅ **Gulf Stream–Labrador** off Newfoundland; **Oyashio–Kuroshio** off Hokkaido (Majid 4.2) | ✅ **Peru/Humboldt** (Leong PDF 93) |
| Stop the wind | **unaffected** | little immediate effect | **stops** |
| Deepen the thermocline | **unaffected** | weakened | **collapses** |
| 🇮🇳 India | **abundant** — 3.2 lakh sq km of shelf | **essentially absent** — no closed gyre, no persistent cold limb | **seasonal** — Somali and SW coast |

- **The over-claim to refuse: "ocean currents create fishing grounds."** ① is a **depth-and-light**
  effect and supplies **three of the four named grounds** in the base.
- **The fog and the fishery share a cause; the fog does not produce the fishery.**
- ⚠️ 📗 C1's own evidence unit names **two** mechanisms and omits the **shelf**. **Corrected to three.**
- ↪ **Fisheries economics, aquaculture, Blue Transformation (2026 Prelims Q38) → `Economy/basic/30`.**
  ↪ **Dead zones as ecology (2018 GS-I Q7) → `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24`.** ↪ **Primary
  producers (2021 Prelims Q22, 2025 Prelims Q40) → `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01`.**

## § M — THE SLOW LOOP AND THE LIMITS OF WHAT MAY BE CLAIMED

- **Four stages:** warm surface limb poleward → **cooling + evaporation raise density** → **sinking**
  → **deep return limb equatorward over centuries** → diffuse upward mixing.
- ✅ **Leong printed 88 — the only book evidence in the base:** "almost **two-thirds** of the water
  brought by the Gulf Stream to the Arctic regions is **returned annually** to the tropical latitudes
  by **dense, cold polar water that creeps southwards in the ocean depths**." ✅ His factor 2 gives the
  physics without the name.
- **H1:** **"thermohaline" and "AMOC" return zero occurrences in all four books.**
- **The sinking site is the only switch.** Density-raising: **cooling, evaporation**. Density-lowering:
  **freshwater addition, surface warming** — **both act the same way**. 📗 C1 line 104 hedges
  correctly: freshening acts "**alongside wind and heat-flux changes**".
- **Asymmetry:** surface limb **wind-driven and fast** (a season); deep limb **density-driven and
  slow** (centuries). **The surface reverses seasonally; this does not reverse at all.**
- **The loop cannot be observed directly**; it is identified through **water-mass T-S signatures** —
  which is why §G precedes it.
- ✗ **N-32:** 📗 C1's AMOC anchor is **undated**. **No bare claim, no tipping year, no percentage
  weakening, no rate.** 📗 C1's own lines 106-107 govern over its line 113: the Indian-monsoon response
  is "**model-dependent rather than a settled one-direction forecast**".
- 🇮🇳 **The Indian Ocean has no northern high-latitude source region**, being closed in the north, so
  its deep and intermediate water **arrives from the south** — a **basin-geometry** statement, and the
  background to the Arabian Sea's poor ventilation (§G).
- ↪ **IPCC content, tipping-point assessment, sea-level-as-climate (2023 GS-III Q18, 2025 GS-I Q4) →
  `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/18` and `/17`.** ↪ **Monsoon response → `Geography/advanced/16`.**

## § N — TIDES: ASTRONOMY FOR THE RHYTHM, GEOMETRY FOR THE AMPLITUDE

- ✅ **Definition (Majid 4.12):** "the **periodic short-term rise and fall in the sea levels** …
  produced due to **gravitational interaction of Earth, Moon and Sun**. **Moon exerts the strongest
  influence** because of its closeness."
- ⚠️ The force is a **DIFFERENCE** in pull across the Earth, hence **two bulges** — near side **and far
  side** — hence **two highs and two lows** per rotation. **No local book explains the far-side bulge.**
- ✅ **Spring tides** at **new and full Moon** (aligned → **maximum range**); **neap tides** at the
  **first and third quarters** (right angles → **minimum range**). 📗 **"Spring" means the tide springs
  up, not the season.**
- ✅ **The interval closes exactly:** **24 h 50 min** for the Earth to return the same meridian below
  the Moon; hence tides at **12 h 25 min**; twice a day. **1490 ÷ 2 = 745 min ✓.**
- ✅ **World maximum: the Bay of Fundy** (✗ **N-29** corrects "Bay of Funday").
- ✗ **N-23: Leong's Chapter 12 contains NO TIDE THEORY.** All spring-neap material is **Majid's
  alone**; C1's grounding line is corrected.
- ⚠️ **Range = astronomical forcing × geometry**, through **shoaling**, **funnelling** and
  **resonance**. ✅ Leong printed 85: the shelf's "limited depth and gentle slope … **increase the
  height of tides**", so "ships can only **enter and leave port on the tide**" — **Southampton,
  London, Hamburg, Rotterdam, Hong Kong, Singapore** are shelf ports.
- **A question of the form "why does X have a great tidal range" is answered by geometry, never by
  astronomy.** The Moon is the same everywhere.
- 🇮🇳 **The Gulf of Khambhat and the Gulf of Kachchh** combine a **wide shelf** with **landward
  narrowing** — both amplifiers acting together. ✗ **N-11 / U-04:** Majid's "highest tides in India …
  at **Okha, Gujarat**" is **attributed and unverified**; **no Indian tidal range is asserted
  anywhere**.
- 📗 **Terminology control:** a **current transports water**; a **wave mainly transmits energy**; a
  **tide** raises and lowers the whole column, and it is the **tidal current** — flood and ebb — that
  **scours**. ✗ Majid's "a ridge of water between two **depressions**" is garbled; a **crest** lies
  between two **troughs**.
- ↪ **Tsunami genesis, propagation, warning, 2025 GS-I Q7 → `Geography/basic/03` and
  `Disaster-Management/basic/06`. U-26: Topic 12 has NO GS-III disaster mandate.**

## § P — THE TIDAL RESOURCE LADDER AND ITS TERMINATION

- **Four distinct quantities: RANGE → POTENTIAL → SANCTION → PRODUCTION.** A claim at one rung does
  not license a claim at the next.
- 📄 **PIB / Ministry of New and Renewable Energy, 08-March-2013** (Lok Sabha reply) — **the only
  first-party tidal document retrieved, thirteen years old at generation**:
  - **Central Electricity Authority** assessed **potential**: **≈7000 MW Gulf of Cambay**, **≈1200 MW
    Gulf of Kutch**, **≈100 MW Gangetic Delta, Sunderbans**.
  - A **3.75 MW demonstration** tidal power plant at **Durgaduani Creek**, sanctioned to **WBREDA,
    Kolkata, Feb 2008**.
  - "**Government of West Bengal has decided not to continue with the project due to the very high
    project cost.**" **The ladder terminates at rung ③.**
- ✗ **N-09:** the book's present-tense production claim is wrong in **tense, magnitude and location**
  — potential is not production; 3.75 MW is not a fleet; the plant was in **West Bengal**, not Gujarat.
- ⚠️ **U-05: no current MNRE installed capacity was retrieved. No present capacity is asserted,
  including not asserting zero.** ✗ **N-10:** "followed by **Japan**" is **not reproduced**.
- ⚠️ **Potential ≠ range**: potential depends on range **and** impoundable area, so the CEA table is
  **not** an Indian tidal-range ranking.
- ✅ **Tide as enabler (Majid 4.12):** tides make rivers "**navigable for ocean going ships**" —
  **London on the Thames, Kolkata on the Hooghly** — and "**retard the formation of deltas**".
  🇮🇳 **The Kolkata case runs both ways**: the tidal regime made the port and constrains it.
- ↪ **Sagarmala, port-led development (2026 Prelims Q35) → `Economy/basic/18` and
  `Geography/basic/33`.** ↪ **CRZ, erosion, coastline length (2023 GS-I Q14) → `Geography/basic/10`
  and `advanced/10`.**

## § R — THE REVERSING OCEAN, AND THE OFFICIAL DEFINITION OF ITS INDEX

### R·1 — The reversal

- ✅ **Leong, scan PDF p.93, verbatim:** "a **complete reversal** of the direction of currents between
  summer and winter, **due to the changes of monsoon winds**." **In summer from June to October**, the
  **South-West Monsoon Drift**; **in winter beginning from December**, the **North-East Monsoon
  Drift**. These currents "**demonstrate most convincingly the dominant effects of winds**."
- ✅ **Fig. 95, printed folio 89**, labels **both drifts with their seasons**. 📗 **C1's scale/map
  control: every North Indian Ocean arrow carries a season.**
- ✅ **The South Indian Ocean is normal:** South Equatorial → **Agulhas / Mozambique** → **West Wind
  Drift** → **West Australian**, anti-clockwise. **The exception is confined to the north, and the
  confinement identifies the cause.**
- ✗ **N-22:** C2's §1-§2 content is **Leong's**, not Khullar's or Majid's. **Khullar has no
  oceanography chapter; Majid's Indian Ocean row has no monsoon drift.** C2 is **not edited**.
- **ONE CAUSE — the basin is closed in the north by Asia, so no gyre can close — FIVE CONSEQUENCES:**
  no fixed gyre · no persistent cold eastern-boundary current · **no cold-current coastal desert in
  India** · no persistent confluence fishery · **seasonal rather than year-round upwelling**.
- ✅ **C2 §3:** Arabian Sea **higher** salinity ("high evaporation, fewer large rivers"); Bay of Bengal
  **lower** ("diluted by Ganga-Brahmaputra system and heavy rainfall"); the Bay's "**freshwater cap
  affects stratification and cyclones**". ⚠️ **U-31: no number for either.**

### R·2 — ENSO and the Walker circulation

- **Describe the NEUTRAL state first.** ⚠️ Strong easterly **trades** → **warm pool** piled west with
  rising air and heavy rain → **upwelling** in the east with a **shallow thermocline**, subsidence and
  a dry coast → together, the **Walker circulation**.
- ⚠️ **El Niño:** trades **weaken or reverse** · warm pool **spreads east** · eastern thermocline
  **deepens** · Walker **weakens**, rising limb **shifts east** · rainfall **moves east** · the eastern
  **fishery collapses**. ⚠️ **La Niña:** the reverse, with upwelling **intensified**.
- 📗 **The Bjerknes feedback:** weaker trades → pool east → weaker contrast → weaker trades. **A
  positive feedback**, explaining growth **and** eventual reversal. 📗 ENSO is a **coupled
  ocean-and-atmosphere** phenomenon — "two faces of one system".
- 📄 **CA-1, ENSO limb:** **La Niña Sep 2025–Feb 2026 → ENSO-neutral Mar 2026 → El Niño threshold
  crossed May 2026 → ≈+2.08°C by Aug 2026**, *as of the IMD bulletin of September 2026, on August 2026
  data*. 📄 **Criterion: La Niña ≤ −0.5, Neutral <0.5 to >−0.5, El Niño ≥ 0.5.**
- 📗 **BINDING PROHIBITION:** "El Niño causes drought in India" is **explicitly prohibited**;
  **probabilistic framing is mandated**, naming **three offsets** — the **Indian Ocean state** (IOD),
  the **longitude of the warming** (**Modoki**, which is **not** a weak El Niño), and
  **intra-seasonal variability** (**MJO**).
- 📗 **The 15-mark spine:** thesis (probability, not determination) → **neutral state** → warm phase →
  Bjerknes feedback → transmission → the three offsets → consequences (**routed**) → a **graded**
  conclusion: the best available seasonal predictor, to be used as a **risk signal**.
- **H1:** "**Walker**" returns **zero** occurrences in four books; Majid mentions El Niño **once**, in
  Table 4.2's Peru cell; the only ENSO **section** in any book is **Topic 16's**.

### R·3 — The IOD, defined by a number

- 📄 **THE OFFICIAL NUMERICAL DEFINITION (IMD, September 2026): "Criteria used for Probabilistic DMI
  Forecast: negative DMI ≤ −0.4, Neutral <0.4 to >−0.4, positive DMI ≥ 0.4."** **Neither owner
  carries it.**
- 📄 **Against the ENSO criterion of ±0.5.** **Same number, opposite verdict**: −0.45 is a **negative
  IOD** and an **ENSO-neutral** Pacific.
- 📄 **The gradient-not-level insight:** in **August 2026**, "positive SST anomalies were observed over
  **both** the western and eastern Indian Ocean … with notably warmer SSTs prevailing over the
  **Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal**", **and** "At present, **neutral Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD)
  conditions prevail**." **The DMI is a DIFFERENCE (west minus east), not a LEVEL. A uniformly warm
  basin is IOD-neutral.**
- 📄 **Phase history: NEGATIVE (Sep-Dec 2025) → NEAR-NEUTRAL (Jan 2026) → POSITIVE (Feb-Apr 2026) →
  NEGATIVE (May-Jun 2026) → NEUTRAL (Jul-Aug 2026).** **Five labelled states in twelve months**,
  proving 📗 C1's control clause: "**a seasonal index is not a permanent basin label**".
  ⚠️ **U-09: only the phase LABELS; no DMI value except the published ±0.4.**
- ✗ **N-C2-01:** C2 line 106's "**Positive IOD → stronger Indian monsoon; Negative → weaker**" is
  contradicted by **C2's own lines 96-97**, by **C1 §9.3**, and by the **audit's line 345**. **The
  hedged formulation governs. C2 is not edited.** 📗 A positive IOD can **partly offset** an El Niño's
  drying tendency; a negative IOD can **compound** it — "which is why monsoon forecasting requires
  **both indices**, and why **single-index reasoning fails**".

### R·4 — The mode family and the ocean-change family

- 📗 **ORDER BY TIMESCALE, do not list: MJO (weeks) → IOD (seasonal to inter-annual) → ENSO
  (inter-annual) → Modoki (inter-annual, different longitude) → PDO (decadal).** "An answer that
  **arranges** the modes by timescale demonstrates command; one that **lists** them does not."
- 📗 **Marine heatwave:** "a prolonged period during which SST in a region is unusually high
  **relative to its own normal seasonal range**" — **a local baseline, not an absolute temperature**.
  Mechanism: warming background + weak winds and reduced mixing + persistent high pressure.
- 📗 **The five ocean changes:** warming and marine heatwaves · acidification · deoxygenation ·
  stratification · overturning change. **OURS: the physics. ROUTED: the ecology, the chemistry and
  the assessment.**
- 📗 **Factual caution:** no index value, no Niño anomaly, no named event strength, **no pH figure**,
  no catch tonnage, no projected weakening from memory.

## § S — THE SPINES: HOW THE TWO OWNED MAINS ANSWERS ARE BUILT DIFFERENTLY

| | **O1 — 2019 GS-I Q17** | **O2 — 2022 GS-I Q14** |
|---|---|---|
| Directive | ***How do they differ***, with examples | ***What are*** and ***Describe*** |
| Logical form | A **differentiation** | A **two-part** demand |
| Required shape | A **two-column discrimination held throughout**, converging on **one shared consequence** | An **enumeration**, then a **mechanism** |
| Core content | **Current = motion; water mass = T-S signature.** Driven vs formed; traced vs labelled; forcing vs history | Six forces + **the divergence between three standard lists**; then **three fishing mechanisms** |
| The single worked case | **Gibraltar** — one sill yields a current inward and a water mass outward | **Grand Banks / Hokkaido / Peru** — three grounds, three mechanisms |
| The closing move | **Shared consequence**: both govern where nutrients meet light | **Over-claim refusal**: the shelf is not a current effect |
| Length | **≤ 250 words** | **≤ 250 words** |
| **They must not share a paragraph skeleton.** One is a sustained contrast; the other is a list that becomes a sequence. | | |

## § T — MEASURING THE OCEAN: THE CONVENTIONS THAT GOVERN EVERY NUMBER

- 📄 **CA-2 (29-30 July 2026) — India's layered inventory:** **19 subsurface moorings (16 mainland EEZ
  + 3 equatorial Indian Ocean) ✓ 16+3=19**, for "ocean currents and **zooplankton biomass**" · **17
  broadband seismic stations, 7 deep-ocean tsunami buoys, 36 coastal tide gauges, 35 GNSS receivers**
  · **17 Directional Wave Rider Buoys, 20 ADCPs, 32 AWS, 2 Water Quality Buoys, 50 tide gauges** ·
  **302 Argo profiling floats (2014-2025), 12 glider missions, 45 drifting buoys, 64 wave drifters**,
  plus **VMPs, uCTD, Wire Walker** · **14 additional tide gauges and 15 co-located GNSS** · a
  **Directional Wave Rider Buoy off Mauritius** for "**Southern Ocean swells**".
- 📄 **Outcomes against baselines:** wave forecast accuracy **+about 43 % compared to 2014**; coastal
  forecast accuracy **about 70 %**; tsunami advisories within the **10-minute window**.
- 📄 **Assimilation chain: GTS → INCOIS → INCOIS-GODS → CFS; HYCOM → HWRF.**
- ⇄ ✗ **N-25: "36 coastal tide gauges" and "50 tide gauges" in the same answer, plus "14 additional".
  36 + 14 = 50 EXACTLY — and NEITHER DOCUMENT MAKES THE LINK.** Show the arithmetic; **assert neither
  total**.
- ⇄ ✗ **N-26: INCOIS-GODS (July 2026) against INCOIS-GODAS (September 2026).** One ministry, one
  system, two acronyms. **Both quoted; neither adopted.**
- 📄 **CA-6 (19 March 2025) — three sea-level series, kept apart:** **≈1.7 mm/yr (1900-2000, Indian
  Ocean)**; **≈3.3 mm/yr ("since 1993-2015", north Indian Ocean)**; **gridded satellite altimeter
  1993-2020 — Mumbai 4.59±0.19, Mormugao 4.30±0.17, Kochi 4.10±0.16, Chennai 4.31±0.26, Visakhapatnam
  4.27±0.33, Paradip 4.43±0.36 mm/yr**. ⚠️ **U-16: three periods, three extents, three methods. NOT A
  TREND SERIES; MAY NOT BE DIFFERENCED.** ⚠️ **U-17: "since 1993-2015" is quoted, not repaired.**
- 📄 **CA-5 — the warming pair:** India surface air **+≈0.7°C (1901-2018)**; tropical Indian Ocean SST
  **+≈1°C (1951-2015)**, both from "**Assessment of Climate Change over the Indian Region**".
  ✗ **N-35: ≈0.156 and ≈0.060 °C/decade are DERIVED BY DIVISION and are never cited as MoES figures.**
  The windows do **not** overlap, so the ≈2.6 ratio is **indicative only**.
- 📄 **CA-1's anomaly conventions:** **ERSSTv5 (NCEP-NOAA)**; base period **1991-2020**;
  **INCOIS-GODAS** subsurface relative to the **20°C isotherm**; **MMCFS** with **AGCM T382L64 ~38
  km**, **OGCM ~25 km**, **42 members**, hindcast bias correction **1999-2008**, initial conditions
  from **ESSO-INCOIS and ESSO-NCMRWF**. **Change the base period and the anomaly changes — the ocean
  does not.**
- ✗ **N-27: the bulletin's text layer loses its degree signs.** The correct values are **5°S-5°N**,
  **10°S-Equator** and the **20°C isotherm**; the stripped strings are never reproduced.
  ⚠️ **U-18:** the NDJ/DJF season labelling is **recorded, not resolved**. ⚠️ **U-32:** monthly
  product; re-fetch at generation time returned **HTTP 404** on all candidate URLs.
- ✅ **The 150-year arc (Leong printed 84):** the ***Challenger*** expedition **1873-1876** (⇄ **N-31**
  against Khullar's 1872-76) · **ICES, headquarters Copenhagen** · **echo sounding** · **deep-sea
  cores** · **current meters** using propellers, vanes or pendulums · **drift bottles**.
- **No single instrument sees the whole ocean.** Moorings resolve **time**, not space; altimeters
  **space**, not depth; Argo **depth**, not currents; tide gauges the **coast**, and they **move with
  the land** — hence the **co-located GNSS**. **The inventory is a design, not a list.**
- ⚠️ **U-07: no primary Ocean Mean Temperature anchor.** OMT is taught **conceptually only** —
  integrated upper-ocean heat to the depth of the **26°C isotherm**, distinct from **skin SST**. **No
  value, no skill figure, no correlation.** ⚠️ **U-08: no RAMA count.** The **INCOIS buoy page returned
  HTTP 404**.

## § U — THE EIGHT CONFLICTS AND THE SIX SELF-CONTRADICTIONS, AS A RECALL LIST

**Eight inter-source conflicts, both sides always shown:** ① the **ocean inventory** (five / four /
de-recognised-and-proposed) · ② the **Japan Trench** (10,554 m / 28,000 ft) · ③ the **abyssal extent**
(two-thirds of the ocean / 80-85 % of the Pacific) · ④ the ***Challenger* start year** (1873 / 1872) ·
⑤ the **Alaska Current's sign** (cold / warm) · ⑥ **Agulhas or Mozambique** (one feature / two rows) ·
⑦ the **forces taxonomies** (five / five / four) · ⑧ **36 against 50 tide gauges**.

**Six intra-source self-contradictions, both locations always shown and — under **N-04**, **N-05**
and **U-03** — none asserted:** ① **Leong on the Caspian** (180‰ / "often over 37‰") · ② **Leong on
the Dead Sea** (250‰ text / 240‰ exercise) · ③ **Majid on
temperature and salinity** (direct relation / low equatorial salinity) · ④ **Majid on vertical
salinity** ("no generalisation" / a generalisation) · ⑤ **Majid on ocean area** (367 million km² /
70.8 %) · ⑥ **C2 on the IOD-monsoon relation** (hedged at 96-97 / deterministic at 106).

**And the structural finding that frames them all (H1):** across the four local books, **upwelling,
Ekman, Walker, the IOD, the MJO, the PDO, thermohaline circulation, AMOC, acidification,
deoxygenation, the oxygen minimum zone and the marine heatwave return ZERO occurrences** — and those
are precisely the mechanisms graded highest in answer value. **The analytical core of this topic rests
on the controlling owner's own standard-knowledge tier plus dated official sources, and this edition
says so wherever it is true.**

**The geophysical clause's water-body limb, unpacked.** The ocean is the largest water body on the
planet and Topic 12 owns its **measured changes**: **warming** (§T), **sea-level change** (§T),
**circulation change** (§M), **stratification and deoxygenation** (§G, §R·4), and the **oscillating
coupled modes** that redistribute heat within it (§R). This correspondence is what justifies teaching
the IOD inside a Geography paper at all.
